A single global limit produces a specific, predictable failure. If it is 1,000 requests per second and one client is sending 100, the limit is never reached and is therefore useless; if that client is sending 10,000, the limit is reached and you have throttled every other tenant for one tenant's behaviour. A per-IP limit does nothing at all about a distributed attack, and it punishes an office behind a single NAT address for its router's habits.

The four problems are laid out side by side below. Read it as a checklist: for each row, name the key you will count on and the mechanism that implements it, and refuse to let a single mechanism answer two rows.

// FOUR DIFFERENT PROBLEMS, four different mechanisms

// 1. TRAFFIC SHAPING - fairness between tenants
//    "no single client may take more than 10% of capacity"
//    token bucket per API key, or a weighted fair queue

// 2. ABUSE PREVENTION - the same client hammering
//    "60 requests per minute per IP"
//    fixed or sliding window

// 3. COST CONTROL - expensive operations
//    a report that scans a million rows costs 200 times a health check
//    "10 report generations per day per account"
//    this is the one people forget, and it is the one that saves money

// 4. BACKPRESSURE - protecting YOURSELF when overloaded
//    "if the queue depth exceeds 1000, return 503 with Retry-After"
//    this is a load-shedding decision, not a per-client limit

// conflating them produces bad systems: a global 1000 req/s limit is
// useless against one client sending 10,000, and a per-IP limit does
// nothing about a distributed attack.

The algorithms

Fixed windows and the boundary burst

A fixed window is one counter and one expiry, which is why every framework ships it first. Increment a key named after the current minute, set a TTL of one minute, compare with the ceiling. Its defect is arithmetic rather than implementation: at 60 requests per minute, a client that sends 60 at 10:00:59.9 and 60 at 10:01:00.1 has sent 120 requests in 200 ms, and both windows are legal.

That burst is not theoretical. It is exactly what a client that pauses on a 429 and then resumes does. Fixed windows remain the right choice for coarse budgets where a boundary burst is harmless — a daily export allowance, a monthly per-account ceiling — and the wrong choice for anything that protects a shared resource against a single client.

// fixed window: O(1) memory, one key per window, one expiry
async function fixedWindow(key: string, limit: number, windowSec: number) {
  const bucket = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000 / windowSec);   // the boundary
  const k = `rl:${key}:${bucket}`;
  const n = await redis.incr(k);
  if (n === 1) await redis.expire(k, windowSec);   // set ONCE, on creation
  return { allowed: n <= limit, remaining: Math.max(0, limit - n) };
}

// setting the expire on every call is the classic bug: the window then
// slides forward with activity and never resets for an active client,
// so a client that keeps calling is eventually locked out permanently.
// INCR then EXPIRE is also two round trips. Use a Lua script or a
// pipeline when the extra RTT is on the critical path.

Sliding window counter: the production default

The sliding window counter removes the boundary burst while storing two integers instead of a timestamp per request. Keep the current window's count and the previous window's count, then estimate the rate by weighting the previous window by how much of it still overlaps a window that ends now. At 10:01:15 with a 60-second window, 75% of the previous minute still counts, so the estimate is current + 0.75 * previous.

It is approximate: it can under-count a client that front-loads a window and over-count one that sends evenly across a boundary. But it needs no cleanup job, no per-request storage, and it is exact in the two cases that matter — a client at a steady rate, and a client bursting. For an API, where the limit exists to shape behaviour rather than to enforce a contract, that is the right trade.

// sliding window counter: two integers, no boundaries, no cleanup
function slidingWindowCounter(
  key: string, limit: number, windowSec: number, nowMs = Date.now(),
) {
  const nowSec = nowMs / 1000;
  const current = Math.floor(nowSec / windowSec);
  const elapsed = (nowSec % windowSec) / windowSec;    // 0 at the boundary, 1 just before it

  const cur = counters.get(`${key}:${current}`) ?? 0;
  const prev = counters.get(`${key}:${current - 1}`) ?? 0;

  // weight the previous window by the portion of it still inside the
  // sliding window: elapsed = 0.25 means 75% of the previous window counts
  const estimate = cur + prev * (1 - elapsed);

  if (estimate >= limit) {
    return { allowed: false, retryAfterSeconds: Math.ceil(windowSec * (1 - elapsed)) };
  }
  counters.set(`${key}:${current}`, cur + 1);
  return { allowed: true, remaining: Math.floor(limit - estimate - 1) };
}
// counters older than two windows are dead weight: sweep them on write,
// or set a TTL of 2 * windowSec and let the store do it for you.

Choose by ceiling and by memory, not by habit

The tie-breaker is the size of the ceiling. A sliding window log with a limit of 5 attempts per 15 minutes stores at most five numbers per key: exact, cheap, and obviously correct. The same algorithm at 10,000 requests per minute stores 10,000 timestamps per key, per route, per client, and trimming the log is O(n) on every single request — at which point the limiter is a larger load on your system than the traffic it is limiting.

The comparison below records, for each algorithm, its memory behaviour, its worst failure and the case it was designed for. Pick the row whose failure you can live with.

// FIXED WINDOW
//   count resets on a fixed boundary
//   1   2   3   4   5   6   7   8   9   0   1   2   3   4   5
//   |-------- window 1 --------||-------- window 2 --------|
// +  one memory cell, O(1)
// -  BURST PROBLEM: 60 requests at 10:00:59 and 60 at 10:01:00 is 120
//    in two seconds, against a limit of 60/minute
// -  boundary-dependent: the limit changes at midnight
// -  good for: coarse budgets, quota accounting

// SLIDING WINDOW LOG
//   store the timestamp of every request, slide the window
// +  exact, no burst
// +  no boundary problem
// -  O(n) memory where n is requests in the window
// -  if a client is allowed 1000/min you store 1000 timestamps per
//    client, per route
// -  good for: security-sensitive limits, low ceilings

// SLIDING WINDOW COUNTER
//   a weighted blend of the current and previous window
// +  O(1) memory, no boundary problem
// -  approximate
// -  good for: most production use

// TOKEN BUCKET
//   tokens accrue at a fixed rate, up to a burst capacity
// +  allows controlled bursts, which is what real traffic looks like
// +  O(1)
// +  the most useful behaviour model for an API
// -  requires arithmetic rather than a counter

// LEAKY BUCKET
//   like a token bucket but the bucket drains at a constant rate and
//   holds no burst allowance
// +  perfectly smooth output
// -  bad for APIs: it punishes a client for saving up quota
// -  good for: writing to a downstream with a hard rate limit

Token bucket

Capacity is burst, refill is sustained

A token bucket has two numbers and they answer two different questions. refillPerSecond is the sustained rate you are willing to serve forever, and it is the number your capacity planning should use. capacity is the burst allowance — how many requests a client may make back-to-back after an idle period — and it is a product decision, not a measure of your servers.

The mistake is to derive capacity from hardware. A bucket with a 6,000 capacity and 100 tokens per second does not mean you can serve 6,000 concurrent requests; it means that if a thousand clients each arrive after an idle period at the same moment, you have promised to absorb 6,000 requests before any refill. Measure the burst you need from your own traffic, then watch the 429 rate after you deploy, because a 429 to a well-behaved client is a capacity signal rather than a security success.

// size the bucket from observed traffic, not from the size of the machine
const observed = {
  // requests per key in the busiest 10-second window, read off your logs
  p50Burst: 20,
  p99Burst: 420,
  sustainedRps: 100,          // what you are willing to serve forever
};

// capacity covers the 99th-percentile burst plus headroom; refill covers
// only what arrives for the rest of the minute
const capacity = Math.ceil(observed.p99Burst * 1.2);   // 504
const refillPerSecond = observed.sustainedRps;         // 100

// a client cannot exceed refill for a sustained period, and cannot exceed
// capacity in an instant. both numbers are enforced by the same arithmetic.
const limiter = new TokenBucketLimiter(capacity, refillPerSecond);

// sanity check the shape of the decision over a busy minute
//   t=0    burst 504  -> all allowed, bucket empty
//   t=1s   100 more   -> 100 allowed, bucket back to 0
//   t=2s   600 more   -> 100 allowed, 500 rejected with Retry-After: 1
// the rejections are correct: 600/s is six times the sustained rate.

Charge cost, not calls

Once the bucket exists, making it charge more than one token turns a rate limiter into a cost limiter with no extra machinery. Give every route a weight in one table, charge it on the request, and return the weight in the rate-limit headers so a client can see what a call actually cost. The client that calls the report endpoint twenty times and is throttled has learned something useful; the same client throttled by a call counter has learned nothing.

Keep the weights in one place and make them part of the API contract. A weight that changes silently is a breaking change (ch7), and a weight of zero — which happens when someone forgets a route — is an endpoint with no limit at all.

const COSTS = {
  'GET /v1/health': 0,          // deliberately unlimited, but see the note
  'GET /v1/products': 1,
  'GET /v1/orders': 5,          // joins, filtered, paginated
  'POST /v1/orders': 10,
  'POST /v1/reports': 200,      // scans the orders table
  'GET /v1/reports/:id/export': 500,
};

function costOf(req, route) {
  const weight = COSTS[`${req.method} ${route}`];
  // an unknown route must never be free: fail closed, not open
  return weight ?? 1;
}

// in the middleware, the only difference from a plain limiter is the cost
app.use(async (req, res, next) => {
  const cost = costOf(req, req.route);
  const d = limiter.check(keyFor(req), cost);
  res.set('RateLimit-Limit', String(capacity));
  res.set('RateLimit-Remaining', String(d.remaining));
  if (!d.allowed) return res.status(429).set('Retry-After', String(d.retryAfterSeconds)).end();
  next();
});

// a cost of 0 is a decision, not an oversight: only health checks and
// static metadata should ever be free, and they must be cheap by
// construction. Everything that touches the database costs at least 1.

The implementation, and the one idea in it

The class below is thirty lines and contains exactly one non-obvious idea: there is no timer. Tokens accrue lazily, computed on read from the wall-clock gap since the bucket was last touched. That single decision makes the limiter O(1) in both time and space, makes an idle client free to track, and removes the million-timers problem that a real refill loop would create for a million keys.

Read it with the two consequences in mind, because both are discussed in the second half of the sample: a bucket that has refilled to full is indistinguishable from a bucket that never existed, which is what makes eviction safe; and a bucket that is charged varying costs needs only the arithmetic to change.

// the token bucket, in full
//   capacity:  the largest burst allowed
//   refillRate: tokens per second added back
//   a request costs 1 token (or more for an expensive endpoint)

type Bucket = { tokens: number; lastRefillMs: number };

class TokenBucketLimiter {
  constructor(
    private capacity: number,        // burst size
    private refillPerSecond: number,
  ) {}

  // returns { allowed, retryAfterSeconds, remaining }
  check(key: string, cost = 1, now = Date.now()): Decision {
    const b = this.getBucket(key, now);   // lazy refill on read

    if (b.tokens >= cost) {
      b.tokens -= cost;
      return { allowed: true, retryAfterSeconds: 0, remaining: Math.floor(b.tokens) };
    }
    const deficit = cost - b.tokens;
    return {
      allowed: false,
      retryAfterSeconds: Math.ceil(deficit / this.refillPerSecond),
      remaining: 0,
    };
  }

  private getBucket(key: string, now: number): Bucket {
    let b = this.buckets.get(key);
    if (!b) {
      b = { tokens: this.capacity, lastRefillMs: now };
      this.buckets.set(key, b);
    }
    // LAZY REFILL: compute how much time has passed since the last
    // interaction and add the tokens for that interval. This is what
    // makes the limiter O(1) and stateless-looking.
    const elapsedSec = (now - b.lastRefillMs) / 1000;
    if (elapsedSec > 0) {
      b.tokens = Math.min(this.capacity, b.tokens + elapsedSec * this.refillPerSecond);
      b.lastRefillMs = now;
    }
    return b;
  }
}

// a concrete policy
//   6000 capacity, 100 per second
//   means: burst of 6000, then sustained 100/s
const limiter = new TokenBucketLimiter(6000, 100);

// WHY LAZY REFILL MATTERS
//   a timer that refills every second for 1 million clients is a
//   million timers. lazy refill computes the same result on read, and
//   a client that stops calling costs you nothing to track.
//
// THE EVICTION PROBLEM
//   a Map of buckets grows forever. One bucket per IP per route, and
//   an attacker rotates IPs.
//   FIX: sweep periodically, dropping buckets that are full (a full
//   bucket is indistinguishable from a new one, so it is safe to drop).
//   This makes the store bounded AND lossless, which is a neat trick
//   specific to token buckets.

The store must be bounded, and full buckets are free to drop

A bucket map grows without bound as keys come and go, and an attacker who rotates IPs is a machine for manufacturing keys. The eviction rule falls out of the model: a bucket that has refilled to capacity holds exactly the same state as a brand-new bucket, so dropping it changes no future decision. Sweep periodically and delete every bucket whose tokens would be at capacity by now; the store stays bounded and the limiter stays lossless.

The sweep below also demonstrates the second use of weights. A store of credits — rather than a store of tokens — is how the same arithmetic expresses quotas: the report endpoint costs 200 credits, a product page costs one, and a client's budget is enforced by the same check() call. That is the difference between a limiter and a pricing model: one rate, many prices.

// the sweep
setInterval(() => {
  const now = Date.now();
  for (const [key, b] of limiter.buckets) {
    const elapsedSec = (now - b.lastRefillMs) / 1000;
    if (b.tokens + elapsedSec * limiter.refillPerSecond >= capacity) {
      limiter.buckets.delete(key);     // refilled to full: equivalent to new
    }
  }
}, 60_000).unref();

// cost-based limiting for expensive endpoints
const COSTS: Record<string, number> = {
  'GET /v1/products': 1,
  'GET /v1/orders': 5,
  'POST /v1/reports': 200,        // 200 credits
  'GET /v1/reports/expensive': 500,
};
// a client with 10,000 credits/minute can call the report endpoint 20
// times, or the product endpoint 10,000 times. that is cost control,
// and it is the part that keeps your infrastructure bill predictable.

Sliding window log

Trim with an index, not shift()

The sliding window log is exact: it stores the timestamp of every request and slides the window over the stored list. Its cost is that the trim is O(n) — shift() re-copies the array on every call, and a long window with a high ceiling spends more time trimming than deciding. The fix is to advance a head index past expired entries and compact rarely, which makes the steady-state check O(1) and keeps the code simple enough to audit.

An index pointer instead of a shift is the difference between a limiter that survives at a ceiling of 10,000 per minute and one that caps you at a few hundred, and it is the hardest-won detail in this section: the naive version works, which is exactly why the bug goes unnoticed until the load arrives.

// the same log as below, but with O(1) trim and occasional compaction
class SocialLoginGuard {
  private entries: number[] = [];
  private head = 0;

  check(key: string, now = Date.now()): boolean {
    const cutoff = now - 15 * 60_000;

    // advance the head past expired timestamps WITHOUT re-copying
    while (this.head < this.entries.length && this.entries[this.head] <= cutoff) {
      this.head++;
    }

    // compact only when the dead prefix has grown large
    if (this.head > 10_000) {
      this.entries = this.entries.slice(this.head);
      this.head = 0;
    }

    if (this.entries.length - this.head >= 5) return false;   // limit reached
    this.entries.push(now);
    return true;
  }
}
// note the bound still holds: the live portion is never longer than it
// was with shift(), and compaction cannot resurrect expired timestamps.

Key by the pair, not by the IP

Login limiting is the canonical sliding-window case, and the key choice decides whether the limiter protects anything. Keyed by IP alone, a password reset on a corporate NAT locks out an entire office after a handful of attempts; keyed by email alone, an attacker who knows a victim's address can trivially mount a distributed brute force. The pair — ip + normalised email — stops both: the office's four users only consume their own four slots, and ten thousand distinct IPs at one account get exactly five attempts between them.

Normalise before you key. Trim whitespace, lowercase, and canonicalise the account identifier, because User@Example.com and user@example.com are one login to you and two keys to the limiter.

// the route, with the pair-keyed guard in front of the credential check
app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  const email = String(req.body.email).trim().toLowerCase();
  const key = `${req.ip}:${email}`;

  if (!loginGuard.check(key)) {
    return res.status(429).set('Retry-After', '900').json({
      error: { code: 'too_many_attempts', retryAfterSeconds: 900 },
    });
  }
  const ok = await verifyCredentials(email, req.body.password);
  if (!ok) {
    // failed logins consume the budget too: count the attempt
    loginGuard.check(key);          // a second consume within the same guard
    return res.status(401).json({ error: { code: 'invalid_credentials' } });
  }
  // success resets the pair's budget: a legitimate owner is not punished
  // for a past attacker's choice of their email address
  loginGuard.reset(key);
});
// keyed by IP alone, this route would lock out a shared office router
// after five attempts across five desks. keyed by the pair, it does not.

The memory ceiling decides when this is the right tool

The exact log is the right tool when the ceiling is low and the consequence of a burst is severe — login attempts, coupon redemption, password reset. At a ceiling of five per fifteen minutes, five timestamps per key is nothing, and the exactness buys a guarantee that a fixed window cannot make: a client that times out one attempt at a boundary cannot land a sixth request legally. At a ceiling in the thousands, the same guarantee costs thousands of timestamps per key and a trim on every request, and the approximate counter from earlier in this chapter is correct.

The implementation below is the one that starts this decision. Note where it stores the timestamps, where it trims them, and what retry-after it computes — then measure before you scale it tenfold.

// exact, at the cost of storing every timestamp
class SlidingWindowLimiter {
  constructor(private limit: number, private windowMs: number) {}

  check(key: string, now = Date.now()): Decision {
    const cutoff = now - this.windowMs;
    const log = this.logs.get(key) ?? [];

    // drop timestamps that have aged out. this is the "sliding" part.
    while (log.length && log[0] <= cutoff) log.shift();

    if (log.length >= this.limit) {
      const retryAfterMs = log[0] + this.windowMs - now;
      return { allowed: false, retryAfterSeconds: Math.ceil(retryAfterMs / 1000) };
    }
    log.push(now);
    this.logs.set(key, log);
    return { allowed: true, retryAfterSeconds: 0, remaining: this.limit - log.length };
  }
}

// when this is the right choice
//   - the limit is LOW (under ~100), so the log is small
//   - the limit is a security control: login attempts, password reset,
//     coupon redemption, anything where exceeding the limit is abuse
//   - bursts must be impossible: "5 password attempts per 15 minutes"
//     with a fixed window would allow 10 at the boundary

// login attempt limiting is the canonical case
const loginLimiter = new SlidingWindowLimiter(5, 15 * 60_000);
// key by IP + email, not just IP, or a NAT'd office locks itself out
const key = `${ip}:${normalise(email)}`;

// memory: 5 timestamps per key is nothing. 10,000 timestamps per key
// is 10,000 numbers. Choose the algorithm by the ceiling, not by habit.

// the reset problem
// log.shift() is O(n). Over a long window with many entries this is
// the dominant cost. Use an index pointer instead of shifting, and
// compact periodically.

Distributed enforcement

One decision per script, never two round trips

With twenty instances behind a load balancer, an in-memory bucket per instance means the effective limit is twenty times what you configured, and a shared store is the correction. But a shared store introduces a worse failure: a check followed by a consume is two round trips, and between them another instance can drain the same bucket. Two servers both read three tokens, both subtract three, and the limit is silently doubled — a race in a rate limiter is a rate limiter that does not rate limit.

The fix is not a lock, it is atomicity: run the whole read-modify-write as one Redis Lua script, which executes to completion without interleaving. The round trip still happens, but there is only one of them, and the cost is a bounded latency hit on a hot path that a local-first design can avoid (next heading).

// THE WRONG WAY: check, then consume, in two round trips
const n = await redis.hget(`bucket:${key}`, 'tokens');      // round trip 1
if (Number(n) >= cost) {
  await redis.hincrby(`bucket:${key}`, 'tokens', -cost);    // round trip 2
  allow();
}
// between the two round trips, instance 7 may have drained the same
// bucket: both servers read 3 tokens, both subtract, neither was denied.

// THE RIGHT WAY: one script, one decision
const r = await redis.eval(TOKEN_BUCKET_LUA, {
  keys: [`rl:${key}:${routeGroup}`],
  arguments: [String(capacity), String(refillPerSec), String(cost), String(nowMs)],
});
// r[0] === 1 -> allowed, r[1] = remaining tokens. atomic by construction
// because Redis serialises scripts; this is also why the EXPIRE lives
// inside the script and not in a separate call.

Divide locally, reconcile centrally

The alternative is a local bucket per instance set to limit / N, with usage reported to a shared store for dashboards and alerting. The fast path never leaves the machine, so the limiter costs nothing on the hot path and degrades gracefully when Redis is slow. It is an approximate enforcement: the aggregate ceiling is N × local, and an instance that happens to serve 40% of the traffic while enforcing 1/N throttles clients that another instance would let through.

That makes it wrong in exactly the situations the shared store is right for. A security ceiling — login attempts, coupon redemption — must be exact and cross-instance, because the attacker is the one person not sharing your load. A traffic-shaping ceiling on a public endpoint can be approximate, because the cost of a 1/N error is a slightly early 429, not a breach.

// local-first approximation: each instance enforces 1/N of the limit
const SHARDS = await registry.instanceCount();              // 20 instances
const localLimit = Math.max(1, Math.floor(GLOBAL_LIMIT / SHARDS));

// fast path: the in-process bucket, no network
const d = shardBucket.check(key, cost);
// slow path, fire-and-forget: usage for dashboards and global alerting
reportUsage(key, cost);

// the failure modes to test against
//   - a rolling deploy changes N mid-day, so a fresh pod enforces 1/21
//     while traffic is still split 20 ways: clients get throttled for
//     the other pod's share
//   - traffic skew: one instance at 40% of traffic and 1/20 of the
//     limit is rejecting clients it should serve
// fine for shaping, wrong for security. the composite policy in the
// next section uses the shared store for the security rows and a local
// bucket for the volume rows.

The edge is a limiter that costs you nothing

There is a third place to enforce, and it is the cheapest one: the CDN or API gateway in front of your servers. Nginx limit_req, an API Gateway throttle or a Cloudflare rate rule rejects traffic before it reaches your instances, consumes the actual origin resources, or occupies your sockets. It is also the only layer that protects you from traffic your application should never have seen — a scraper at 10,000 requests per second is a network problem, and answering it in application code means your application is the network's buffer.

It is a coarse instrument: it keys on IP or a header, knows nothing of your pricing tiers, and cannot charge cost per route. The division of labour that works in practice is the one in the sample that follows: the edge absorbs volume and shape, the application enforces the contract, and the database is protected by a timeout.

// the problem: your API is 20 instances behind a load balancer.
// an in-memory counter on each instance means the effective limit is
// 20x what you configured, and it is not even consistent.

// THE OPTIONS

// 1. A SHARED STORE. Correct, and it costs a round trip per request.
// Redis, the most common choice:
const r = await redis.eval(TOKEN_BUCKET_LUA, {
  keys: [`rl:${apiKey}:${routeGroup}`],
  arguments: [String(capacity), String(refillPerSec), String(cost), String(nowMs)],
});
// Do the whole check-and-consume in ONE Lua script. Two round trips
// introduces a race, and a race in a rate limiter is a rate limiter
// that does not rate limit.

// tokenbucket.lua, atomically
//   local tokens = tonumber(redis.call('HMGET', KEYS[1], 'tokens')[1])
//   local ts = tonumber(redis.call('HMGET', KEYS[1], 'ts')[1])
//   if tokens == nil then tokens = capacity; ts = now end
//   local delta = math.max(0, now - ts) / 1000
//   tokens = math.min(capacity, tokens + delta * refill)
//   local allowed = 0
//   if tokens >= cost then tokens = tokens - cost; allowed = 1 end
//   redis.call('HMSET', KEYS[1], 'tokens', tokens, 'ts', now)
//   redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], math.ceil(capacity / refill) + 60)
//   return { allowed, math.floor(tokens) }

// 2. A LOCAL COUNTER plus a periodic sync to a shared store.
//    Approximate: each instance enforces 1/N of the limit, and reports
//    usage centrally. Fast, and the effective limit is N times the
//    configured one - which you compensate for by dividing. Fine for
//    traffic shaping, wrong for security limits.

// 3. AT THE EDGE. Cloudflare, API Gateway, an nginx limit_req zone.
//    The limit is enforced before your servers see the traffic, which
//    is both faster and more protective. Use this for coarse
//    protection, and your application for anything fine-grained.
//
// WHAT TO CHOOSE
//   login attempts, password reset, coupon redemption
//     -> must be exact and shared. Redis or the data store.
//   overall request volume
//     -> the edge. Your application will not survive what gets past it.
//   internal service-to-service
//     -> often not needed at all: a service mesh or a connection
//        pool limit is more appropriate.

Headers and client behaviour

Emit the headers on every response

The rate-limit headers are control information, and they belong on every response, not only on the 429. A client that reads RateLimit-Remaining on successful requests can slow down voluntarily ahead of the ceiling; a client that only discovers the limit by being throttled cannot. The RateLimit- prefix is the IETF draft convention and the one new integrators will look for; the X-RateLimit-* form is what most existing clients actually parse, so emit both until the ecosystem has moved.

RateLimit-Reset is the one header people argue about: the IETF draft wants a fixed UTC instant, the original convention sent a resets-in-seconds value, and X-RateLimit-Reset-After exists because "how long do I wait" is the question a client actually asks. Whatever you choose, be consistent and document it, because a client that misparses Reset as a duration when it is a timestamp will sleep for fifty years.

// middleware that reports the budget on success AND on 429
app.use(async (req, res, next) => {
  const d = limiter.check(keyFor(req));

  res.set({
    'RateLimit-Limit': String(capacity),
    'RateLimit-Remaining': String(d.remaining),
    'RateLimit-Reset': String(windowEndsAtEpochSeconds),
    'X-RateLimit-Limit': String(capacity),
    'X-RateLimit-Remaining': String(d.remaining),
    'X-RateLimit-Reset': String(windowEndsAtEpochSeconds),
    'X-RateLimit-Reset-After': String(d.resetAfterSeconds),
  });

  if (!d.allowed) {
    res.set('Retry-After', String(d.retryAfterSeconds));
    return res.status(429).json({
      error: {
        code: 'rate_limited',
        message: `Rate limit exceeded. Retry after ${d.retryAfterSeconds}s.`,
        retryAfterSeconds: d.retryAfterSeconds,
      },
    });
  }
  next();
});
// the body matters for clients that log bodies and for humans reading
// logs years later: the headers are the contract, the body is the story.

What a good client does with Retry-After

A well-behaved client reads the header and waits. The two details that distinguish good from bad are jitter and an upper bound. Without jitter, ten clients that hit a 429 at the same moment all sleep exactly the same interval and all retry at the same moment — the thundering herd is a coordinated self-inflicted attack. Without an upper bound, a client in a retry loop turns a downed or misconfigured route into a constant background drumbeat of requests that itself looks like an attack.

Note that a retry-after value of zero or an absent header is not permission for an instant retry: treat a missing header as the most conservative case (the limit is presumably still active) and apply the same jittered backoff.

// a well-behaved client: honour the header, jitter, and cap the loop
function jitter(waitMs: number) {
  return Math.floor(Math.random() * waitMs);       // full jitter: 0..wait
}

async function callWithRateLimit(fn, maxAttempts = 5) {
  let attempts = 0;
  for (;;) {
    const res = await fn();
    if (res.status !== 429) return res;
    if (++attempts >= maxAttempts) throw new Error('backoff exhausted');

    const header = res.headers.get('retry-after');
    const baseMs = (Number(header) || 5) * 1000;   // absent -> 5s minimum
    await sleep(baseMs + jitter(baseMs));
  }
}
// the loop must terminate: a 503 with Retry-After on every attempt is
// "your quota is spent", and retrying it to exhaustion helps nobody.
// surface the exhaustion to the caller, and let a human decide.

The 429 response, and the client that ignores it

When the limit is exceeded, the complete answer — status, headers, and a body the client can log — is the one shown below. The status code is the machinery, Retry-After is the instruction, and the JSON body is the part a support ticket will quote back to you. For clients that ignore all three, the escalation is not a bigger 429 but a different decision: a penalty multiplier for repeat offenders, or a temporary block at the edge, applied to the key, not to the IP, because an IP is shared and a key is not.

The sample below is the canonical response shape and the header set that most clients still parse. The numbers are illustrative; the shape is the contract.

// 429 Too Many Requests
Retry-After: 30

RateLimit-Limit: 100
RateLimit-Remaining: 0
RateLimit-Reset: 1773587230        // seconds until the window resets

{
  "error": {
    "code": "rate_limited",
    "message": "Too many requests. Retry after 30 seconds.",
    "details": { "limit": 100, "windowSeconds": 60, "retryAfterSeconds": 30 }
  }
}

// the standard header set (draft IETF ratelimit-headers, and the
// older RFC-style x- prefixed set that most clients still parse)
RateLimit-Limit: 100
RateLimit-Remaining: 42
RateLimit-Reset: 45
X-RateLimit-Limit: 100
X-RateLimit-Remaining: 42
X-RateLimit-Reset: 1773587230
X-RateLimit-Reset-After: 45          // seconds, more useful than an epoch

// set them on SUCCESSFUL responses too, not only on 429. A client that
// can see it is approaching the limit can slow down voluntarily, which
// is far better for everyone than discovering the limit by being
// throttled.

// WHAT GOOD CLIENTS DO
//   read Retry-After, wait that long, retry with jitter
//   a well-behaved SDK exposes a backoff hook and does this for you

// WHAT BAD CLIENTS DO, and what you do about it
//   ignore Retry-After and hammer at the same rate
//     -> double or triple your penalty for repeat offenders, or
//        temporarily block at the edge
//   many clients behind one NAT share an IP
//     -> rate limit on API key where one exists, not on IP. An IP
//        limit punishes an office for its router's public address.
//   a mobile client on a moving network changes IP mid-session
//     -> prefer user or token identity over IP

Which key to limit on

Every dimension leaks something

There is no perfect key, only a leak you can live with, and the discipline of this section is to write the leak down before you choose. IP survives everything and needs no credentials, but it punishes the office behind a NAT and the phone on a moving network, and it is trivial to rotate. An API key is stable and fair, but anonymous endpoints have no key, and one compromised account can mint a thousand keys. A user id matches the commercial relationship exactly — the customer's plan is a user's ceiling — but it is blind to the case where one user is many IPs.

The pattern that falls out is a ladder: use the most identity-heavy key you have on the request (user, then key, then IP) and keep the weaker dimensions only where the stronger one cannot exist.

// the key functions, and the leak each one implies
const KEYS = {
  ip:     (req) => `ip:${req.ip}`,
  // leaks: a shared NAT divides one household's budget among its users;
  // a mobile client on a moving network loses all history mid-ride
  apiKey: (req) => `key:${req.apiKey ?? 'anon'}`,
  // leaks: nothing about one-account-many-keys abuse, and anonymous
  // traffic has no key, so it falls through to ip anyway
  user:   (req) => `user:${req.userId}`,
  // leaks: one user, many IPs - which is the attack the ip row exists
  // for, so the user row alone is not enough on a public endpoint
};
// the choice is which guarantee is lost when the dimension is spoofed,
// not which dimension is hardest to spoof.

The composite: independent limits, tightest wins

The design that survives production is several independent limits applied to the same request, each keyed on a different dimension, with the tightest deciding. It is not a clever data structure — it is the insistence that no single value grants a bypass. A bot farm rotating IPs dies on the API-key row; the legitimate office sharing one router dies on none. Each row has its own ceiling because each dimension has its own plausibility: an IP behind a large NAT legitimately sees more traffic than one user, and the user row is the one the customer's pricing tier actually grants.

Order matters in one way only: evaluate the cheapest dimensions first and short-circuit on the first denial, because the IP row is a hash lookup and the user row may be a database call.

const LIMITS = [
  { key: (req) => `ip:${req.ip}`,          limit: 600,   windowSec: 60 },
  { key: (req) => `key:${req.apiKey}`,    limit: 10_000, windowSec: 60 },
  { key: (req) => `user:${req.userId}`,   limit: 1_000,  windowSec: 60 },
];

function enforceAll(req): Decision {
  let tightest: Decision = { allowed: true, retryAfterSeconds: 0 };
  for (const { key, limit, windowSec } of LIMITS) {
    const d = limiter.check(key(req), limit, windowSec);
    if (!d.allowed) return d;                    // first denial wins
    if (d.retryAfterSeconds > tightest.retryAfterSeconds) tightest = d;
  }
  return tightest;
}
// one request must clear THREE rows. the ip row stops the office, the
// key row stops the bot farm, the user row is the plan the customer
// bought - and the dashboard should report which row denied each request.

The tier is the policy

The numbers in the tiered example below are the product surface: they are what customers compare, what the sales page quotes, and what support argues about. Put them in configuration, not in code, and derive them from the pricing tier at request time. The weight of two further decisions will show up immediately: whether anonymous requests get the lowest tier (they should), and whether a tier change takes effect on the next request or the next window (it should be the next request, which means the limiter must read the tier per call rather than caching it).

The example that follows is a composite in miniature — three independent limits, the tightest wins — and it is the shape to copy for your own policy, with your own ceilings, and with the user row carrying the tier you sell.

// the choice of key is a policy decision, and there is no single right one

// BY IP
//   + survives everything, works with no credentials
//   - punishes shared NAT (offices, mobile carriers, CGNAT)
//   - trivial to evade by rotating source addresses
//   USE FOR: anonymous endpoints, as a coarse abuse floor

// BY API KEY
//   + stable, fair, survives IP changes
//   - requires a credential
//   USE FOR: server-to-server, and any authenticated client. This is
//           the right default for a commercial API.

// BY USER / ACCOUNT ID
//   + exactly matches the commercial relationship
//   + lets you set limits per pricing tier
//   - one user behind a corporate NAT does not exhaust their own limit
//   USE FOR: the plan limits customers actually see: "your plan
//           includes 10,000 calls per month"

// COMPOSITE
//   { ip, apiKey, userId } all rate limited independently, with
//   different ceilings. The tightest wins.
//   THIS IS THE RIGHT DESIGN for a public API: several independent
//   limits, so no single dimension is a bypass.

// A TIERED EXAMPLE
const LIMITS = [
  { key: (r) => `ip:${r.ip}`,           rate: 600,   burst: 60,   windowSec: 60 },
  { key: (r) => `key:${r.apiKey}`,     rate: 10_000, burst: 500,  windowSec: 60 },
  { key: (r) => `user:${r.userId}`,    rate: 1000,  burst: 100,  windowSec: 60 },
];
// all three must pass. A single attacker with 10,000 IPs is stopped by
// the apiKey limit. A legitimate office is stopped by neither.

Layered limits

The edge rejects traffic before it costs anything

The first layer is the one that never allocates a socket on your servers. An nginx limit_req zone, an API Gateway throttle or a CDN rate rule keys on the connection metadata it already holds, and a response from the edge is cheaper by orders of magnitude than the same rejection after you have parsed a request, opened a connection to the database and started a transaction. More importantly, the edge is the only layer that protects you from traffic your application was never meant to see: ten thousand requests per second from one botnet are a network event, not an application event.

The trade is bluntness. The edge keys on an IP or a header, so it cannot know your pricing tiers, and it throttles by request, not by cost. Keep it coarse and generous; the fine-grained contract belongs one layer deeper.

# LAYER 1 - the edge, before your origin sees a byte
http {
  # one shared zone of 10 MB of state, keyed by IP, 20 r/s sustained
  limit_req_zone $binary_remote_addr zone=api:10m rate=20r/s;
  server {
    listen 80;
    location /api/ {
      # a burst of 40; nodelay means it is served immediately, not
      # smoothed out over time (smoothing would let a client sustain
      # the burst rate forever)
      limit_req zone=api burst=40 nodelay;
      limit_req_status 429;
      proxy_pass http://api_backend;
    }
  }
}
# a CDN rule is the same idea with more geography: rate the whole ASN
# or country, so a bot population is blocked before its first request
# is even load-balanced to you.

The database layer is the last resort, and it sheds

The bottom layer has no idea who a client is, and that is fine — its job is to protect itself. A connection pool with a bounded queue turns overload into an explicit refusal instead of a slow-motion outage: every request that cannot get a connection within a budget returns 503 with Retry-After, and the request that is refused cost you nothing but its own socket. The alternative is the classic incident: a few thousand requests all queueing inside the pool, each timing out after thirty seconds, while the workers behind the pool grind at a fraction of the pace — the pool becomes a denial of service aimed at your own database.

This is load-shedding, and it must be a deliberate decision with its own response shape, not the accidental behaviour of a full pool. The distinction is measurable: a shed request returns in milliseconds with a clear contract; a queueing request returns in thirty seconds with a timeout error that looks like every other timeout.

// explicit load shedding at the pool boundary
const QUEUE_DEPTH_LIMIT = 1000;
const backlogs = new Map();   // per route, so a hot route sheds alone

async function acceptJob(route, job) {
  const depth = (backlogs.get(route) ?? 0) + 1;
  if (depth > QUEUE_DEPTH_LIMIT) {
    // shed: the caller can retry in 5s; they cost us one response
    return { status: 503, retryAfter: 5 };
  }
  backlogs.set(route, depth);
  try {
    await pool.query(...);          // bounded workers behind the gate
  } finally {
    backlogs.set(route, depth - 1);
  }
  return { status: 200 };
}

// the same decision also belongs in the database configuration itself:
//   ALTER SYSTEM SET statement_timeout = '10s';          -- never 30+
//   ALTER SYSTEM SET max_connections = 100;
// the timeout is the database's load-shedding, and a 10s hard ceiling
// beats a default 30s that nobody chose on purpose.

The middle layers carry the contract

Between the edge and the database sit the layers that know things: the application, with a token bucket per API key and cost per route, and the operation layer, with per-operation quotas such as "ten report generations per day". A quota is not a rate limiter — it is a counter row with a period, reconciled against billing — and the two belong in different stores for a good reason: a rate limiter can lose state without harm, a quota cannot.

The example below walks all four layers for one request. Notice what each layer knows and what it does not, because that knowledge asymmetry is the argument for having four layers at all: the edge does not know your pricing plan, the application does not know an IP is a bot farm, and the database knows nothing and catches everything, too late — but catching everything, too late, is exactly why it has the timeout.

// LAYER 1: the edge or CDN - coarse, volumetric
//   reject 10,000 req/s from one ASN before it reaches your origin
//   protects you from the traffic you would not have served anyway
//   Cloudflare rate limiting rules, API Gateway throttling, nginx:
//     limit_req_zone $binary_remote_addr zone=api:10m rate=20r/s;
//     limit_req zone=api burst=40 nodelay;

// LAYER 2: the application - semantic, per route group
//   the token bucket from earlier, per API key
//   this is where cost control lives

// LAYER 3: the expensive operation - semantic, per operation
//   "10 report generations per day", "100 exports per month"
//   often implemented as a counter row rather than a rate limiter,
//   because it is a quota, not a rate

// LAYER 4: the database - the last resort
//   statement_timeout, max_connections, a connection pool with a
//   queue depth limit
//   if the pool is exhausted, return 503 with Retry-After rather than
//   letting requests queue indefinitely and time out at 30 seconds

// WHY LAYERS MATTER
//   a single-layer limiter is bypassed by whatever that layer does not
//   know about. Layer 1 does not know your pricing plan. Layer 2 does
//   not know an IP is a bot farm. Layer 4 knows nothing and catches
//   everything, too late.
//   The right design: the edge absorbs volume, the application enforces
//   the contract, and the database is protected by a timeout.

Beyond rate limits

Enumeration is defeated by opaque ids and a 404

Enumeration is the attack that a rate limiter structurally cannot stop: the attacker walks /v1/users/1, /2, /3 at a perfectly legal rate, and every request succeeds or fails exactly as it should. The three parts of the defence are about information, not volume. First, unguessable identifiers — a UUID instead of an incrementing integer makes the walk a dead end. Second, 404 rather than 403 for someone else's resource: a 403 is a positive answer to "does this object exist?", which is precisely the fact a sequence-walk is trying to establish. Third, alert on the pattern itself, because a burst of 404s across consecutive ids bypasses every limiter while announcing itself in your logs.

The access-check code below is the piece teams get wrong most often: the existence check and the authorisation check are fused so that either failure produces the same response.

// the fused existence + authorisation check: one response for both
app.get('/v1/users/:id', async (req, res) => {
  const user = await db.users.find(req.params.id);
  if (!user || user.orgId !== req.user.orgId) {
    // 404 for EITHER "does not exist" or "not yours". a 403 here
    // answers the enumeration question for the attacker.
    return res.status(404).json({ error: { code: 'not_found' } });
  }
  return res.json(user);
});

// the alarm for a walk in progress (the limiter will never see it)
SELECT count(*) AS attempts, count(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 404) AS missing,
       count(DISTINCT id) AS distinct_ids
  FROM access_log
 WHERE path ~ '^/v1/users/[0-9]+$'
   AND at > now() - interval '1 minute';
-- distinct_ids high and missing high: a sequence walk, whichever
-- limiter is on top. alert, and consider per-object read ceilings.

Credential stuffing is an account pattern

Credential stuffing has one reliable fingerprint and it is not the IP — the attacker has thousands. It is the account: one email address probed from dozens of distinct source addresses in a short window. The limiter for this class must therefore key on the account, with per-account ceilings for login and password reset, and the alarm is the IP cardinality per account. A dozen IPs and one inbox is the signal; a per-IP limiter would not only miss it, it would blame the legitimate owner behind a NAT for the attacker's traffic.

The response body is part of the defence too. If your login endpoint distinguishes "unknown user" from "wrong password", you have built the attacker a free user-enumeration oracle: the stuffing script uses your 401 differences to harvest valid addresses before trying a credential at all.

-- the stuffing alarm: many sources, one target
SELECT email,
       count(*)                       AS attempts,
       count(DISTINCT ip)             AS sources
  FROM login_attempts
 WHERE attempted_at > now() - interval '15 minutes'
 GROUP BY email
HAVING count(DISTINCT ip) > 10;    -- a dozen IPs converging on one inbox

-- and the response contract that does not leak accounts. both cases
-- return the same 401 body; the only difference is server-side logging
app.post('/login', async (req, res) => {
  const user = await db.users.findByEmail(email);
  const ok = user && (await verifyPassword(user, req.body.password));
  if (!ok) {
    log('login_failed', { email, ip: req.ip, userExists: Boolean(user) });
    return res.status(401).json({ error: { code: 'invalid_credentials' } });
  }
  // ...session...
});
// same body for "no such user" and "wrong password": the oracle is
// closed, and the difference is a log line only the operator can read.

The expensive input is a request-level limit

Resource exhaustion is the attack no rate limit answers, because one legal request is the whole attack: a body that parses into a billion-node array, a page size of a million, a depth that recurses twenty thousand levels. The defences are input limits applied on the way in — maximum body size, maximum array length, maximum nesting depth, maximum page size — plus a timeout and a memory cap on every request. A 50 MB JSON body is valid JSON and a two-second CPU spike in most parsers; the body-size limit is an input limit, not a preference.

One discipline matters everywhere here: reject, do not clamp. Silently clamping a requested page size of 100,000 to 500 hides the client's bug from the client and from your logs. Return a 422 with the ceiling in the body, and let the caller fix their code.

// input limits, applied before the work starts
import express from 'express';
const app = express();
app.use(express.json({ limit: '1mb' }));   // the body-size line in the sand

// a page-size limit that rejects instead of clamping
function parsePage(req, res) {
  const raw = Number(req.query.page_size ?? 100);
  if (raw > 500) {
    res.status(422).json({ error: { code: 'page_size_too_large', max: 500 } });
    return null;
  }
  if (raw < 1) {
    res.status(422).json({ error: { code: 'page_size_invalid' } });
    return null;
  }
  return raw;
}

// a depth guard for recursive payloads, dollar-for-dollar the same
// instrument as the GraphQL depth limit in chapter 9
function depth(obj, d = 0) {
  if (d > 32) throw new Error('nesting_too_deep');
  if (obj && typeof obj === 'object') {
    for (const k of Object.keys(obj)) depth(obj[k], d + 1);
  }
}
// every one of these is a ceiling on the SHAPE of the request, and
// they are the layer beneath all four rate-limit mechanisms above.

Degrade deliberately when the attack lands

The under-attack mode is a different policy, not a bigger limiter. When you are actually being hurt, switch to a low global ceiling and a queue — explicitly, measuredly, and with the essential paths exempted — and serve less rather than fail completely. A degraded service beats none, and the switch belongs in the ops playbook, rehearsed, with the trigger defined in the same sentence as the metric it reads.

The remaining classes in this chapter — scraping, expensive-operation amplification and the ones below — are each a separate decision rather than one rule. The commentary in the following walkthrough keeps them apart, because a single "abuse" bucket is where incidents go to be reclassified repeatedly.

// a rate limiter is not an abuse-prevention system. these are separate:

// ENUMERATION
//   an attacker walks /v1/users/1, /2, /3... at a legal rate.
//   rate limiting does nothing. you need:
//     - unpredictable, long, opaque identifiers (uuid, not sequential)
//     - 404 rather than 403 for other people's resources (ch2)
//     - alerting on sequential-access patterns
//     - per-object rather than per-account limits on sensitive reads

// CREDENTIAL STUFFING
//   rate limit by IP is useless: the attacker has thousands.
//   rate limit by ACCOUNT (not IP), and alert when one account is
//   targeted from many IPs. That pattern is the signal.

// SCRAPING
//   a legitimate-looking rate from a single client.
//   toSterm: rate limits, ToS, robots.txt (advisory), and the honest
//   answer: if it is public, it will be scraped. Add value (an API with
//   SLAs) or accept it and monitor for impact.

// EXPENSIVE-OPERATION AMPLIFICATION
//   a cheap endpoint that triggers an expensive one.
//   "preview" endpoints that do the full work and discard most of it
//   FIX: cost-based limits, and make previews actually cheap

// RESOURCE EXHAUSTION
//   one request that allocates 1GB, or forks 100 processes, or
//   triggers a recursion. No rate limit saves you.
//   FIX: input limits (max page size, max depth, max array length),
//         timeouts everywhere, and a memory cap per request

// THE UNDER-ATTACK MODE
//   when you are actually being hurt, switch to a global low limit and
//   a queue, rather than continuing to serve. Communicate it, and keep
//   the essential paths working. A degraded service beats none.

Exercises 6

  1. Implement a token bucket and write property tests: capacity is never exceeded, refill is capped, and a full bucket after a long idle is indistinguishable from a new one. Then write the sweep and assert the store stays bounded.
  2. Implement a sliding window log for login attempts keyed by IP plus email. Write a test that fires 10 attempts and asserts the 6th onwards are rejected, including across the window boundary.
  3. Implement the Redis token bucket in Lua. Fire 200 concurrent requests against 20 instances and assert the total accepted never exceeds the limit. A naive non-atomic version will fail this test.
  4. Add cost-based limits to your API, with a cheap and an expensive endpoint, and prove the expensive one is proportionally restricted.
  5. Enumerate your API's endpoints and classify each as anonymous, key-limited or user-limited. Find any endpoint that takes an object id with no ownership check, and treat that as the priority fix.

7 · Versioning and Deprecation

What counts as a breaking changeThe versioning strategiesURI versioningHeader and media-type versioningEvolutionary design: no versionsGet your clients to upgradeDeprecation as a processSunsetting and the end

You will change your API. The only question is whether you break the clients you did not know about, or whether you planned for it. Versioning is the mechanism for that, and most of it is social rather than technical.

What counts as a breaking change

Classify changes mechanically, in CI

A breaking change is one that a well-behaved client stops working on, and the reliable way to know you have shipped one is to diff the published schema, not to argue about it in review. The diff below compares two OpenAPI documents and labels every delta additive or breaking: a removed request field, a type change, a field that became required, a removed endpoint. The build then fails unless a human has attached a review ticket to the batch. This is the check that catches the changes nobody notices at the time — a status code that changed meaning, a default that moved — because it compares documents, not intentions.

Run it against the last published version, not the last commit. The contract your clients hold is the one that shipped.

// classify a schema diff mechanically; fail the build on "breaking"
// unless the batch carries a review ticket
import { readFileSync } from 'node:fs';
import { parse } from 'yaml';

const v1 = parse(readFileSync('openapi.v1.yaml', 'utf8'));
const v2 = parse(readFileSync('openapi.yaml', 'utf8'));
const verdicts = [];

for (const [pathItem, p1] of Object.entries(v1.paths)) {
  const p2 = v2.paths[pathItem];
  if (!p2) { verdicts.push({ pathItem, kind: 'REMOVED_PATH' }); continue; }
  for (const method of ['get', 'post', 'put', 'patch', 'delete']) {
    if (!p1[method]) continue;
    if (!p2[method]) { verdicts.push({ pathItem, method, kind: 'REMOVED_METHOD' }); continue; }
    const req1 = p1[method].requestBody?.content?.['application/json']?.schema?.properties ?? {};
    const req2 = p2[method].requestBody?.content?.['application/json']?.schema?.properties ?? {};
    for (const [field, schema] of Object.entries(req1)) {
      if (req2[field] === undefined) {
        verdicts.push({ pathItem, method, field, kind: 'REMOVED_REQUEST_FIELD' });
      } else if (req2[field].type !== schema.type) {
        verdicts.push({ pathItem, method, field, kind: 'REQUEST_TYPE_CHANGE' });
      }
    }
    // a required field added to a REQUEST is breaking; one added to a
    // RESPONSE is not, which is why they live in different lists
  }
}

const breaking = verdicts.filter((v) => v.kind !== 'REMOVED_PATH');
if (breaking.length && !process.env.REVIEW_TICKET) {
  console.error('breaking changes without a review ticket:', breaking);
  process.exit(1);
}

The asymmetry is in the client's parser

The reason adding to a response is nearly free while adding to a request is expensive is not a protocol rule, it is a parser habit. A client that JSON-parses a response and reads the fields it knows will silently ignore an unknown field — additive response change, no break. A client that serialises a request strictly will refuse to send an unknown field, or a server that validates strictly will reject it — additive request change, possible break. You cannot know which clients are strict, so the rule is structural: be strict about requests, liberal about responses, and make your own SDKs lenient on the read side by default.

The fatal pattern is a round-trip parser that destructures the fields it knows and rebuilds the object from them. The day the server adds a field, that parser silently deletes it from every subsequent write, and you have manufactured a data-loss bug out of an additive change.

// LENIENT ON THE READ: keep what you do not understand
function parseOrder(raw: string) {
  const data = JSON.parse(raw);
  return {
    id: data.id,                           // required in your version
    status: data.status ?? 'pending',      // a NEW enum value must survive
    note: data.note ?? null,               // a missing field gets a default
    ...data,                               // carry the unknown fields along
  };
}

// THE FATAL VERSION: rebuild from the known fields only
function parseOrderBad(raw: string) {
  const data = JSON.parse(raw);
  return { id: data.id, status: data.status };   // everything else: gone
}
// the next PUT serialises this object. the server's new `total` field
// is deleted by this client on every round trip - a data-loss bug with
// an additive change at its origin, and one your monitoring will not
// see until a support ticket quotes the wrong number back at you.

The rule that prevents most incidents

The classification in this section is worth printing and pinning to the wall: removing or renaming a field, changing a type, tightening validation, changing a default, or changing the meaning of an existing field is breaking, however few clients you believe use it. Adding an optional response field, a new endpoint, a new optional query parameter or loosening validation is not, and the correct posture is to be liberal about every one of those — the cost of an unnecessary major version is paid forever, in support matrix terms.

The one exception to "adding is safe" deserves its own warning: adding an enum value to a response is a real compatibility hazard, because a strict client with an exhaustive switch will fail on a value it cannot exhaust. Document any enum as "may be extended" and version it if the set must stay closed. The strictness contract that governs all of this is spelled out in the callout that follows the sample below.

// BREAKING: a well-behaved client stops working
//   - removing or renaming a field
//   - changing a field's type: string -> number
//   - making an optional field required
//   - adding a required field to a REQUEST body
//   - removing an enum value, or adding one (clients may not handle it)
//   - changing a status code for an existing condition
//   - changing the meaning of an existing field
//   - tightening validation that previously passed
//   - changing a default
//   - removing an endpoint, even one nobody uses

// NOT BREAKING, and you should be liberal here
//   - ADDING an optional response field
//   - ADDING a new endpoint
//   - ADDING a new optional request field
//   - ADDING a new enum value to a response, if the field is documented
//     as "may be extended" (this is a real compatibility hazard: a
//     strict client with an exhaustive switch will break)
//   - loosening validation
//   - adding a new optional query parameter
//   - changing log or diagnostic output

// THE ASYMMETRY, and why it decides your strategy
//   adding to a RESPONSE is nearly free
//   adding to a REQUEST is expensive: a strict client will reject the
//   unknown field, and you cannot know who is strict
//   So: be strict about requests, liberal about responses.

// THE RULE THAT PREVENTS MOST INCIDENTS
//   a client must IGNORE fields it does not recognise.
//   Write that in your documentation, in the first section, in bold.
//   Then you can add response fields forever without breaking anyone.
//   Enforce it in your own SDKs with a "lenient by default" parser.

This is the single highest-leverage thing in API design: the strictness contract with your clients. If your documentation says "unknown fields are ignored", you have an additive-only path forward forever. If it says nothing, every consumer assumes strict parsing, and you are frozen.

The versioning strategies

Every strategy trades visibility for wander-proofing

The four strategies are not interchangeable flavours; each one makes a different trade, and the trade decides the failure mode you will live with. URI versioning (/v1/orders) is visible everywhere — in logs, in curl, in the support ticket a user pastes into Slack — and trivially cache-safe, because different versions are different URLs. Its cost is that clients hardcode the version and then forget to upgrade, which is the whole point but also the whole problem. Header versioning keeps the URL a stable identifier for the resource forever, at the price of invisibility: a bug report containing a URL carries no version information at all, and a cache will mix two representations of one URL unless you set Vary: Accept.

Date versioning (/2026-03-01/orders) is the same mechanics as URI versioning with a different argument: it says "this is a snapshot of the contract as of a date" and removes the argument about which number is newer. Its cost is that you will be asked to maintain a lot of snapshots. Choosing a strategy is choosing the failure you rehearse; the comparison below is the rehearsal.

// choosing a strategy is choosing which failure you can defend
const MATRIX = [
  {
    strategy: 'URI /vN',
    visible:     'yes - in every ticket, curl line and access log',
    cacheSafe:   'yes - versions are different URLs',
    failure:     'clients hardcode /v1/ and press on forever',
    testable:    'two base URLs, trivially testable by hand',
  },
  {
    strategy: 'Accept: application/vnd.acme.v2+json',
    visible:   'no - a pasted URL says nothing about version',
    cacheSafe: 'only with Vary: Accept - and Vary fragments the cache',
    failure:   'the wrong variant served to a client you cannot see',
    testable:  'requires curl -H, and a human to remember the header',
  },
  {
    strategy: 'Date /2026-03-01/',
    visible:   'yes - a snapshot of the contract as of a date',
    cacheSafe: 'yes',
    failure:   'the support burden of many snapshots',
    testable:  'as URI versioning, with date semantics',
  },
];
// "no versions at all" is the fourth row, and it is a different kind
// of answer: it does not manage the problem, it avoids it, with the
// discipline of chapter two of this chapter section as the price.

The support matrix is the real cost

Whatever the strategy, the cost is not the code — a version is a router and a set of DTOs. The cost is the support matrix: every version you keep is a contract you can no longer change, with a population of clients you increasingly cannot identify. The honest form of the matrix is written down, with a sunset date on every row except the current one, so that "we support everything forever" — which every team says and every team regrets — becomes a visible pile rather than an oral tradition.

Write it as data and derive the page from it: the version column, the client count, the sunset date, and the rule that governs the version. The moment the data says a version has zero active clients, the sunset date on the row is the only thing between you and deleting it.

{
  "versions": [
    {
      "url": "/v1",
      "released": "2024-01-15",
      "clients_last_90d": 412,
      "status": "maintained",
      "rules": "additive changes only",
      "sunset": "2027-01-15"
    },
    {
      "url": "/v2",
      "released": "2026-03-01",
      "clients_last_90d": 89,
      "status": "current",
      "rules": "additive changes only, plus approved deprecations",
      "sunset": null
    }
  ],
  "policy": {
    "minimum_notice": "12 months for a public API",
    "communication": "headers, changelog, direct email to active callers"
  }
}
// the dashboard that renders this file is the entire deprecation
// programme's evidence base: who is on what, and when it ends.

The realistic combination

Almost nobody picks a single row, and the combination that works is the one the sample below closes with: a /v1/ that is the stable surface everyone uses, a /v2/ built alongside, additive-only changes inside each version, and a dated migration between them when v2 is ready. The two numbers in the OpenAPI file matter as much as the two URLs: info.version (1.4.0) is the spec version and changes on any non-breaking change, while the URL version (v1, v2) is the contract version and changes only on a break. Teams that conflate the two end up shipping a v3 that contains a change that should have been additive.

The four strategies below are laid out with their exact consequences. Keep the combination in mind while reading: every row in that table is a piece you will reuse, and the art is assembling them rather than subscribing to one.

// A. URI VERSIONING        /v1/orders
//   + visible in logs, in the browser, in curl, in a support ticket
//   + trivially testable: two base URLs
//   + obvious to a human reading a URL
//   - the version is in every URL, which is repetitive
//   - encourages clients to hardcode a version (which is the point,
//     but they then forget to upgrade)
//   - cache keys naturally separate by version
//   MOST COMMON. A good default.

// B. HEADER VERSIONING     Accept: application/vnd.acme.v2+json
//   + the URL is stable forever, so links never rot
//   + a clean separation of concerns: URL identifies the resource,
//     the header identifies the representation
//   - invisible in a URL bar; harder to test by hand
//   - intermediaries may cache the wrong variant unless you set Vary
//   - a developer pasting a URL into Slack gets the default version and
//     reports "the bug is fixed" when it is not
//   USE WHEN: you have many clients you cannot contact, and you want
//   the URL to be a stable identifier for the resource.

// C. DATE VERSIONING       /2026-03-01/orders
//   + conveys "this is a snapshot of the contract as of a date"
//   + removes the argument about which is newer
//   - many versions quickly; support burden grows
//   USE FOR: public APIs with many independent implementors, where
//   you want to make the timeline explicit. Twilio and Stripe-adjacent
//   APIs do this.

// D. NO VERSIONS, EVOLUTIONARY DESIGN
//   + no version to choose, no support matrix
//   - you must be rigorous about what you add
//   - the only strategy that avoids the problem rather than managing it
//   - needs strong communication
//   Viable, and increasingly common, for APIs that are internally owned.

// A REALISTIC COMBINATION
//   /v1/ for the stable surface everyone uses
//   /v2/ for the next generation, built alongside
//   within v1: additive changes only, forever
//   when v2 is ready: announce, migrate clients, sunset v1 on a date

URI versioning

Two numbers with two different jobs

URI versioning ships two version numbers that teams regularly conflate, and the conflation is how you end up with a v3 that contains a change which should have been additive. The URL version — v1, v2 — is the contract version: it changes only on a breaking change, and every increment is a public event. The info.version in the OpenAPI file — 1.4.0, 1.5.0 — is the spec version: it changes on every release including the purely additive ones, and no client should ever depend on it. Bumping 1.4.0 to 1.5.0 breaks nobody. Changing v1 to v2 breaks everybody.

The practical rule is to push the decision up: a change is "breaking or additive" first, and the version number is decided by that, not by how big the diff feels. A new required request field is a contract break even when it is a two-line edit.

// two numbers, two jobs, one place each
const CONTRACT_VERSION = 'v1';   // changes only on a breaking change
const SPEC_VERSION = '1.4.0';    // changes on every release

server.use(`/${CONTRACT_VERSION}`, v1Router);
// openapi.yaml carries the same split:
//   info: { version: "1.4.0" }      <- the spec, additive releases
//   servers: [ { url: ".../v1" } ]  <- the contract, breaks only

// the standard template for a release:
//   1.4.0 -> 1.4.1   a bug fix, additive: no URL change
//   1.4.1 -> 1.5.0   a new endpoint or a new optional field
//   1.5.0 -> 2.0.0   anything in the breaking list of the last section
// and never both in one release: a v2 that ALSO fixes a typo in a v1
// field name is a migration nobody can reason about.

Carry the old version with an adapter, not a fork

The expensive part of URI versioning is not the new version, it is keeping the old one alive. The branching style — a full router, DTOs and tests per version — is the clearest to read and the most expensive to maintain, because every fix is implemented twice and every drift between the versions is a bug. The adapter style keeps one domain model and one handler, with a transform layer that projects the model into each version's shape. The complexity moves into the transforms, and the transforms are small; a fix lands once, and a test that both versions agree on the underlying facts runs in CI.

The rule that makes the adapter safe is that the fact is tested, not the shape: amount in v1 and total.value in v2 must agree, because they are the same money. A drift test is cheap insurance against the exact bug — a fix applied to one projection only — that otherwise reaches clients in the version you are trying to retire.

// one model, one handler, N projections
const toV1 = (o: Order) => ({ id: o.id, amount: o.totalMinor });
const toV2 = (o: Order) => ({
  id: o.id,
  total: { value: o.totalMinor, currency: o.currency },
});

app.get('/v1/orders/:id', async (req, res) => res.json(toV1(await loadOrder(req.params.id))));
app.get('/v2/orders/:id', async (req, res) => res.json(toV2(await loadOrder(req.params.id))));

// the drift test: both versions must agree on the money
test('v1 and v2 agree on the underlying value', async () => {
  const [a, b] = await Promise.all([
    fetch('/v1/orders/42').then((r) => r.json()),
    fetch('/v2/orders/42').then((r) => r.json()),
  ]);
  expect(a.amount).toBe(b.total.value);      // 2598 both ways
});
// the moment this test fails, one projection has drifted, and a client
// on the OTHER version has a bug you did not ship for them.

The version in the URL is a cache key for free

URI versioning has one unglamorous advantage that the header strategies never recover: the version is in the URL, so caches separate versions without any Vary work. A CDN can hold /v1/orders/42 and /v2/orders/42 side by side, expire them independently, and never risk serving a v1 response to a v2 client. Header versioning has to solve this by hand, and the solution fragments the cache (the previous section shows the trade).

The mechanics and the three strategies for carrying the old version — branch, adapter, and header negotiation over a single handler — are laid out in the sample next. The cache point is the tie-breaker when a public API is choosing between URI and header versioning.

// the mechanics
GET  /v1/orders
POST /v1/orders
GET  /v2/orders

// keep the version in ONE place in your code
const API_VERSION = 'v1';
app.use(`/${API_VERSION}`, v1Router);

// the OpenAPI definition, with servers per version
// openapi.yaml
openapi: 3.1.0
info:
  title: Orders API
  version: "1.4.0"        // the API's own version, not the URL version
servers:
  - url: https://api.example.com/v1
  - url: https://api.example.com/v2
paths:
  /orders:
    get:
      operationId: listOrders
      responses:
        "200": ...

// TWO SEPARATE NUMBERS, and teams conflate them
//   the URL version: v1, v2. a BREAKING contract change.
//   the info.version: 1.4.0. a non-breaking change: a new field, a
//                      new endpoint, a clarification.
// Changing 1.4.0 to 1.5.0 breaks nobody. Changing v1 to v2 breaks
// everyone. Getting this backwards is how you end up shipping v3
// containing a change that should have been additive.

// HANDLING THE OLD VERSION
// three strategies, all in use

// 1. BRANCH BY HANDLING. Every version has its own router, own DTOs,
//    own tests. Highest effort, clearest code, most bugs fixed twice.
app.use('/v1', v1Router);
app.use('/v2', v2Router);

// 2. ADAPTER. One internal domain model, one handler, and a transform
//    layer that converts to each version's shape. The transform is
//    where the complexity lives, and it is much smaller than branching.
const toV1 = (order: Order) => ({ id: order.id, amount: order.total, ... });
const toV2 = (order: Order) => ({ id: order.id, total: { value: order.total, currency: order.currency } });
app.get('/v1/orders/:id', async (req, res) => res.json(toV1(await load(req.params.id))));
app.get('/v2/orders/:id', async (req, res) => res.json(toV2(await load(req.params.id))));

// 3. HEADER NEGOTIATION over a single handler. Compact, and it pushes
//    complexity into a branch per field. Avoids URL clutter.

Header and media-type versioning

Negotiate, in order, with a deliberate default

Header versioning resolves the version from the request at serve time, and the resolution function is a small piece of protocol that deserves care. The media type carries the version (application/vnd.acme.orders.v2+json), q-values carry preference, and there is a fallback order: explicit version in the media type, then a dedicated header (Accept-Version), then the default. Every step of that chain is a decision: what the default is, what an unsupported version does, and whether the media type and the header may conflict.

Two traps earn most of the bug reports. A client sending an unknown version — v99 — will get it verbatim unless you define the response for an unknown version, and clients commonly do send stale or speculative versions. And a single Accept: */* from a browser, which carries no version at all, silently exercises the default path; if the default is not the newest version, the browser is one of your consumers too.

// negotiate in explicit order, with a deliberate fallback chain
function negotiateVersion(req, DEFAULT_VERSION = 1): number {
  // 1. the media type carries the version, q-values carry preference
  const accept = req.headers.get('accept') ?? '';
  const candidates = [];
  for (const part of accept.split(',')) {
    const [type, ...params] = part.trim().split(';');
    const qParam = params.find((p) => p.trim().startsWith('q='));
    const q = qParam ? Number(qParam.split('=')[1]) : 1;
    const m = /application\/vnd\.acme\.orders\.v(\d+)\+json/.exec(type.trim());
    if (m) candidates.push({ version: Number(m[1]), q });
  }
  candidates.sort((a, b) => b.q - a.q);          // highest q wins
  if (candidates.length) return candidates[0].version;

  // 2. the dedicated header is a plain floor
  const header = req.headers.get('accept-version');
  if (header) return Number(header) || DEFAULT_VERSION;

  // 3. the default is a PRODUCT decision, not an accident of ordering
  return DEFAULT_VERSION;
}
// decisions to make explicit:
//   - unknown version "v99" -> 404 with a list of supported versions
//   - media type says 3 but header says 2 -> reject, do not silently win

Vary is the whole game

Two different responses sharing one URL is a cache bug waiting for a first request: whatever variant lands in the cache is served to every later client that matches the key. The correction is Vary: Accept, which tells shared caches to include the Accept header in the cache key — and that correction has its own cost. A wildcard */* accept is a different entry from application/vnd.acme.v1+json, so the hit rate collapses for exactly the clients that do not send a tight Accept, which is most of them.

The pragmatic answer is to pay the cost only where it earns its keep: URI versioning for public endpoints, where the cache key is free, and media-type versioning where the URL must remain a stable identifier — a deep link, a long-lived resource — and the Vary fragmentation is a tax you accept because link stability is the requirement. Also set Cache-Control: private when the response is per-client, or the first-client's variant leaks to everyone.

// the two-response-one-URL problem, stated mechanically
//   curl -H "Accept: application/vnd.acme.v1+json" /orders  -> v1 JSON
//   curl -H "Accept: application/vnd.acme.v2+json" /orders  -> v2 JSON
//   a shared cache sees ONE url (/orders) unless you say otherwise.

res.set('Cache-Control', 'private, Vary: Accept');
// Vary: Accept keys the cache on the Accept header. the cost:
//   1. hit rate collapses if clients send "*/*" or nothing
//   2. private skips shared caches entirely - right for per-user data,
//      wrong for genuinely public responses you wanted cached

// the decision procedure used in practice:
//   - public, cacheable, many unknown clients  -> URI versioning
//   - URL must stay stable (links, citations)  -> media type + Vary
//   - per-user data, no shared cache anyway    -> either, Vary is free

Link the successor, and keep the citation

The Link header with rel="successor-version" is how a versioned client discovers the next one without you emailing them: a v1 response that links to the v2 media type turns a future migration into a follow-up request rather than an investigation. Emit it as soon as v2 exists, so that clients which log headers already have the pointer.

The practical objection below is the one that usually decides the argument: a bug report containing a URL — "it is broken" — carries no version with header versioning, and the reply "which version?" is a round trip. For internal and partner APIs you can train your way out of that. For public APIs the version travels with the citation only in the URL, which is why URI versioning wins that audience.

// media type, the cleanest form
GET /orders
Accept: application/vnd.acme.orders.v2+json

// or a dedicated header
GET /orders
Accept-Version: 2

// implementing it
function negotiateVersion(req: Request): 1 | 2 {
  const accept = req.headers.get('accept') ?? '';
  // explicit version in the media type wins
  const m = /v(\d+)\+json/.exec(accept);
  if (m) return Number(m[1]) as 1 | 2;
  // fall back to the header
  const h = req.headers.get('accept-version');
  if (h) return Number(h) as 1 | 2;
  return DEFAULT_VERSION;
}

// THE CACHE PROBLEM, which is the main reason this is not the default
// two different responses share a URL. unless you say otherwise, a
// cache will serve the wrong one.
Cache-Control: private, Vary: Accept
