// Vary: Accept fragments the cache per Accept header, which for a
// wildcard Accept means almost no cache hits. Consider it carefully.

// and links between versions, so clients can discover the new one
Link: <https://api.example.com/orders>; rel="successor-version"; type="application/vnd.acme.orders.v2+json"

// the practical objection that wins most arguments
//   a bug report containing a URL is useless with header versioning.
//   "it is broken" + "which version" is a round trip.
//   for internal and partner APIs this is fine. for public APIs,
//   URI versioning wins because the version travels with the citation.

Evolutionary design: no versions

The disciplines as a release gate

Evolutionary design does not mean "no process"; it means the process is a discipline of classification, enforced per change rather than once per version. Four rules carry the whole approach: never break a field, add new endpoints instead of re-purposing old ones, deprecate rather than remove, and observe everything. The first three are decisions; the fourth is infrastructure, and it is the one that makes the other three honest, because "deprecate, do not remove" is only affordable when you know who still depends on what.

Write the first three as a gate in the repository, reviewed like any other change. The point of a gate file is not bureaucracy — it is that "we decided this was additive" is stated at the time, in writing, rather than reconstructed after an incident from commit messages.

// the release gate for an unversioned API: a checked-in file, like a
// LICENSE or a CODEOWNERS, reviewed with every change
const gate = {
  // a change must be ... additive: no rename, no removal, no type change
  additive: true,
  // ... measured: the response-field logger covers the surface you touch
  measured: true,
  // ... communicated: changelog entry + a dated announcement
  communicated: true,
  // ... and the counter that is never allowed to move:
  removedFieldCount: 0,       // "deprecated, not removed" means 0 here
};

// the practical sequence for changing a field, in order:
//   1. add the new field, populate both   (additive, ships today)
//   2. announce, with the date for the old field's removal
//   3. watch adoption of the new one against the usage logger
//   4. only then consider removing the old field, on the announced date
// step 3 is where the gate earns its keep: without it you are guessing.

The usage log is the prerequisite, not a nice-to-have

You cannot deprecate what you cannot measure, and in an unversioned API the measurement is not aggregate traffic — it is which response fields which clients read, per endpoint. The honest proxy for "reads" is harder than it looks: server logs see what was sent, not what was consumed. The two proxies that work are the round-trip (a field the client echoes back in a future request was certainly read) and the codegen correlation (the fields a client's generated types declare are its reads). Both are approximations; the important thing is to have one, and to have it before you need it.

The output is one query: "who breaks on Tuesday when I change this field?" answered by "these four clients, with their traffic, and their last-contact dates".

// instrument the response path so a change is not a guess
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  const original = res.json.bind(res);
  res.json = (body) => {
    if (res.statusCode < 400 && req.headers['x-client-id']) {
      // which fields THIS client received on THIS endpoint
      reportFieldAccess(req.path, req.headers['x-client-id'], Object.keys(body ?? {}));
    }
    return original(body);
  };
  next();
});

// the question the dashboard answers, per candidate field change:
SELECT client_id, count(*) AS calls, max(last_seen) AS last_seen
  FROM field_access
 WHERE path = '/orders' AND field = 'amount'
 GROUP BY client_id
 ORDER BY calls DESC;
-- the four rows on top are the four people you email when the field
-- changes. the rows with last_seen over 90 days ago are the clients
-- you stop worrying about, because they will never move.

The honest cost is a permanent tax

What evolutionary design actually buys is time, and what it costs is a permanent tax. Every consumer sees a response twice the size it needs, because both fields are carried forever; every new field must be triaged against the "will this ever need to change" question; and the discipline never ends, because it is the discipline. For an internal API, owned by one team whose clients you can reach, that is a fine trade and the answer is genuinely "no versions, ever". For a public API with thousands of unknown consumers it is a permanent cost that eventually outweighs the savings, and the v2 you avoided arrives anyway, with interest.

The conditions that make it genuinely work, and the fuller form of the argument, are in the sample that follows: a single owning team, clients you can reach when something changes, and a field-level usage log proving who uses what. If any of the three is missing, you are not doing evolutionary design — you are storing up a migration.

// the approach: no v2, ever. Instead, be strict about what you change.

// 1. NEVER BREAK A FIELD. Additive changes only.
// 2. ADD NEW ENDPOINTS for new capability; do not re-purpose old ones.
// 3. DEPRECATE, DO NOT REMOVE. Mark it, stop using it yourself, and
//    leave it running.
// 4. BE OBSERVANT. You cannot deprecate what you cannot measure.

// making it work
//   - log which endpoints and fields real clients use, per client
//   - a usage dashboard is the prerequisite, not a nice-to-have
//   - when you need to change a field, add the new one, populate both,
//     announce, watch adoption of the new one, and only then consider
//     removing the old

// the honest cost
//   you carry both fields forever, and every consumer sees a response
//   twice the size it needs. For an internal API that is a fine trade.
//   For a public one with thousands of unknown consumers it is a
//   permanent tax, and eventually a v2 is the right answer anyway.

// when it genuinely works
//   - a single owning team
//   - clients you can reach when you change something
//   - a field-level usage log proving who uses what

Get your clients to upgrade

The migration document is an acceptance test

Shipping v2 alongside v1 is the non-negotiable first step — no client breaks on the day of the announcement, and the migration becomes an opt-in exercise rather than an outage. The second step is the migration document, and it belongs in the repository in a form that can be enforced: a field-by-field table mapping old to new to rationale. When the table is data instead of prose, it can drive a codemod, a shim SDK that speaks both versions, and an automated check that every old field the table names actually appears in v1 and every new field in v2. That is the difference between a version that gets adopted and one that is documented and ignored.

The rationale column is the part to argue about before you announce: money became a minor-unit integer plus a currency because a bare number is ambiguous, and if your clients cannot see why, the migration table is where they will learn it.

// the migration table, in a form CI can read and a shim can execute
const MIGRATION = [
  { oldField: 'amount',         newField: 'total.value',   why: 'a bare number is ambiguous; money needs a currency' },
  { oldField: 'currency',       newField: 'total.currency', why: 'moved beside the value it describes' },
  { oldField: 'created',        newField: 'createdAt',      why: 'naming consistent with the rest of v2' },
  { oldField: 'customer_name',  newField: 'customer.name',  why: 'object shape, not a flat concatenation' },
];

// a shim client that reads the OLD shape and presents the NEW one:
// the consumer's code is written once, against v2, today
export class OrdersV2Shim {
  async get(id) {
    const v1 = await fetch(`/v1/orders/${id}`).then((r) => r.json());
    return {
      id: v1.id,
      total: { value: v1.amount, currency: v1.currency },
      customer: { name: v1.customer_name },
      createdAt: v1.created,
    };
  }
}
// the shim is temporary infrastructure with a delete-by date on it:
// the goal is zero shims, because a shim is a second implementation
// of the same mapping, and mappings drift.

Per-client numbers decide who you contact

Adoption work is a set of three populations and you cannot tell them apart without per-client data. The clients already on v2 need to hear about it casually. The clients still on v1 with real traffic are the migration: email them with their own usage numbers, not a generic notice, and make the migration automatable so the answer to "will it take us a day?" is "there is a codemod". The clients on v1 with no calls in ninety days will never move — they have already de-integrated — and spending a migration sprint on them is how adoption plans miss their own dates.

The query below, run before you announce, is the entire evidence base for "who breaks on Tuesday", and it is worth building long before you need it.

-- three populations, three plans. run this before announcing.
SELECT client_id,
       min(api_version) AS oldest_seen,
       max(api_version) AS newest_seen,
       count(*)         AS calls_90d,
       max(ts)          AS last_seen
  FROM request_log
 WHERE ts > now() - interval '90 days'
 GROUP BY client_id
 ORDER BY calls_90d DESC;

-- decided from the result:
--   newest_seen = 2 and calls_90d > 0     -> tell them what changed
--   newest_seen = 1 and calls_90d > 0     -> email with THEIR numbers
--   newest_seen = 1 and calls_90d = 0     -> already gone, stop spending
-- the middle row is a finite, addressable list. the last row is not.

Give them a reason, and set a date you will honour

None of the machinery moves a client who does not want to move, so the plan needs an incentive: the new version is faster, cheaper, or enables something the old one cannot do at all. A shim plus an incentive is a migration. A deprecation header alone is a request. And the last two steps of the plan — set a date, and honour it — are the ones that compound: an open-ended deprecation is a permanent fork you are paying to maintain, and a sunset date that passes with nothing happening teaches every client that your deprecations are advisory, which is the most expensive lesson an API team can give.

The fuller sequence — ship alongside, document precisely, provide code, measure, make the old version the slow one, set the date — is the list the sample below walks through, and the item that looks like a trick ("make the old version the slow one") is the one to keep: a deprecation that also degrades performance is a message that you want them gone, and clients read that message faster than any changelog.

// announcing a new version without breaking anyone

// 1. SHIP v2 ALONGSIDE v1. Both work from day one. No client breaks.
// 2. DOCUMENT the migration precisely: a field-by-field table.
//    old field -> new field -> why it changed
//    GET /v1/orders
//    { "amount": 5000 }
//    GET /v2/orders
//    { "total": { "value": 5000, "currency": "GBP" } }
//    money became a minor-unit integer plus a currency, because a bare
//    number is ambiguous
// 3. PROVIDE CODE. A migration guide plus a codemod, or a shim SDK
//    that speaks both. This is the difference between a version that
//    gets adopted and one that does not.
// 4. MEASURE. Per-client version usage. Know who has not moved, and
//    who has not used the API in 90 days (they will never move; stop
//    worrying about them).
// 5. MAKE THE OLD VERSION THE SLOW ONE. Deprecate, but keep it
//    reliable. A deprecation that also degrades performance is a
//    message that you want them gone, and it invites a migration
//    sprint to something else.
// 6. SET A DATE. And honour it. An open-ended deprecation is a
//    permanent fork you are paying to maintain, and clients know it.

// what accelerates adoption
//   - the new version is faster, or cheaper, or enables something
//     the old one cannot do at all. Give them a reason.
//   - the migration is automatable
//   - you contact the top users directly, with their own usage data
//   - the changelog is specific and dated, not a marketing page

Deprecation as a process

The headers are the mechanical half

Two headers carry the whole machine-readable message, and the difference between them is the difference between "plan" and "there is a date on the calendar". Deprecation announces that the endpoint is already deprecated, and its value is the announcement date. Sunset states when it will stop working, and its value is the date clients must plan against. Add a Link with rel="successor-version" pointing at the replacement, and emit all three on every response from the endpoint — not on the first one, and not only on errors, because the client that needs them is the one calling happily in a loop.

The response body should say so too, for the clients that log bodies and for the operators reading logs years later. A _deprecated marker beside the data, or a X-Deprecated style field, costs one key and turns a support ticket into a diagnosis.

// emitted on EVERY response from the deprecated surface
const SUNSET = new Date('2026-06-14T00:00:00Z');

app.use('/v1/orders', (req, res, next) => {
  res.set({
    // announcement date: "this is deprecated, effective now"
    Deprecation: 'Sat, 14 Mar 2026 00:00:00 GMT',
    // the contract date: "it stops here"
    Sunset: SUNSET.toUTCString(),
    Link: '<https://api.example.com/docs/v2/orders>; rel="successor-version"',
  });
  next();
});

// and in the body, for the logs:
// { "id": "ord_8f2a", "amount": 5000, "_deprecated": true }
// a client that logs bodies now has a marker in the exact place it
// looks, which is worth more than the header nobody parses.

A calendar with four phases, and the one everyone skips

A deprecation is a programme with dates, not a header you switch on. Announcement with nothing changing; stop investing in the endpoint; contact the stragglers with their own numbers; and then actually sunset on the announced date. The phase that gets skipped is "stop investing", and skipping it is what destroys the credibility of the date: if you keep shipping new features to a deprecated endpoint, the clients who read your changelog can see that the Sunset header is aspiration rather than contract, and your own internal services are under no pressure to move.

Put the calendar in the repository as data and let tooling read it. "Are we on schedule?" becomes a query instead of a recollection, and the phase dates become reviewable like any other change.

// the deprecation calendar, as data the tooling can check
const DEPRECATIONS = [
  {
    endpoint: 'GET /v1/orders',
    announced:                '2026-03-14',   // headers go live, nothing breaks
    phase2_stopInvesting:     '2026-04-14',   // no features, no non-security fixes
    phase3_contactStragglers: '2026-05-14',   // direct email, their own usage data
    sunset:                   '2026-06-14',   // 410 Gone, with a migration hint
    aftercare:                '2026-07-14',   // 410 becomes 404
    successor:                'GET /v2/orders',
  },
];

// a check that runs in CI so the skip cannot happen quietly:
//   - no commit touching the deprecated route after phase2_stopInvesting
//     unless it is labelled security
//   - the changelog has an entry for each phase that has started
//   - the successor endpoint is documented and live before phase 3

Honouring the date is the whole product

The process only works if the last step is real. A deprecation whose Sunset date arrives with the endpoint still serving 200s teaches every client, including your own teams, that your deprecation headers are advisory. That lesson is expensive to unlearn: the next deprecation you announce will be read as a suggestion and will miss its date too, and you will have built a support matrix that only grows.

The phases in the sample below are the full programme — announce, stop investing, contact the stragglers, sunset — and the honest difficulty is that a date you cannot commit to is worse than no date at all. The per-client usage log from the previous section is what makes the commitment possible: without it, "who breaks in June?" is an investigation, and investigations are how dates slip.

// a deprecation is a PROGRAMME, not a header

// PHASE 1 - announce, nothing changes
//   add the headers, publish the date, tell the top consumers directly
//   your documentation and API reference list it as deprecated
Deprecation: Sat, 14 Mar 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Sunset: Sat, 14 Jun 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: <https://api.example.com/docs/v2/orders>; rel="successor-version"
Sunset: true

//   the response body should also say so, for clients that log bodies
{ "id": "ord_8f2a", "amount": 5000, "_deprecated": true }

// PHASE 2 - stop investing
//   no new features, no bug fixes that are not security fixes
//   your own services and internal tools stop using it
//   this is the step everyone skips, and it is the one that makes the
//   sunset credible

// PHASE 3 - contact stragglers
//   per-client usage says exactly who is still on the old version.
//   email them with THEIR OWN numbers, not a generic notice.

// PHASE 4 - the sunset date arrives
//   and then you must actually do it, or the next deprecation you
//   announce will be believed by nobody.

THE HONEST DIFFICULTY
//   a deprecation you do not honour teaches every client that your
   deprecations are advisory. That is expensive to undo. If you cannot
   commit to the date, do not set one.

Sunsetting and the end

410 Gone is the status a retired endpoint deserves

The choice of status at sunset decides what clients and caches do next. 410 Gone is permanent and non-cacheable-by-default for the client's purposes, and it means the specific, actionable thing: this resource existed, it was removed on a date, and here is where it went. A 404 means "no such resource, possibly a typo" and sends an integrator into a search for the right path — exactly the wrong message for an endpoint you deliberately retired. Put the sunset date, the successor URL and the documentation link in the body, because the client reading it is a developer five minutes before a deadline.

The one nuance is that a sunset response is worth keeping for about a month, then dropping to a plain 404: a client that was mid-migration when the date passed gets one more useful month, and after that the endpoint is genuinely gone and should look gone.

// 410: permanent, specific, and pointing at the replacement
app.all('/v1/orders{*rest}', (req, res) => {
  res.status(410).json({
    error: {
      code: 'endpoint_sunset',
      message: 'GET /v1/orders was removed on 14 June 2026.',
      sunsetDate: '2026-06-14',
      successor: 'https://api.example.com/v2/orders',
      docs: 'https://api.example.com/docs/v2/orders',
    },
  });
});

// after the aftercare window, make it look truly absent:
//   res.status(404)
// keeping a 410 forever means keeping the endpoint's dead shape in
// your code, and the point of sunsetting is to stop paying for it.

Redirects are for renames, not for retirements

A redirect is the right tool when the resources live on unchanged and only their address moved: you renamed a path, or moved a tenant to a new hostname. It is the wrong tool for a retired contract, because a 307 from v1 into v2 means v1 never actually ended — the migration cost is quietly paid by your servers on every request, forever, and the client never learns which version it is depending on. The distinction is worth stating explicitly in the deprecation plan, because "we will just redirect" is the most common way a sunset becomes a permanent alias.

When you do redirect, use 307 (or 308 for a permanently moved resource), because they preserve the method and body: a PATCH stays a PATCH. 301 and 302 are permitted to rewrite the method to GET, and clients frequently do not follow redirects for non-GET methods at all — test the client before promising it a migration path.

// 307 keeps the VERB and the body, which is what a renamed
// resource's clients actually need
app.get('/v1/orders/:id', (req, res) => {
  res.redirect(307, `https://api.example.com/v2/orders/${req.params.id}`);
});

// what to check before you promise a redirect as a migration path:
//   1. does the client follow a redirect on PATCH/PUT at all?
//   2. does it re-send the body, or send an empty one?
//   3. does it treat 307 as an error? (some HTTP clients do)
//   4. does the redirect preserve query strings you rely on?
// "we'll redirect for six months" is a plan until it meets a client
// that does not follow non-GET redirects. then it is a rename that
// silently stopped working for half your integrations.

The communication plan is the actual work

The technical end of a sunset is a status code. The work is the calendar around it: announced with headers six months out, a direct email to every remaining caller at three months with their own usage numbers attached, a final warning and a named human contact at one month, 410 on the day, 404 a month later. Each step has one job — to make the affected client able to act — and the step that makes them able is the one with their data in it, not the generic notice.

The thing almost nobody builds, and the thing that makes the whole calendar cheap, is the ability to answer "who is affected?" as a query rather than an investigation. The plan below is worth reading as a specification for that query: every date in it is a date on which somebody has to be contacted, and if you cannot produce the list of who, the date will slip.

// what "sunset" actually means, and the options

// OPTION A - 410 Gone. The correct status for a retired endpoint.
GET /v1/orders
410 Gone
{
  "error": {
    "code": "endpoint_sunset",
    "message": "This endpoint was removed on 14 June 2026.",
    "docs": "https://api.example.com/docs/v2/orders"
  }
}
// 410 is permanent and non-cacheable by default, so clients keep
// showing the right message instead of a cached 200

// OPTION B - redirect. Use for a renamed resource.
GET /v1/orders/8f2a
307 Temporary Redirect
Location: https://api.example.com/v2/orders/8f2a
// 307 preserves the method, so a PATCH redirects as a PATCH. Note that
// clients often do not follow redirects for non-GET, so test it

// OPTION C - a stub that returns 410 and a migration hint, for 30 days
//   after sunset. Cheap insurance for clients that were mid-integration.

// THE COMMUNICATION PLAN, which is the actual work
//   6 months out   announced, headers live
//   3 months out   direct email to every remaining caller, with their
//                  own usage numbers
//   1 month out    final warning, and a named contact
//   day 0          410, with a clear message
//   month +1       404, for anyone who somehow missed it

// THE THING NOBODY DOES
//   make it easy to find out you are affected. Per-client usage makes
//   "who breaks on Tuesday" a query rather than an investigation, and
//   that is worth building long before you need it.

Exercises 7

  1. Diff your API's OpenAPI file against a previous version and classify every change as breaking, additive or clarification. Argue each classification. This exercise finds real breaking changes you shipped by accident.
  2. Build a field-level usage logger: which clients read which response fields, per endpoint. Then make one change to a field's type and know exactly who breaks.
  3. Build v2 of one endpoint alongside v1 using the adapter pattern, with a transform layer. Write a test asserting both versions return the same underlying data in their respective shapes.
  4. Write a migration guide for your own field change: old shape, new shape, rationale, and a codemod or shim. Ask a colleague to follow it without asking you questions.
  5. Run a full deprecation cycle on one endpoint, with the headers, the 410, and the communication steps, and record how long each step actually took against the plan.

8 · Webhooks and Event Delivery

When to push rather than pollThe webhook contractVerifying the signatureDelivery, retries and orderingEvent naming and versioningDesigning the consumer sideTesting webhooks locallyWhen to use a queue instead

A webhook is your API calling someone else's, and it inverts every reliability problem you have spent this guide fixing. This chapter is about the contract, the signature, and what a consumer is obliged to do.

When to push rather than poll

A cursor poll is cheap, consistent and yours to control

Polling is not a primitive fallback; done properly it is a small amount of protocol with excellent properties. The client sends the cursor it last saw, the server returns everything after it in a bounded page, and when there is nothing new the response is 204 with no body. The client controls when it reads, so there is no delivery state to own, no retry scheduler, no per-consumer endpoint to keep alive. The consistency argument is the one that matters most: the client asks, so what it sees is what existed at ask time, and there is no window in which an event was produced but not delivered.

The costs are the ones the intuition names: one request per interval forever, mostly empty; latency up to the interval; and no way to distinguish an inactive client from an active one. For three consumers and an event an hour, that is the entire correct answer.

// a cheap, correct poll endpoint: cursor in, bounded page out
app.get('/v1/events', async (req, res) => {
  const since = req.query.since ?? req.headers['if-none-match'];
  const rows = await db.events.after(since, 100);   // ALWAYS bounded

  // nothing new: 204 with no body is the cheapest possible answer
  if (!rows.length) return res.status(204).end();

  const cursor = rows.at(-1).cursor;                // monotonic, opaque
  res.set('ETag', cursor).json({ events: rows, cursor: rows.at(-1).cursor });
});
// the client's whole loop:
//   while (true) { const r = await get(`/v1/events?since=${cursor}`);
//                  if (r.status !== 204) { for (const e of r.events) handle(e); cursor = r.cursor; }
//                  await sleep(intervalMs); }
// one request per interval, mostly empty. that is the entire bill.

Pushing inverts the reliability model

A webhook takes the delivery problem away from the client's control and puts it in your process. You now own a delivery record per event per endpoint, a retry schedule with a long tail, a deduplication id, an ordering strategy, a redelivery API and a dashboard that answers "what happened to my event". None of that exists when the client polls, and it is not incidental work — it is the chapter you are reading. The counter-argument is latency and waste: a webhook delivers in milliseconds and scales with events rather than with pollers, which is decisive when there are five hundred consumers and five hundred events a second.

The second cost is harder to see: your consumers must write servers. Many integrations that can poll successfully cannot correctly verify a signature, acknowledge in time, and tolerate duplicates, and the support burden of teaching them lands on you.

// the moment you push, all of this becomes yours - note what is absent
// when the client polls instead
type Delivery = {
  id: string;             // dlv_... : the consumer's dedupe key
  eventId: string;
  endpointId: string;
  attempt: number;        // 1..8 across ~31 hours
  status: 'pending' | 'succeeded' | 'failed';
  nextAttemptAt: Date;
  lastError?: string;     // refused, timeout, 500, DNS failure
};

async function deliver(d: Delivery, endpoint: Endpoint) {
  const body = JSON.stringify(await loadEvent(d.eventId));      // byte-exact
  const timestamp = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
  const res = await fetch(endpoint.url, {
    method: 'POST',
    headers: {
      'Content-Type': 'application/json',
      'X-Acme-Event': await eventType(d.eventId),
      'X-Acme-Delivery': d.id,
      'X-Acme-Signature': sign(body, timestamp, endpoint.secret),
    },
    body,
    signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5_000),       // 5s, or it counts as failed
  });
  await recordAttempt(d, res.status);          // the log the dashboard reads
}
// every line above is a thing you must run, monitor and debug. that is
// the trade: latency and waste on one side, delivery ownership on the
// other. neither is free, and the volume of change decides.

The deciding variable is the rate of change

The decision is not about the number of consumers, it is about the volume of change. Three consumers and one event an hour is a poll, and building webhooks for it is building a distributed system to deliver mail. Five hundred consumers and five hundred events a second is a webhook, and polling it would be a denial of service aimed at yourself. The useful intermediate question is the latency budget: if the consumer can wait up to a minute, poll; if the consumer must know within a second — a payment, a deploy, an alert — push.

There is also a hybrid that earns its place more often than either pure form: poll on a cursor for correctness, and push only the notification that says "there is something to poll". The push payload is a nudge, the poll is authoritative, and a lost notification costs latency rather than data. The two forms below are the pure cases; keep the hybrid in mind as the default for anything where a missed event is expensive.

// POLLING: the client asks
GET /v1/events?since=2026-03-14T09:00:00Z
// + trivial for you: no state, no delivery, no retries
// + always consistent: the client controls when it reads
// - the client pays: N requests per interval, forever, mostly empty
// - latency: up to the poll interval
// - you cannot tell an inactive client from an active one
// GOOD FOR: few consumers, infrequent changes, and anything where
// correctness matters more than latency.

// WEBHOOKS: you push
POST https://client.example.com/hooks/acme
// + near-zero latency
// + no wasted requests
// + scales with events, not with pollers
// - you now own delivery: retries, ordering, duplication, expiry
// - the client must expose a public HTTPS endpoint
// - clients write servers, which many will get wrong
// GOOD FOR: many consumers, frequent change, and events that matter
//           when they happen (payments, deploys, alerts).

// THE DECISION
//   the volume of change matters more than the count of consumers.
//     3 clients, 1 event an hour: poll. 500 clients, 500 events a
//     second: webhook, and you need the infrastructure in this chapter.
//   if the consumer can wait up to a minute: poll.
//   if the consumer must know within a second: webhook.

The webhook contract

The envelope, and why it is not the resource

A webhook body is an event, not the object it describes, and keeping that distinction turns out to matter. The envelope carries the identity of the event (id), its type, the API version whose shape the payload follows, the creation time, and a data.object that is a snapshot at that moment — not a live reference. A consumer that treats the payload as current state will be wrong: by the time it processes the message the object may have changed twice more, which is why the correct move is always to acknowledge, then re-fetch the authoritative object if the event type requires the latest state.

The envelope is also what makes the event self-describing. Without type and api_version in the body, a consumer that logs only the body has a payload it cannot interpret, and a replay six months later is undecidable. Include both, always, even when a header carries the same value.

# the sender in another language, to show that the envelope and the
# signature are protocol, not a library
import json, hmac, hashlib, time, urllib.request

def send(endpoint_url: str, secret: bytes, event: dict) -> int:
    # byte-exact serialisation: the consumer verifies the RAW bytes,
    # so signing and sending must produce identical output
    body = json.dumps(event, separators=(",", ":")).encode()
    ts = int(time.time())
    mac = hmac.new(secret, f"{ts}.".encode() + body, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()

    req = urllib.request.Request(
        endpoint_url,
        data=body,
        headers={
            "Content-Type": "application/json",
            "X-Acme-Event": event["type"],
            "X-Acme-Delivery": event["id"],
            "X-Acme-Signature": f"t={ts},v1={mac}",
        },
        method="POST",
    )
    with urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=5) as res:
        return res.status
# json.dumps defaults to ", " and ": " separators. that single space is
# a signature failure on the consumer side, and it is invisible in a
# diff because both sides "parse the same JSON".

What you owe the consumer, and what you can test

The consumer's obligations are half of the contract and you should document them as though they were your own: HTTPS, publicly reachable, 2xx within five seconds, acknowledge before processing, tolerate duplicates, out-of-order delivery and unknown event types, and verify the signature against the raw body. You cannot test the consumer's code, but you can test your own side of the contract — that the body you sign is the body you send, that a 500 is retried and a 204 stops the loop, and that the delivery log records the status a human will read at 2am.

Those two properties are the ones that fail silently in production. A sender whose serialisation differs between the signing and the sending call produces a 100% signature failure that looks like the consumer's bug; a retry loop with no terminal condition turns a consumer's outage into a sustained attack from your own fleet.

import { createHmac } from 'node:crypto';

// test the SENDER against a local stub, not the consumer
it('signs the exact bytes it sends', async () => {
  const captured = stub.captureNext();
  await send(endpoint.url, secret, event);

  const expected = createHmac('sha256', secret)
    .update(`${captured.headers['x-acme-timestamp']}.${captured.rawBody}`)
    .digest('hex');
  expect(captured.headers['x-acme-signature']).toContain(expected);
});

it('retries a 500 and stops on a 204', async () => {
  stub.respondOnce(500).respondOnce(204);
  const d = await deliver(event);
  expect(stub.attempts).toBe(2);
  expect(d.status).toBe('succeeded');
});

it('does not retry a 400', async () => {
  stub.respondOnce(400);
  const d = await deliver(event);
  expect(stub.attempts).toBe(1);        // malformed is malformed forever
  expect(d.status).toBe('failed');
});

The four things a consumer needs

Every webhook, whatever its shape, must answer four questions from the payload alone: what happened (the type and data.object), when (the created time, and a header timestamp for the signature), which delivery this is (a unique delivery id, which is the deduplication key), and proof that it came from you (the signature). A payload missing any of the four forces the consumer to guess, and guessing is what produces duplicate side effects and support tickets.

The request below is a complete contract in one example: the envelope, the headers, and the endpoint requirements that a consumer is expected to meet. Read it as the specification a client integrates against, because that is precisely what it becomes.

// what you send
POST /hooks/acme HTTP/1.1
Host: client.example.com
Content-Type: application/json
User-Agent: Acme-Webhooks/1.0
X-Acme-Event: order.paid
X-Acme-Delivery: dlv_8f2a91c4e7
X-Acme-Timestamp: 1773587200
X-Acme-Signature: t=1773587200,v1=5257a869e7ec...
X-Acme-Signature-256: t=1773587200,v1=5257a869e7ec...

{
  "id": "evt_1a2b3c4d",
  "type": "order.paid",
  "api_version": "2026-03-01",
  "created": "2026-03-14T09:31:22.104Z",
  "livemode": true,
  "data": {
    "object": {
      "id": "ord_8f2a91",
      "status": "paid",
      "total": { "value": 2598, "currency": "GBP" },
      "paidAt": "2026-03-14T09:31:20.000Z"
    }
  }
}

// the four things a consumer needs
//   1. WHAT happened      -> data.object, and the type
//   2. WHEN               -> created, and X-Acme-Timestamp
//   3. WHICH delivery     -> X-Acme-Delivery, for deduplication
//   4. PROOF it is you    -> X-Acme-Signature

// the endpoint requirements, which you must document
//   - must be HTTPS, publicly reachable
//   - must return 2xx within 5 seconds, or the delivery counts as failed
//   - must NOT process synchronously: acknowledge, enqueue, return
//   - must tolerate duplicates (it will get them)
//   - must tolerate out-of-order delivery (it will get them)
//   - must tolerate events it does not recognise (it will get them)
//   - must verify the signature (see next section)
//   - must respond with 2xx, not 200 exactly. 204 is fine.

Verifying the signature

Without a signature, a webhook endpoint is an unauthenticated write into your system that anyone on the internet can trigger. This is not optional.

The timestamp is inside the signed material

The header format is t=<unix>,v1=<hmac-sha256-hex>, and the material signed is the string <timestamp>.<raw body>. Putting the timestamp on the wire but outside the signature would make the signature reusable forever: an attacker who captured one delivery could replay it with a fresh timestamp. Putting it inside means a captured request is valid only inside the tolerance window, and the window is the whole replay defence — five minutes by convention, and it must be enforced against the server's clock, not the client's.

The verifier below is the code most integrators actually need. Note the shape of the header parsing: a header may legally carry more than one v1= value (which is how secret rotation works — see the next heading), so collecting candidates into a list is not pedantry, it is the difference between a verifier that survives rotation and one that breaks on rotation day.

# verify in Python: same protocol, same pitfalls, standard library only
import hmac, hashlib, time

def verify(raw_body: bytes, header: str, secret: bytes, tolerance: int = 300) -> bool:
    # a header may carry SEVERAL v1= values during a secret rotation,
    # so collect them all rather than overwriting in a dict
    parts = dict(p.split("=", 1) for p in header.split(",") if "=" in p)
    sent = [p.split("=", 1)[1] for p in header.split(",") if p.startswith("v1=")]
    try:
        ts = int(parts["t"])
    except (KeyError, ValueError):
        return False

    # 1. the tolerance window: a captured delivery cannot be replayed later
    if abs(time.time() - ts) > tolerance:
        return False

    # 2. recompute over the RAW bytes, never over parsed JSON
    expected = hmac.new(secret, f"{ts}.".encode() + raw_body, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()

    # 3. constant-time compare against each candidate
    return any(hmac.compare_digest(expected, s) for s in sent)
# hmac.compare_digest exists in every standard library precisely because
# `expected == s` leaks the shared prefix through comparison timing.

Rotate by signing twice, not by cutting over

A single shared secret cannot be rotated without a coordinated outage, and coordinated cutovers are the migration nobody schedules correctly. The standard trick is to sign once per accepted secret and concatenate the values into one header, letting the verifier accept if any candidate matches. Consumers holding the old secret verify; consumers who have already been given the new one verify too; nobody has to be synchronised with your deployment.

The step that is forgotten is the drain period. Your own retry queue is still re-sending requests signed with the old secret for up to the length of the retry schedule — around thirty-one hours with the usual exponential tail — so removing the old secret from the accept list before that window has drained produces failures on requests that you, not the consumer, are making.

// signer side: emit two v1 values during the overlap
import { createHmac } from 'node:crypto';

function signWithRotation(payload: string, ts: number, current: string, previous?: string) {
  const mac = (secret: string) =>
    createHmac('sha256', secret).update(`${ts}.${payload}`).digest('hex');

  const parts = [`t=${ts}`, `v1=${mac(current)}`];
  if (previous) parts.push(`v1=${mac(previous)}`);   // accept either
  return parts.join(',');
}

// the sequence, with the drain window made explicit
//   1. publish the new secret to consumers (they may or may not add it)
//   2. SIGN WITH BOTH for the length of the retry tail (~31 hours)
//   3. after the tail has drained, stop sending the old v1
//   4. revoke the old secret from the dashboard
// skipping step 2 breaks consumers that have not switched; skipping
// step 3 leaves a secret you cannot revoke, which is the same as never
// having rotated at all.

Verify the bytes you received

The single most common webhook integration bug is verifying against JSON.stringify(req.body) instead of the raw request body. The two are not byte-identical: key order can change, whitespace can change, unicode escaping can differ, and 1.0 can come back as 1. The result is a correct signature that fails verification on every request, which looks like a signing bug on your side and is actually a parsing bug on theirs.

Fix it once, in the framework: capture the raw body on the route, verify against it, and only then parse. The sample below contains the trap and the correct wiring side by side, and it is the shape to copy.

// the scheme, which is what Stripe popularised and is now the norm
//   t=<unix timestamp>,v1=<hmac-sha256 hex>
//   the signed payload is:  "<timestamp>.<raw request body>"
//   the timestamp is INSIDE the signed material, which is the point.

// your side, signing
import { createHmac } from 'node:crypto';

function sign(payload: string, timestamp: number, secret: string): string {
  const mac = createHmac('sha256', secret)
    .update(`${timestamp}.${payload}`)          // note the dot
    .digest('hex');
  return `t=${timestamp},v1=${mac}`;
}

// THE CONSUMER SIDE, which is the code most people need
import { createHmac, timingSafeEqual } from 'node:crypto';

function verify(rawBody: Buffer, header: string, secret: string, toleranceSec = 300): boolean {
  const parts = Object.fromEntries(
    header.split(',').map((p) => p.split('=') as [string, string]),
  );
  const t = Number(parts.t);
  const v1 = parts.v1;
  if (!t || !v1) return false;

  // 1. TOLERANCE. Reject anything outside the window, so a captured
  //    request cannot be replayed next week. 5 minutes is conventional.
  if (Math.abs(Date.now() / 1000 - t) > toleranceSec) return false;

  // 2. RECOMPUTE over the RAW body.
  const expected = createHmac('sha256', secret).update(`${t}.${rawBody}`).digest('hex');

  // 3. CONSTANT-TIME compare. A string === is vulnerable to timing
  //    attacks, and this is a security check.
  const a = Buffer.from(expected, 'utf8');
  const b = Buffer.from(v1, 'utf8');
  return a.length === b.length && timingSafeEqual(a, b);
}

// THE TRAP THAT BREAKS EVERYONE'S VERIFICATION
//   you must verify against the RAW body, not the parsed JSON.
//   JSON.stringify(req.body) is NOT byte-identical to what you received:
//     - key order can change
//     - whitespace can change
//     - unicode escaping can differ
//     - a number 1.0 can become 1
//   so a correct signature check fails on a correct request. This is
//   the single most common webhook integration bug.
app.post('/hooks/acme', express.raw({ type: 'application/json' }), (req, res) => {
  if (!verify(req.body, req.headers['x-acme-signature'], process.env.WEBHOOK_SECRET)) {
    return res.status(400).send('bad signature');
  }
  const event = JSON.parse(req.body.toString('utf8'));   // parse AFTER verifying
  res.status(204).end();                                 // ack fast
  queue.push(event);                                      // process after
});

// use constant-time comparison and check the length first, or
// timingSafeEqual throws on a length mismatch and becomes a 500 that
// an attacker can trigger deliberately.

Delivery, retries and ordering

The retry schedule is a commitment

At-least-once is the realistic guarantee, and the schedule is how you express it: the first attempt immediately, then thirty seconds, two minutes, ten minutes, an hour, six hours, a day, and then stop. Eight attempts spanning roughly thirty-one hours gives a consumer that is down for a maintenance window the chance to recover without you holding state forever, and it gives the consumer that is down for a week a clear terminal answer rather than an eventual flood. Publish the schedule — a consumer sizing its alerting needs to know how many attempts to expect and when the last one arrives.

Two details decide whether the schedule helps or hurts. The first attempt must be immediate, not jittered, so a healthy consumer sees no latency at all. And every subsequent retry must be jittered, because a thousand consumers that all fail on the same minute and all back off by exactly thirty seconds will all arrive together, and your retry fleet becomes the outage it was reacting to.

// the schedule as data, with the two rules that make it safe
const BACKOFF_SECONDS = [0, 30, 120, 600, 3_600, 21_600, 86_400, 86_400];

function nextAttemptAt(attempt: number, from = new Date()): Date | null {
  const delay = BACKOFF_SECONDS[attempt];
  if (delay === undefined) return null;      // 8 attempts, then give up

  // attempt 0 is immediate (a healthy consumer sees zero delay);
  // later attempts are fully jittered, so a mass failure does not
  // reconverge into a second mass failure
  const jitter = attempt === 0 ? 0 : Math.floor(Math.random() * delay);
  return new Date(from.getTime() + (delay + jitter) * 1_000);
}

// give up explicitly, and say so: after the last attempt the delivery
// is 'failed', it appears in the dashboard, and the consumer's only
// recourse is the redelivery API or a poll. retrying forever is a
// denial-of-service aimed at a consumer that has been down for a week.

Ordering is a version field, not a queue

You cannot guarantee ordering, and the reason is structural: event two can be delivered and succeed while event one is still on its third retry. The fix is not a queue per object, it is a monotonic version on the object carried in the event. The consumer compares the incoming version against the highest it has already applied and discards anything older. Two events arriving in the wrong order therefore converge on the newest state, because the stale one is an explicit no-op rather than an unhandled case.

Per-object ordering queues do work and they are the expensive answer: a queue and a worker per object, plus a strategy for the queue's own failure modes. The version guard costs one integer on the object and one comparison on the consumer, and it is the reason Stripe's webhooks are safe to process out of order. Prefer it, and reserve the per-object queue for the genuinely stateful cases where the events are not a projection of one versioned object.

// the stale-event guard, on the consumer
type Incoming = { type: string; data: { object: { id: string; version: number } } };

async function apply(event: Incoming) {
  const { id, version } = event.data.object;
  const current = await db.orders.findOne({ where: { id } });

  // already newer: this event is a straggler from a retry queue
  if (current && current.version >= version) return;

  await db.orders.update({
    where: { id },
    data: { version, status: project(event).status },
  });
}
// the producer's job: bump `version` on the object on every change, and
// put it in the event. without it, the consumer has no way to tell a
// straggler from a fresh change - and no amount of queueing fixes that.

Deliveries need a log, and a redelivery button

"The event was lost, send it again" is one of the most common support tickets an integration API receives, and it is either a two-second answer or an investigation depending entirely on whether you kept a delivery log. Record every attempt — delivery id, event, endpoint, URL, response status, duration, attempt number, error — and expose a redelivery endpoint that re-sends the stored payload byte-for-byte. A dashboard with the same data turns the ticket into a self-service action, and it also gives you the answer to "is the consumer's endpoint slow or is my fleet slow", which is otherwise a week of guessing.

Two properties of the log are worth designing in from the start: the stored payload must be the exact bytes that were signed, so a redelivery verifies the same way the original did; and the log must be queryable per endpoint, because the consumer's first question is always "how many of my deliveries failed this week".

// redelivery re-sends the STORED bytes, never a re-serialised event
app.post('/v1/webhook-deliveries/:id/redeliver', async (req, res) => {
  const original = await db.deliveries.findOne({ where: { id: req.params.id } });
  if (!original) return res.status(404).json({ error: { code: 'not_found' } });

  // re-queue the same bytes with the same signature material but a NEW
  // delivery id, so the consumer's dedupe key does not swallow it
  const redelivery = await db.deliveries.create({
    endpointId: original.endpointId,
    eventId: original.eventId,
    payload: original.payload,          // byte-identical to the original
    status: 'pending',
    attempt: 0,
  });
  res.status(202).json({ id: redelivery.id });
});
// re-serialising the event instead would change the bytes, change the
// signature, and make the redelivery unverifiable against the original
// log entry - which is exactly the thing the log exists to prove.

What counts as a failure decides the whole schedule

The classification of responses is what the schedule operates on, and getting it wrong turns retries into noise. Any non-2xx, a connection refusal, a DNS failure and a timeout past five or ten seconds are failures and deserve a retry. A 400 is not: a malformed payload is well-formed JSON that the consumer rejects, and it will be rejected identically forever, so retrying it eight times is eight times nothing. The two 4xx exceptions are 408 and 429, which are explicitly "try again later" and should be retried, honouring Retry-After where present.

The schedule, the failure rules, the ordering strategy and the deduplication key are laid out together in the sample below. Read the failure list as the specification for the state machine the first block implements.

// you cannot guarantee delivery. design for that.
//   at-least-once is the realistic guarantee. the consumer MUST be
//   idempotent, and you MUST be able to re-send.

// THE RETRY SCHEDULE
//   attempt 1   immediately
//   attempt 2   +  30 seconds
//   attempt 3   +  2 minutes
//   attempt 4   + 10 minutes
//   attempt 5   +  1 hour
//   attempt 6   +  6 hours
//   attempt 7   + 24 hours
//   then give up, and let the consumer poll or re-fetch
//   8 attempts over ~31 hours. exponential with a long tail.

// WHAT COUNTS AS FAILURE
//   non-2xx status                    -> failure, retry
//   connection refused or DNS failure -> failure, retry
//   timeout over 5 or 10 seconds      -> failure, retry
//   2xx                              -> success, stop
//   do NOT retry a 4xx other than 408 and 429: a 400 means your
//   payload is malformed and will be malformed forever

// ORDERING
//   you cannot guarantee it. two events for the same object can arrive
//   out of order, because event 2 may be delivered and succeed while
//   event 1 is still retrying.
//
//   THE FIX: put the version in the event.
//   "data.object.version": 7
// The consumer keeps the highest version it has seen and ignores
// anything older. This is how Stripe handles out-of-order webhooks,
// and it works because the object has a monotonic version.
//
//   the alternative, per-object ordering queues, serialises deliveries
//   for one object. It works and it costs a queue per object.

// DEDUPLICATION
//   X-Acme-Delivery is a unique id per delivery attempt. The consumer
//   stores it and skips a repeat. This is required, because retries
//   will deliver duplicates and your consumer's logic must be safe.

// DASHBOARD
//   log every attempt: id, event, url, status, duration, attempt number
//   expose a redelivery button in your dashboard
//   expose the same as an API: POST /v1/events/evt_1a2b/redeliver
//   "the event was lost, send it again" is a support ticket you can
//   resolve in seconds instead of one you cannot

Event naming and versioning

Name the fact that happened

An event type should name a fact in the past tense — order.paid, payment.failed, customer.subscription.deleted — so that a consumer can subscribe to the specific thing it cares about without parsing a payload. The competing convention, order_status_changed, pushes that work onto every consumer: each one must receive the event, compare the previous and current state it does not have, and decide whether it cares. A name that already states the fact removes the decision, which is the entire point of an event registry.

The second decision is granularity. data.updated with the payload deciding what changed is the worst option on the board, because every subscriber receives every update and filters on the body — the event bus becomes a broadcast with a hand-rolled routing layer in each consumer. One type per meaningful transition scales; one type per entity does not.

// object.past-tense, lowercase, dotted. the name is the subscription.
const EVENTS = {
  'order.created':                 'an order came into existence',
  'order.paid':                    'payment settled for an order',
  'order.cancelled':               'the customer or the system cancelled it',
  'customer.subscription.deleted': 'the subscription ended',
};

// the names to reject, and the cost of each:
//   order_status_changed  every consumer must diff two states it lacks
//   OrderPaid             case-sensitive, so a different string
//   order.paid.v2         a version folded into the name when the
//                         envelope already carries one
//   data.updated          a broadcast: everyone filters on the body

Version the envelope, not only the API

Events are a contract exactly like endpoints, and they break clients just as badly — but the failure is worse, because the event arrives asynchronously and the consumer may be three retries behind when the shape changed. Every envelope therefore carries an api_version: the shape of data.object, fixed at the moment the event was created. A consumer built against 2026-03-01 keeps receiving that shape even after you move the API forward, which is what makes an event ledger replayable months later.

Two strategies cover the practical cases. Keep names stable and version the envelope when changes are additive — the type registry does not churn, and old consumers simply ignore fields they do not know. Append .v2 to the type name only when the payload shape changes materially and silently upgrading the old subscribers would corrupt their data. The registry and the envelope below show the stable-names form.

// the envelope: versioned, replayable, and self-describing
type EventEnvelope = {
  id: string;                 // evt_... - unique, and stable across redeliveries
  type: string;               // order.paid - the fact
  api_version: string;        // 2026-03-01 - the SHAPE of data.object
  created: string;            // RFC 3339 with milliseconds
  livemode: boolean;          // sandbox vs production, never ambiguous
  data: { object: unknown };  // a snapshot, not a live reference
};

// per-endpoint subscriptions: a consumer receives only what it asked for
type WebhookEndpoint = {
  url: string;
  enabled_events: string[];   // exact types. no "order.*" prefixes:
                              // a wildcard silently enrols a consumer in
                              // every future event, which is a surprise
  description: string;
};
// the pair of these two type definitions is the whole event contract.
// if a consumer cannot replay a year-old event through these two, the
// envelope is missing something.

The registry is a product surface

Once events are contracted, the type registry becomes something consumers read and depend on: the list of types, the shape of each payload, and the version each was introduced at. Treat it as a paginated, versioned API of its own, and treat a new event type as a non-breaking change while a payload change to an existing type is breaking — the same classification as the previous chapter, applied to a different transport.

The sample below is the naming scheme, the versioning decision and the subscription API in one place, including the sandbox flag and the redelivery escape hatch. The two fields worth copying exactly are api_version in the envelope and enabled_events on the endpoint: omit the first and you cannot evolve the payload, omit the second and you deliver a firehose to every consumer.

// NAMING: object.event, past tense, lowercase dotted
order.created
order.paid
order.cancelled
payment.succeeded
payment.failed
customer.subscription.deleted

// "order.paid" is better than "order_status_changed" because it names
// the specific thing that happened. A consumer can subscribe to
// order.paid without parsing anything.

// VERSIONING EVENTS, which people forget
//   events are a contract, exactly like endpoints, and they break
//   clients just as badly
"api_version": "2026-03-01"    // in the envelope, always present

// strategy: the version is in the event NAME
order.paid.v1
order.paid.v2
// explicit, greppable, and the consumer subscribes to a specific one.
// use this when the payload shape changes materially.

// strategy: the version is in the envelope, names are stable
// use this when changes are additive. Less churn in the type registry.

// SUBCRIPTIONS, so consumers get only what they need
//   per endpoint, per set of event types
POST /v1/webhook-endpoints
{
  "url": "https://client.example.com/hooks/acme",
  "enabled_events": ["order.paid", "order.cancelled"],
  "description": "Production orders"
}

// also: a sandbox mode, so a consumer can integrate without real money
"livemode": true
// and a way to send a test event: POST /v1/webhook-endpoints/we_1/redeliver

Designing the consumer side

This is the code you will write most often, so it is worth getting right once.

Deduplicate with a constraint, not a read

The delivery id is the deduplication key, and the tempting implementation — read the deliveries table, and if the row is missing, process and then write it — races with itself. Two retries of the same delivery arrive within milliseconds, both reads return nothing, both process, and the consumer applies the effect twice. The database has the tool for this: a unique constraint on the delivery id, and an insert that returns zero rows when the id is already present. Then the check and the claim are one atomic operation and there is no window.

The deduplication row must also be durable across restarts. An in-memory set of seen ids is a dedupe that evaporates on deploy, which means a deployment in the middle of a retry storm replays every in-flight event. Redis can serve as the store, with a TTL longer than the retry window, but "longer than the retry window" has to be a number you actually looked up.

-- the check and the claim as ONE atomic statement
CREATE TABLE webhook_deliveries (
  id            text PRIMARY KEY,          -- the X-Acme-Delivery value
  event_id      text NOT NULL,
  processed_at  timestamptz
);

INSERT INTO webhook_deliveries (id, event_id)
VALUES ($1, $2)
ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING
RETURNING id;
-- zero rows returned == already seen: acknowledge and stop.
-- a read-then-insert has a window between the two; the constraint does
-- not, and no amount of application-level locking recreates that
-- guarantee as cheaply as the unique index does.

The effect and the marker share one transaction

Once the marker exists, the question becomes where it sits relative to the effect. Writing the marker first and processing after means a crash between them loses the event silently — the marker says processed, the database says otherwise, and nothing will ever retry it. Processing first and writing the marker after means a crash reprocesses, which is recoverable, and safe only if the processing is idempotent. The second order is the correct one, and the cleanest way to get it is to put both statements in one transaction so they cannot diverge at all.

Note the guard inside the update — version < incoming.version — which combines the stale-event protection from the previous section with the idempotent write. A duplicate delivery that gets this far changes nothing because the row is already at that version or newer.

// effect + marker in one transaction: both or neither
await db.transaction(async (tx) => {
  const inserted = await tx.query(
    `INSERT INTO webhook_deliveries (id, event_id)
     VALUES ($1, $2) ON CONFLICT (id) DO NOTHING RETURNING id`,
    [event.id, event.id],
  );
  if (inserted.rowCount === 0) return;           // already processed: stop

  await tx.query(
    `UPDATE orders
        SET status = 'paid', paid_at = $2, version = $3
      WHERE id = $1 AND version < $3`,            // stale guard, idempotent
    [event.data.object.id, event.created, event.data.object.version],
  );
});
// a crash rolls back BOTH rows, so the retry reprocesses the whole
// thing. the alternative - commit the marker, crash before the effect -
// is an event that is permanently lost with no alarm raised.

The minimum viable consumer, in order

The consumer has five steps and their order is not negotiable: verify against the raw body, claim the delivery id atomically, acknowledge immediately, process from a queue, and record the delivery last. The acknowledgement must come before the work, because the work takes longer than the five-second budget and a synchronous consumer is retried while it is still working. The processing must be version-aware and idempotent, because it will be retried. And the recording must come after success, because a crash between them must reprocess rather than skip.

The sample below is that whole consumer — verification, dedupe, immediate ack, queued processing with the stale guard, and the local testing notes that follow it. It is the code to copy, and the two lines worth memorising are res.status(200).end() before queue.push(event), and the version comparison in the handler.

// THE MINIMUM VIABLE CONSUMER
app.post('/hooks/acme', express.raw({ type: 'application/json' }), async (req, res) => {
  // 1. verify against the RAW body
  if (!verify(req.body, req.headers['x-acme-signature'], SECRET)) {
    return res.status(400).end();
  }
  const event = JSON.parse(req.body.toString('utf8'));

  // 2. deduplicate on the delivery id
  if (await db.webhookDeliveries.findUnique({ where: { id: event.id } })) {
    return res.status(200).end();          // already processed
  }

  // 3. acknowledge IMMEDIATELY. Processing takes longer than 5 seconds
  //    and you will be retried, forever, while you work.
  res.status(200).end();
  await queue.push(event);
});

// 4. process from the queue, idempotently and version-aware
async function handle(event: Event) {
  switch (event.type) {
    case 'order.paid': {
      // ignore stale events: an older version of the same object
      const current = await db.orders.findUnique({ where: { id: event.data.object.id } });
      if (current && current.version >= event.data.object.version) {
        return;                                // out of order, already newer
      }
      // the operation itself must also be idempotent
      await db.orders.update({
        where: { id: event.data.object.id },
        data: { status: 'paid', paidAt: new Date(event.created) },
      });
      break;
    }
  }
  // record the delivery LAST, after success, so a crash mid-way
  // causes a reprocess rather than a silent skip
  await db.webhookDeliveries.create({ data: { id: event.id } });
}

// WHY THE ORDER MATTERS
//   record-then-process: a crash after recording loses the event
//   process-then-record: a crash after processing reprocesses, which is
//                        safe ONLY if processing is idempotent
//   choose the second, and make processing idempotent. Repcessing is
//   recoverable; losing an event is not.

// LOCAL TESTING
//   - a tunnel: ngrok http 3000, or cloudflared tunnel --url localhost:3000
//   - a request bin: webhook.site, requestbin, or a small echo server
//   - your own redelivery API against a real endpoint
//   - replay from your dashboard: the most useful test tool there is
//   - a CLI: curl -X POST localhost:3000/hooks/acme -d @fixture.json
//     with a fixed signature you generate yourself, so the test is
//     deterministic rather than dependent on a live event

Testing webhooks locally

A tunnel, a request bin, and one curl line

Development needs a public HTTPS URL pointing at the laptop, and the two tools that do it without configuration are ngrok http 3000 and cloudflared tunnel --url http://localhost:3000. Register the tunnel hostname as a webhook endpoint in your own dashboard and let the real sender produce real events; that is the only test that exercises the signature, the headers and the retry machinery as they actually run. For the cases where you cannot reach the sender — a production API you do not administer — a request bin does the same job in reverse: it gives you a URL and shows you the exact bytes that were delivered.

The manual replay is the fastest inner loop, and the one detail that matters is --data-binary. curl -d strips newlines and joins the input, so the bytes you sign and the bytes you send differ, and a correct verifier rejects a correct request. The signature is over bytes, so the transport must preserve them.

# a public URL for a laptop, two ways
ngrok http 3000
cloudflared tunnel --url http://localhost:3000

# the manual replay, against your own handler
TS=$(date +%s)
MAC=$(printf '%s.%s' "$TS" "$(cat tests/fixtures/order-paid.json)" \
      | openssl dgst -sha256 -hmac "$WEBHOOK_SECRET" -hex | awk '{print $NF}')

curl -X POST http://localhost:3000/hooks/acme \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -H "X-Acme-Signature: t=$TS,v1=$MAC" \
  --data-binary @tests/fixtures/order-paid.json

# --data-binary, never -d: -d strips newlines, so the bytes you signed
# and the bytes you sent differ, and the verifier is right to reject it.

A fixture and a test beat a live event

A live event is convenient and non-deterministic: it arrives when it arrives, its body changes as the producer's schema evolves, and it proves nothing about the cases that matter. A committed fixture plus a signature you generate in the test is deterministic, runs in CI with no network, and can express the hostile cases deliberately. That is what makes it worth the file.

The one trap in the test code is the client: many HTTP test clients re-serialise an object body, so .send(obj) sends bytes that differ from your fixture and the signature check fails for the wrong reason. Send the buffer, and the test tests what you meant it to test.

import request from 'supertest';
import { createHmac } from 'node:crypto';
import { readFileSync } from 'node:fs';
import { app } from '../src/app';

const FIXTURE = readFileSync('tests/fixtures/order-paid.json');  // a Buffer
const SECRET = 'whsec_test';

function signatureFor(body: Buffer, ts: number) {
  const mac = createHmac('sha256', SECRET).update(`${ts}.${body}`).digest('hex');
  return `t=${ts},v1=${mac}`;
}

it('accepts a valid event and applies the effect exactly once', async () => {
  const ts = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
  const res = await request(app)
    .post('/hooks/acme')
    .set('Content-Type', 'application/json')
    .set('X-Acme-Signature', signatureFor(FIXTURE, ts))
    .send(FIXTURE);                        // Buffer, not an object

  expect(res.status).toBe(200);
  const order = await db.orders.findOne({ where: { id: 'ord_8f2a91' } });
  expect(order).toMatchObject({ status: 'paid' });

  // and the same delivery again must be a no-op, not a second effect
  const again = await request(app)
    .post('/hooks/acme')
    .set('Content-Type', 'application/json')
    .set('X-Acme-Signature', signatureFor(FIXTURE, ts))
    .send(FIXTURE);
  expect(again.status).toBe(200);
  expect(await db.orders.count()).toBe(1);   // one effect, two deliveries
});

The cases that catch real bugs

The valuable tests are the ones that assert a request fails for the right reason. A signature computed over the re-parsed body must be rejected — if it is accepted, the verifier is reading the wrong bytes and you have a latent bug that will surface the first time key order changes. A valid body signed with a different secret must be rejected. A timestamp exactly at the tolerance boundary must have a defined behaviour, whichever way you choose it. An empty body, a huge body and a body that is not JSON must all be handled without a 500, because a 500 invites the sender to retry.

The tests shown below are that set, written as executable assertions on the verifier, and they are the ones worth keeping when everything else is trimmed. The list that follows them adds the cases specific to delivery: the same delivery id twice, an out-of-order pair, and a redelivery after a consumer was down.

// a deterministic local test, without depending on a live event
// 1. a fixture that is byte-exact
//    tests/fixtures/order-paid.json
// 2. a signature you computed once and committed
export const FIXTURE_SIGNATURE = 't=1773587200,v1=5257a869e7eceb3bf1425a951ac8a9e07cd0b6a3...';

// 3. the test
import { readFileSync } from 'node:fs';

it('accepts a correctly signed event', () => {
  const body = readFileSync('tests/fixtures/order-paid.json');   // Buffer
  const sig = 'x-acme-signature';
  expect(verify(body, sig, TEST_SECRET)).toBe(true);
});

it('rejects a tampered body', () => {
  const body = readFileSync('tests/fixtures/order-paid.json');
  const tampered = Buffer.from(body.toString().replace('"GBP"', '"USD"'));
  expect(verify(tampered, sig, TEST_SECRET)).toBe(false);
});

it('rejects a replayed timestamp', () => {
  const old = Date.now() / 1000 - 3600;
  const sig = signWith(TEST_SECRET, old, body);
  expect(verify(body, sig, TEST_SECRET, 300)).toBe(false);   // outside tolerance
});

// the tests that catch real bugs
//   - signature over the parsed body must FAIL. if it passes, your
//     verification is reading the wrong bytes and you have a latent bug
//   - a signature with a valid body but a swapped secret must fail
//   - a timestamp exactly at the tolerance boundary: define the behaviour
//   - an empty body, a huge body, and a body that is not JSON
//   - the same delivery id twice: the second must be ignored

When to use a queue instead

Inside your own boundary, a queue beats HTTP

When both ends are yours, HTTP is the wrong transport for an event. A queue gives you three properties you would otherwise build by hand: ordering within a key, so events for one order are processed in sequence; offset tracking, so a consumer that restarts resumes rather than replays or skips; and a replayable log, so a new service can read history from the beginning. With a Kafka key of order.id, ordering per order is guaranteed by the partition, and the version guard from the previous section becomes unnecessary for consumers inside the boundary — an ordering guarantee is cheaper to rely on than one to reconstruct.

Fan-out comes free with a consumer group: each group receives every message, partitions split the work within a group, and adding a consumer adds capacity. That is the same shape as "many webhook endpoints" with none of the delivery machinery — no per-endpoint retry state, no signature verification to get wrong, no five-second budget to enforce.

// publish once; the broker does the fan-out
await producer.send({
  topic: 'orders',
  messages: [{ key: order.id, value: JSON.stringify(event) }],
});
// key = order.id routes every event for one order to the same
// partition, so ordering is guaranteed PER ORDER with no version field

// N independent consumer groups, each seeing every message
await subscribe('orders', groupId: 'billing',   { fromBeginning: false });
await subscribe('orders', groupId: 'analytics', { fromBeginning: false });
await subscribe('orders', groupId: 'search',    { fromBeginning: true });

// what each group gets, and what it must still do:
//   billing    newest events only, in order per order id
//   analytics  newest events only, batch-oriented, order irrelevant
//   search     the whole log from the start, to rebuild an index
// a group is an independent position in the log, which is the property
// that makes replay and backfill single-line operations.

The transactional outbox

Writing to your database and publishing to a broker cannot be one atomic operation, so the naive sequence — commit the order, then publish — has a window in which the process dies and the event is never published. The fix is to make the intent to publish part of the same transaction: insert the event into an outbox table alongside the state change, then have a relay read unsent rows and publish them. The state change and the intent are now atomic; the relay is a separate, restartable process whose worst failure is publishing a row twice.

That residual failure is why consumers stay idempotent even inside your boundary. The outbox removes the lost-event case; it cannot remove the duplicate-event case, because the crash can happen after the publish and before the marker. What it does remove is the class of bug nobody can debug: an order that is paid in the database and unknown to every downstream system.

// state change + publish intent, in ONE transaction
await db.transaction(async (tx) => {
  await tx.query(`UPDATE orders SET status = 'paid' WHERE id = $1`, [id]);
  await tx.query(
    `INSERT INTO outbox (id, topic, payload)
     VALUES (gen_random_uuid(), 'orders', $1)`,
    [JSON.stringify(event)],
  );
});

// the relay: read unsent, publish, mark. restartable at any point.
const batch = await db.query(
  `SELECT * FROM outbox WHERE sent_at IS NULL ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 100`);
for (const row of batch.rows) {
  await producer.send({ topic: row.topic, messages: [{ value: row.payload }] });
  await db.query(`UPDATE outbox SET sent_at = now() WHERE id = $1`, [row.id]);
}
// ordering note: a single relay gives you publish order, and a
// partitioned relay (one worker per order id) gives you per-key order.
// mark AFTER a successful send; after a failed send, leave the row and
// let the next pass retry it - which is at-least-once, as promised.

Reserve HTTP for the boundary

The rule that keeps this tractable is that the choice follows the organisational boundary, not the technical difficulty. Inside the boundary, a queue: durable, replayable, ordered within a partition, and no verification code to get wrong. Across the boundary, a webhook: a partner, a customer or a third-party integrator cannot join your Kafka cluster, and the moment you expose HTTP this whole chapter applies in full.

The anti-pattern is reaching for webhooks for internal service-to-service calls, which buys you a signature scheme, per-endpoint retry state, a delivery log and a dead-letter queue to maintain — for traffic that could have been a message on a topic. The comparison below is the decision procedure, and the boundary it draws is the one to keep.

// a webhook is HTTP. a queue is better for internal delivery.

// USE WEBHOOKS WHEN
//   - the consumer is outside your control
//   - the event crosses an organisational boundary
//   - the consumer wants to receive it, not poll for it

// USE A QUEUE WHEN
//   - both ends are yours
//   - you need guaranteed delivery with no HTTP involved
//   - you need fan-out to many internal services
//   - the event volume is high and bursty
//   - you need to replay from a log

// the internal equivalent
//   publish to Kafka, RabbitMQ, SQS, NATS or similar
//   services subscribe to topics, with consumer groups for fan-out
//   you get: durable, replayable, ordered within a partition,
//             and no signature-verification code to get wrong

// WHY YOU STILL NEED WEBHOOKS EVEN WITH A QUEUE
//   the boundary case: a customer, a partner, a third-party integrator.
//   Those consumers cannot join your Kafka cluster, so they need HTTP.
//   And the moment you expose HTTP, this chapter applies in full.

// THE ANTI-PATTERN
//   using webhooks for internal service-to-service calls.
//   You now have signature verification, retry state, delivery logs and
//   a dead-letter queue to maintain, for traffic that could have been a
//   function call or a message. Reserve HTTP for the boundary.

Exercises 8

  1. Implement signing and verification with a timestamp tolerance and a constant-time compare. Write tests for: valid, tampered body, wrong secret, stale timestamp, and a valid signature over the re-parsed body (which must fail).
  2. Build a webhook endpoint that verifies against the raw body, acknowledges in under 100 ms, and processes from a queue. Measure the acknowledgement time to prove the budget is met.
  3. Build the sender: a retry schedule with exponential backoff, a delivery log, and a redelivery endpoint. Write a test that a consumer failing three times then succeeding receives exactly four deliveries.
  4. Handle out-of-order delivery on the consumer using an object version field. Write a test that delivers v3 before v2 and asserts the final state reflects v3.
  5. Write the consumer idempotency layer with delivery-id deduplication, and prove it by processing the same event 100 times and asserting exactly one effect.

9 · GraphQL

When GraphQL is the right answerThe schema is the contractNullability, and the one rule that mattersResolvers and the N+1 problemQuery depth, cost and complexity limitsMutations, and why they are not CRUDConnection-based paginationSubscriptions and realtimePersisted queries and allowlistsFederation, and when to stop

GraphQL moves flexibility from the server to the client and charges for it with query cost, caching, and a security surface that is genuinely harder to defend. It is the right tool for a small number of first-party clients and the wrong tool for a public API.

When GraphQL is the right answer

The decision is two axes and a gate

GraphQL moves flexibility from the server to the client, and the price is paid in query cost, HTTP caching and a security surface that is genuinely harder to defend. That trade is worth taking in a narrow region: a small number of consumers you control whose data needs genuinely differ. It is a bad trade when the consumer count is high and the query variety is low, because then you have bought flexibility nobody is using and given up the caching and status-code transparency that HTTP was giving you for free.

The third consideration is not an axis but a gate: are you willing to expose the schema to consumers you cannot change? A public GraphQL schema is a permanent, inspectable commitment — every field a client uses becomes a field you can never remove — and any client can write any query, so the cost-limit work in this chapter is not optional. If the answer is no, GraphQL is a private protocol between your own clients, which is a perfectly good thing to build and not what most advocacy assumes.

// the decision, as two axes and a hard gate
const axes = {
  consumerCount: 'how many independent implementations must you support?',
  queryVariety:  'how different are their data needs, realistically?',
};
