const gate = 'will the schema be exposed to consumers you cannot change?';

const verdicts = [
  // consumers         query variety   verdict
  ['few, all yours',  'high',         'GraphQL fits well'],
  ['few, all yours',  'low',          'two REST endpoints and a field selector'],
  ['many, unknown',   'any',          'REST or gRPC. a public GraphQL schema is a liability'],
  ['one internal UI', 'high',         'GraphQL fits, and is the most common success case'],
];

// the deciding question is never "is GraphQL better" - it is "do I have
// few consumers with genuinely different needs, and control of all of
// them". if either half is missing, the flexibility is not exercised
// and the costs are still paid.

What REST does instead, with the same flexibility

The problem GraphQL solves for a small client set is usually overstated, because the two things clients actually want — fewer fields, and one round trip — have REST answers that keep HTTP's properties intact. A sparse fieldset parameter lets the mobile client ask for six fields and the desktop client ask for forty, from one endpoint, with the response cacheable under a distinct key. A backend-for-frontend endpoint, one per screen, gives the client exactly the shape it needs in one call without moving the query language to the client at all.

Keeping the response cacheable is the part GraphQL gives up and the part a field selector keeps: an ETag plus a conditional request makes the poll cheap, and a CDN can serve it. That is not a small advantage for read-heavy screens.

# the REST answer: a field selector, cacheable, one endpoint
GET /v1/orders?fields=id,status,total&page[size]=20 HTTP/1.1
If-None-Match: "orders-v7"
# 304 Not Modified when unchanged: the poll costs headers only, and a
# CDN absorbs the traffic. GraphQL's POST-carrying-anything shape
# cannot express this without persisted queries and an app-side cache.

# or one endpoint per screen - the backend-for-frontend pattern
GET /v1/screens/order-list?page[size]=20
# exactly the fields the screen renders, no client-side stitching, and
# the response is a normal REST contract with a normal cache key.
# the cost is one endpoint per screen, owned by the team that owns the
# screen - which is usually the team that owns the GraphQL schema too.

The case that works most often is an internal UI

The unglamorous, high-value use of GraphQL is a back-office tool with a complex and changing screen, built by the same team that owns the API. One query fetches exactly the screen's data; the front end stops waterfalling through six REST endpoints; the schema becomes a collaboration tool between the two halves of one team as much as a transport. None of the hard parts apply: there is one consumer, no public schema, no unknown-client cost attack, and the query shapes are known because you wrote them.

The cases to avoid are the mirror image: a public or partner API with independent implementors, deep nested access that anyone can query, and a mobile bandwidth argument that a persisted query plus a field selector also solves. The commentary below draws both sides out; read the "decision, stated plainly" paragraph as the summary this section is built on.

// THE CASE FOR
//   - ONE consumer, or a few, that you control. The flexibility is
//     worth the cost because you can change the queries yourself.
//   - The clients genuinely need different fields. A mobile app needs
//     six fields; a desktop app needs forty. REST solves this with
//     two endpoints and a field selector; GraphQL with one schema.
//   - The domain is a GRAPH and clients traverse it. Connections
//     between entities, navigated by the client, is genuinely
//     awkward in REST and natural in GraphQL.
//   - The client team and server team are the same organisation and
//     move together.

// THE CASE AGAINST
//   - A PUBLIC API, or one with many independent implementors. You
//     are committing to the whole schema forever, and any client can
//     write any query, so a schema mistake is your problem forever.
//   - Deep, nested, public access. Query cost is the whole security
//     story and it is hard to get right.
//   - HTTP caching. GET with a query string is cacheable in principle
//     and useless in practice because every query string is distinct.
//   - You already have REST clients and workers who know it. Migrating
//     is not free and the benefit is not automatic.
//   - Mobile bandwidth is NOT a GraphQL problem in a way that REST
//     does not also have. A persisted query and a field selector solve
//     the same thing.

// THE DECISION, stated plainly
   GraphQL wins when the CONSUMER COUNT is small and the QUERY
   VARIETY is high. It loses when the consumer count is high and the
   query variety is low.
//   "We have a mobile app, a web app and a partner API" is a
   borderline case and the deciding question is whether you are
//   willing to expose a public schema. If not, GraphQL is a private
//   protocol between your own clients, which is a fine thing to do
//   and not the thing people mean when they advocate it.

// GRAPHQL FOR AN INTERNAL UI, the case that works most often
   A back-office tool with a complex, changing screen, built by the
   same team that owns the API. One query fetches exactly the screen's
   data, the front end stops waterfalling, and the schema is a
   collaboration tool as much as a transport. This is a legitimate,
//   unglamorous, high-value use of GraphQL and it does not involve
//   any of the hard parts.

The schema is the contract

SDL is the entire public API

In GraphQL the schema is not documentation about the contract, it is the contract: the server validates every query it receives against the SDL before a resolver runs. A field that is not in the schema cannot be asked for, a misspelled field is a validation error, not an undefined at runtime, and a non-null argument cannot be omitted. That is a genuinely different property from REST, where an endpoint accepts whatever the body says and the shape is enforced by your validation code if you wrote any.

The same property is why the schema is also the client's documentation: a well-typed tool can autocomplete the query, verify it at build time, and generate the response types. The schema deserves review discipline exactly like a REST contract does — a field added casually is added forever, and a field removed is a breaking change for every client that still queries it.

# the client's query is validated against the schema before execution
query OrderScreen($id: ID!) {
  order(id: $id) {
    id
    status
    total { value currency }      # Money is an object, not two loose fields
    customer { name }
  }
}
# what the type system enforces on its own:
#   - "costumer"            -> a validation error, not undefined
#   - "total { value }"     -> fine; sparse fields are the point
#   - "order(no_argument)"  -> rejected: ID! is a required argument
#   - "status: SOME_UNKNOWN"-> rejected by the enum on the way in
# every one of those is a compile-time/validation-time error because
# the schema exists. that is the contract working.

Schema-first, with codegen as the wiring

The schema earns its keep through code generation: one committed schema.graphql, and every client — and the server's own types — generated from it. A field rename becomes a compile error at every usage site instead of a runtime undefined that surfaces in one client and not another. This is the single biggest reason GraphQL works in a team and the single biggest reason ad-hoc GraphQL — schema in the server file, types hand-written in the client — devolves into the undefined-field bug it was supposed to remove.

Codegen is also how a client becomes stale-safe: the generated types for a current client are pinned to a schema version, so when the schema evolves, the mismatch is a compile-time fact somewhere and a deliberate integration task somewhere else — not a field that silently comes back null.

# graphql-codegen.yml: ONE schema, TWO compile sites
schema: src/schema.graphql          # committed; the source of truth
documents: src/**/*.graphql         # the queries the server actually sends

generates:
  # server-side types flow from the same file as the clients
  src/generated/types.ts:
    plugins:
      - typescript
  # the web client compiles against the identical schema
  ../web/src/generated/graphql.ts:
    plugins:
      - typescript
      - typescript-operations
      - typed-document-node

# the CI rule that makes it stick: a pull request touching
# schema.graphql must regenerate and commit the outputs. a generated
# file that is out of date fails the build, so "schema drift" cannot
# exist silently in either direction.

Evolution rules are stricter than REST's

The schema's strength — that every client can validate against it — is also its constraint: there is no version negotiation in GraphQL. Adding a field is safe, unless it lands on a non-null field (the next section's subject). Removing a field is breaking, always, and a client that still queries it fails at runtime with a 200 and an error in the payload — which ordinary HTTP monitoring does not see. Renaming is remove-plus-add, and the two must ship in one deployment, because two deployments mean a window in which the old name is gone and the new one has not arrived. Changing a type, Int to Float, is breaking. Making a nullable field non-null is the most common accidental break, and it is covered in detail next.

The design rules in the sample below — domain names, object types over scalar bags, an id: ID! on every type, enums for closed sets, minor-unit money, DateTime over string, userErrors on every mutation — are in the order to apply them, because each one is a decision that is expensive to reverse.

// A SCHEMA, in SDL. This is the entire public API.
scalar DateTime
scalar URL

interface Node { id: ID! }

type Order implements Node {
  id: ID!
  status: OrderStatus!
  total: Money!
  createdAt: DateTime!
  customer: Customer!          # not null: a real invariant
  shippingAddress: Address     # nullable: not every order has one
  lines(first: Int = 20, after: String): OrderLineConnection!
}

type OrderLineConnection {
  edges: [OrderLineEdge!]!
  pageInfo: PageInfo!
  totalCount: Int              # expensive; make it opt-in
}

type OrderLineEdge { cursor: String!  node: OrderLine! }

type Money {
  value: Int!                  # minor units. always.
  currency: Currency!
  formatted: String!           # display convenience only
}

enum OrderStatus { PENDING PAID SHIPPED CANCELLED }

input OrderFilter {
  status: [OrderStatus!]
  createdAt: DateRange
  total: AmountRange
  search: String
}

type Mutation {
  createOrder(input: CreateOrderInput!): CreateOrderPayload!
}

input CreateOrderInput {
  lines: [OrderLineInput!]!
  shippingAddress: AddressInput
  idempotencyKey: String!      # the same contract as REST (ch5)
}

type CreateOrderPayload {
  order: Order
  userErrors: [UserError!]!    # the validation channel
}

type UserError { field: [String!]!, message: String!, code: String! }

type Query {
  node(id: ID!): Node
  orders(first: Int, after: String, filter: OrderFilter, sort: [OrderSort!]): OrderConnection!
  order(id: ID!): Order
}

// THE DESIGN RULES, in the order you should apply them
//   1. Name things after the domain, not the storage. OrderLine, not
//      order_items_row.
//   2. Expose an OBJECT type, not a bag of scalars. Money instead of
//      amountCents + currency. The client then cannot get it wrong.
//   3. Every type has an id: ID! and implements Node. It costs one
//      field and it buys you a universal fetch.
//   4. Enums, not strings, for any closed set. The schema is the
//      validation.
//   5. Money is minor units plus a currency. A bare number is a bug
//      waiting for a currency with different precision.
//   6. Dates are DateTime, not String. Strings will be local time in
//      someone's system and it will be a bug.
//   7. userErrors on every mutation payload. It is the GraphQL idiom
//      for the REST 422 body, and it belongs on every mutation even
//      when it is usually empty.

// SCHEMA FIRST, AND WHY
   schema.graphql committed to the repository, codegen from it into
   types for every client, and the server types generated from the
   same file. Then a field rename is a compile error everywhere rather
   than a runtime undefined. This is the single biggest reason GraphQL
   works and ad-hoc GraphQL does not.

// EVOLUTION RULES, and they are stricter than REST
   ADD a field: safe, unless it is on a non-null field (see below)
//   REMOVE a field: BREAKING. There is no version negotiation in
//      GraphQL, and clients send arbitrary queries, so a removed field
//      fails at runtime, per client, with a 200.
//   RENAME: remove + add, in the same deployment. Never in two steps.
//   CHANGE A TYPE: breaking. Int -> Float is breaking.
//   MAKE NULLABLE NON-NULL: BREAKING, and it is the most common
//      accidental break. See below.

Nullability, and the one rule that matters

Null propagates to the nearest nullable parent

GraphQL's nullability rule is the one thing to get right, and the surprising half is the propagation: if a field declared non-null resolves to null, the error travels up the selection until it reaches a field that is allowed to be null, and everything above stays null. A single failed customer on one order can therefore null out the entire data object — one failed field, zero data, indistinguishable from the service being down. That is why "add a !" is not a strengthening of a contract, it is a blast radius decision.

The response below shows both stops of the propagation: the nullable root absorbs the null and returns a partial response, and the comment shows what happens when the root is made non-null too — which is where the whole-query collapse comes from.

type Query { order(id: ID!): Order }        # NULLABLE root
type Order { id: ID!  customer: Customer! }  # customer: never null

# query { order(id: 1) { id customer { name } } } when customer fails:
{
  "data": { "order": null },
  "errors": [
    {
      "message": "Cannot return null for non-nullable field Order.customer",
      "path": ["order", "customer"],
      "extensions": { "code": "INTERNAL" }
    }
  ]
}
# the null from customer! bubbled up THROUGH the Order object until it
# hit the nullable root and stopped there. data.order is null and the
# whole selection is gone, because a partially-null Order cannot be
# serialised.
# make Query.order non-null (`order(id: ID!): Order!`) and the SAME
# failure collapses all of data to null. one field, zero data, and a
# client that cannot tell "field failed" from "api is down".

Decide by invariant, and write it in the schema

The practice that makes nullability predictable is deciding it by invariant at design time. Non-null means "if this is null, the system is broken and the client cannot proceed meaningfully": an order always has a customer, every id is present. Nullable means "this might legitimately not exist": shipping address on an order that has not shipped. The mistake every team makes once is starting nullable and tightening field by field over six months — every tightening is a breaking change, because a client that relied on the field being present gets null where it expected a value, and its code does not fail to parse, it fails to render.

Changing a field between the two is therefore a major event: it cannot be detected by a client's type checker, only by its users. Annotate the intent in the schema, because the annotation is what survives a handover where the reasoning does not.

# decide nullability in the schema, with the invariant in a comment
type Order {
  id: ID!                  # always present - it IS the identity
  customer: Customer!      # invariant: an order always has a customer
  shippingAddress: Address # legitimately absent until the order ships
  trackingNumber: String   # absent until shipped, then always present
  lines(first: Int = 20): OrderLineConnection!   # the LIST always exists
}

type OrderLineConnection {
  edges: [OrderLineEdge!]!   # list non-null; elements non-null
}
# [OrderLineEdge!]!  = "the list is never null, and no element is null"
# [OrderLineEdge]   = "an element may be missing from the MIDDLE of the
#                      list" - which no real domain has. type it last.
# the rule of thumb that covers most fields:
#   invariant?     -> Non-null (and a null here is a bug worth crashing)
#   legitimately absent? -> Nullable (ship an explicit null or omit it)
#   can it fail?   -> ALWAYS nullable. never error: Error!. see below.

Errors are always nullable

The one unconditional rule is that a field whose resolution can fail must not be non-null. An error: Error! is a promise that the error can never be null — and the moment a lookup fails, the propagation rules turn that promise into the collapse described above. The response-shaped answer is error: Error (nullable), or better, the mutation userErrors pattern that keeps failures in the payload and out of the resolver graph, which chapter 9's mutation section develops.

The fuller form of the argument — including the list-shaped corner of the rule, edges: [Edge!]! versus [T], and the "mistake that bites every team once" — is in the sample that follows. The heading is not a metaphor: this is the section where a bad decision produces the least-discussed production incident in GraphQL.

// THE NULLABILITY RULE, and it is the one thing to get right
//
//   GraphQL's spec says: if a field's type is non-null and it resolves
//   to null, the error PROPAGATES UP to the nearest nullable parent.
//   The whole query can fail. This is not a detail; it is the
//   difference between a partial response and an error.

type Order {
  customer: Customer!        # if Customer is missing, this field
                             # AND its parent are null, and the parent
                             # of THAT is null... eventually you can
                             # null out the entire `data` object
}

// THE PRACTICE THAT MAKES IT PREDICTABLE
//   Non-null means "if this is null, the system is broken and the
//   client cannot meaningfully proceed." Nullable means "this might
//   legitimately not exist."
//   1. Is it a genuine invariant? Non-null. An order always has a
//      customer. A violation is a bug worth crashing on.
//   2. Is it legitimately absent? Nullable. shippingAddress on an
//      order that has not shipped.
//   3. Errors are ALWAYS nullable. Never `error: Error!`. A field
//      that can legitimately be "it failed" must not be non-null, or
//      its presence destroys the response.
//   4. Lists: `edges: [Edge!]!` is right. The list exists, and an
//      edge exists for every element. Use `[T]` for a list where an
//      element might be null, which is almost never what you want.

// THE MISTAKE THAT BITES EVERY TEAM ONCE
   You start with everything nullable. Then you tighten field by field
   over six months. Every tightening is a breaking change, because a
   client whose query requests a now-nullable field still parses fine
   — the schema declares `String` and they asked for `String` — but a
   client whose query relies on a field now BEING present will get a
   null where it expected a value. Their code does not fail to parse.
//   It fails to render.
//   So: decide nullability deliberately at design time, and treat a
//   change to it as a major event. Annotate the schema:
//     id: ID!             # always present
//     shippingAddress: Address   # absent until the order ships

Resolvers and the N+1 problem

A resolver runs once per field per parent

A GraphQL resolver is executed for every requested field, once per parent object. A query for twenty orders, each selecting a customer, a total and a line list, does not cost four database queries — it does not even cost one per field. It costs 1 for the orders page, 20 for the customers (one per order parent), 20 for the totals and 20 for the line lists: 61 queries for one request, and 301 at a hundred orders. HTTP-level monitoring cannot see it, because the request completes with a single 200; only the database's slow-query log can.

The shape of the fix is visible in the SQL before the code: twenty single-row WHERE id = $1 lookups are the same information as one WHERE id = ANY($1). The loader machinery exists to make your resolvers say the second thing without you writing the batching by hand on every field.

-- the query: 20 orders, each with customer + total + lines
-- naive execution, one query per resolver invocation:
SELECT * FROM orders LIMIT 20;                      -- 1
SELECT * FROM customers WHERE id = $1;              -- x 20
SELECT * FROM order_totals WHERE order_id = $1;     -- x 20
SELECT * FROM order_lines WHERE order_id = $1;      -- x 20
-- total: 61 queries for ONE GraphQL request; 301 at 100 orders

-- with loaders, the 20 single-row lookups become one each:
SELECT * FROM orders LIMIT 20;
SELECT * FROM customers WHERE id = ANY($1);         -- 20 ids in one call
SELECT * FROM order_totals WHERE order_id = ANY($1);
SELECT * FROM order_lines WHERE order_id = ANY($1);
-- 4 queries. that is the whole of the N+1 fix in three lines of SQL.

DataLoader: batch and cache within one request

The standard fix is DataLoader, which does two things inside one request: it batches all the load() calls on the same key set into one loadMany() invocation, and it caches by key so a customer referenced by two orders is fetched once. The contract that trips everyone is positional: loadMany must return one value per input key, in input order, with null for a missing key — a filtering map instead of a positional list is a silent data-corruption bug.

The second contract is lifecycle: a loader must be created per request and attached to the request context. A module-level loader caches across requests, which is a cross-tenant data leak, a memory leak, and the source of the least-debuggable incidents in GraphQL production systems.

import DataLoader from 'dataloader';

const customerLoader = new DataLoader(async (ids: readonly string[]) => {
  const rows = await db.customers.query(
    'SELECT * FROM customers WHERE id = ANY($1)', [ids]);
  const byId = new Map(rows.map((r: any) => [r.id, r]));
  // ONE VALUE PER INPUT KEY, IN INPUT ORDER. never the rows filtered:
  // a missing customer would shift every position and attach the wrong
  // names to the wrong orders.
  return ids.map((id) => byId.get(id) ?? null);
});

// per request, in the context - never a module singleton
export function makeLoaders(): Loaders {
  return {
    customer: new DataLoader(batchCustomers, { maxBatchSize: 100 }),
    totals:   new DataLoader(batchTotals),
  };
}
// the resolver that uses it is now a one-liner, and the SQL remains
// the batch shape from above. if your loaders are carrying a complex
// graph and the queries still look like this section's first block,
// the batching is not happening - measure, do not assume.

The two escalations past DataLoader

DataLoader solves the canonical case and 95% of real ones. When it does not, there are two deeper answers, both of which the sample below shows in full. A batch resolver at the connection level loads the related entities once for the whole page and builds the payload without per-row resolver calls — awkward but mechanical. And resolver-free fields compute everything in one SQL statement, returning the projections outright: for a genuinely nested graph, the database is better at the join than the loaders are, and a loader chain that mimics a join is the sign that the query is wrong.

The discipline that makes either work is measurement, and it is the note the sample closes with: GraphQL returns 200 with errors, so you need per-resolver timing — extensions.timing — and a per-field error-rate metric. A resolver that suddenly takes 400 ms is an N+1 that appeared when someone added a field; timing is the only thing that will tell you it did.

// A RESOLVER is a field resolver. It runs for EVERY field in the query,
// which is the source of the performance problem.

type Query {
  orders: OrderConnection
}
type OrderConnection { edges: [OrderEdge!]! }
type OrderEdge { node: Order! }
type Order {
  customer: Customer       // <-- a resolver, called once per order
}

// THIS QUERY MAKES 21 DATABASE QUERIES
query {
  orders(first: 20) {
    edges { node { id total { value currency } customer { name } } }
  }
}
//   1 for the orders, 1 for each order's total, 1 for each
//   order's customer = 41. At 100 orders it is 201.

type Order {
  resolveTotal: async (order, _args, ctx) => {
    const r = await db.money.query(`SELECT ... WHERE order_id = $1`,
                                   [order.id]);   // N+1
    return toMoney(r);
  },
}

// THE FIX, in three escalating steps

// 1. DATALOADER. Batch and cache per request. This solves 95% of cases.
import DataLoader from 'dataloader';

const customerLoader = new DataLoader(async (ids) => {
  const rows = await db.customers.query(
    'SELECT * FROM customers WHERE id = ANY($1)', [ids]);
  const byId = new Map(rows.map(r => [r.id, r]));
  return ids.map(id => byId.get(id) ?? null);   // must match input order
});
//   DataLoader caches by key WITHIN ONE REQUEST, so a customer
//   requested by two orders is fetched once. 41 queries becomes 2.
//   Wire it per request, never as a module-level singleton: a global
//   cache is a cross-request data leak and a memory leak.
const loader = new DataLoader(keys => loadMany(keys), { maxBatchSize: 100 });

// 2. A BATCH RESOLVER at the connection level
orders(first: 20) {
  const orders = await db.orders.find({ limit: 20 });
  return {
    edges: orders.map(o => ({ node: o })),
    // load the related entities ONCE for all 20
    customers: await customerLoader.loadMany(orders.map(o => o.customerId)),
  };
}
//   awkward but it removes the per-row resolver entirely.

// 3. RESOLVER-FREE FIELDS. Compute in SQL, return in the payload.
SELECT o.*, c.name AS customer_name,
       json_agg(...) AS lines, t.value AS total_value
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
LEFT JOIN order_totals t ON t.order_id = o.id
WHERE ...
GROUP BY o.id, c.name, t.value;
//   one query, no loaders, and the database is good at this. If your
//   loaders are carrying a complex graph, the query is probably wrong.

// THE MEASUREMENT, because guessing is how you ship an N+1
   GraphQL returns 200 with errors, so HTTP-level monitoring sees
   nothing. You need per-resolver timing:
//     extensions: { timing: { orders: 12, customer: 480 } }
//   and an error-rate metric per field. A resolver that suddenly takes
//   400ms is an N+1 that appeared when someone added a field.
//   Turn introspection off in production and this still works: the
//   server knows its own resolver timings.

Query depth, cost and complexity limits

This is the security chapter. GraphQL's flexibility is an unbounded-compute vulnerability unless you put limits on it, and the default deployments are unbounded.

The attack is nested lists and aliases

The canonical GraphQL attack is not a clever exploit, it is arithmetic: a query with seven levels of nested friend lists, a thousand friends each, is 1000⁷ nodes — roughly 10²¹. The server begins answering it and never finishes, and on many deployments it needs no credentials at all. The second multiplier is aliases: requesting the same expensive field 2,000 times under different aliases is 2,000 executions of one resolver, invisible to a depth check because the depth is one.

These facts dictate what the limits must count. Depth limits count list levels, because lists multiply and objects only nest; alias limits cap the per-query multiplication; and the cost limit — the real safety net — assigns each field a weight and rejects a query whose total exceeds the budget. The depth function below is the structural half; the cost model in the sample that follows is the half that understands your schema.

// 1000^7 = 10^21: not slow, not expensive - unknowable. and it needs
// no credentials on a deployment without auth on the endpoint.
query {
  a1: node(id: "1") { ... on User { friends { ... on User {
    friends { ... on User { friends { ... on User {
      friends { ... on User { friends { ... on User {
        friends { ... on User { name } } } } } } } } } } } } } }
}

// count LIST levels, not object levels, because lists multiply
function maxListDepth(selection, depth = 0) {
  if (!selection.selectionSet) return depth;
  const childDepths = selection.selectionSet.selections.map((s) =>
    isListField(s)                     // a list field multiplies by N
      ? maxListDepth(s, depth + 1)     // an object field only nests
      : maxListDepth(s, depth));
  return Math.max(depth, ...childDepths);
}
// max 7 list levels, max 30 aliases, one global cost ceiling: all
// three are cheap to evaluate before execution and none of them
// answers the other. you need all three.

Use the plugins before you write a cost model

Hand-writing this security layer is a mistake, because a depth limit is twenty lines - and then a year later someone adds a nested route and the twenty lines are wrong, silently. The ecosystem shipped the whole set: depth, aliases, cost and introspection are first-class plugins for Envelop and Apollo, with operation allowlisting for the strict form. Install all of them, set the ceilings from your own traffic, and treat the home-written cost model as the fallback for a shape the tools do not express.

The same ceilings should be per-token, not per-IP: a legitimate admin screen may legitimately cost more than a public read endpoint, and identity is the axis that makes the limit a contract instead of a tax on your own power users.

import { useDepthLimit } from '@envelop/depth-limit';
import { useAliasesLimit } from '@envelop/limit-aliases';
import { useCostLimit } from '@envelop/cost-limiter';
import { useDisableIntrospection } from '@envelop/disable-introspection';

const plugins = [
  useDisableIntrospection({ disableInProduction: true }),
  useDepthLimit({ maxDepth: 7 }),          // list levels, not nesting
  useAliasesLimit({ maxAliases: 30 }),     // blocks the 2000-aliases query
  useCostLimit({ maxCost: 1000, objectCost: 2, scalarCost: 1 }),
];
// whatever the plugin set, the four questions stay the same:
//   1. is a list without first/last rejected as unbounded?
//   2. is an alias explosion rejected before a resolver runs?
//   3. is the cost budget a function of the TOKEN, not the socket?
//   4. is introspection off in production, with a published schema doc
//      standing in for it? (see the next heading)

Introspection is reconnaissance, not a feature

Introspection lets a client read the entire schema — including the fields you have not documented and the mutations you consider private. That makes it the perfect reconnaissance tool: it hands an attacker the exact vocabulary of the attack, the field names, arguments and types, for free, at runtime, with no credentials. Turning it off in production is not a security control — the schema is still in your client bundles — but it closes the cheapest information leak you have, and it forces the honest thing anyway: publish the schema document (from the next chapter) for legitimate consumers.

Nothing here is optional on a public GraphQL endpoint, and the sample below — the depth, aliases, cost and timeout limits, plus the introspection decision — is the minimum deployment. The timeout is the one piece that belongs outside the schema machinery entirely: a per-request AbortSignal is the backstop when every other estimate was wrong.

// THE ATTACK, and it is trivially easy to write
query {
  a1: node(id: "1") { ... on User {
    friends { ... on User {
      friends { ... on User {
        friends { ... on User {
          friends { ... on User {
            friends { ... on User { name } } } } } } } } } }
}
//   7 levels of NESTED LISTS. With 1,000 friends each, that is
//   1000^7 = 10^21 objects. Your server will not answer it and may
//   not survive it.
//   This needs no credentials on many deployments. It is the single
//   most common GraphQL vulnerability.

// THE FOUR LIMITS, and you need all four

// 1. MAX DEPTH, counting lists only
function maxDepth(selection, depth = 0): number {
  if (!selection.selectionSet) return depth;
  const fieldDepth = selection.selectionSet.selections.map(
    s => maxDepth(s, depth + (isList(s) ? 1 : 0)));
  return Math.max(depth, ...fieldDepth);
}
//   Lists multiply. Objects do not. That is the insight: the danger is
//   nesting LISTS, not nesting objects.

// 2. MAX ALIASES. An attacker multiplies work with aliases.
query {
  a1: expensiveField   a2: expensiveField   a3: expensiveField
  ... 2000 of them
}
//   count top-level aliases and cap them, e.g. 30.

// 3. COST ANALYSIS, the real fix
   Assign each field a cost, weight nested costs, and cap the total.
const COST = {
  Query:        { orders: 10, order: 5, node: 2, search: 40 },
  Order:        { lines: 15, customer: 3, total: 1, products: 20 },
  Customer:     { orders: 20, addresses: 5, paymentMethods: 2 },
  OrderLine:    { product: 4, fulfilment: 8 },
};
function cost(sel, parentCost = 1): number {
  let total = 0;
  for (const s of sel.selectionSet?.selections ?? []) {
    const c = COST[s.parentType]?.[s.name] ?? 1;
    total += c * Math.max(1, cost(s, c));   // nested multiplies
  }
  return total;
}
//   reject a query costing over, say, 1000. Make the limits per
//   token, not per IP: a legitimate admin page may cost more than a
//   public one, and identity is the right axis.

// 4. TIMEOUTS AND RESULT LIMITS
   - a per-request timeout. In Node, an AbortSignal on the context.
//   - a maximum response size in nodes. A resolver that returns 100k
//     items needs a cap regardless of the query.
//   - pagination REQUIRED on every list field. A list field without
//     first/last is an unbounded query and there is no safe default.

// THE TOOLING, because hand-writing this is a mistake
   @envelop/core has depth-limit, cost-limit, max-aliases and
   disable-introspection plugins, all first-class.
//   Apollo Server has all four plus operation allowlisting.
//   graphql-depth-limit is the original and still widely used.
//   Use one of these. The cost model above is what you write when you
//   need something the tools do not offer.

// DISABLE INTROSPECTION IN PRODUCTION
   Introspection lets anyone read your whole schema, including the
   fields you have not documented and the queries you consider
   private. It is not a security control and it is not a secret — it
   is a reconnaissance tool.
//   Disable it in production and provide a schema document instead,
//   which you should publish anyway (ch13). The legitimate consumer
   is your own front end, and you can ship it a build artefact
   instead of letting it introspect at runtime.

Mutations, and why they are not CRUD

Executed once per request, at the top level

The protocol guarantee that defines a mutation is that it may execute only once per request and only at the top level — never nested inside a selection. That single rule is what removes the ambiguity of a REST POST that might be a retry: the client knows the operation runs once, and the server's idempotency machinery has a clean contract to honour. The server half is identical to chapter 5's: the client generates an idempotencyKey, the server stores the response, and a repeat returns the original instead of running again.

Putting the key in the input rather than a header is the idiomatic GraphQL form, and it is safer: a typo in a header is silently dropped in some SDKs, while a required input field is rejected by validation. A mutation without an idempotency key should be as unthinkable as a POST without one.

# the mutation, exactly as the client sends it
mutation CreateOrder($input: CreateOrderInput!) {
  createOrder(input: $input) {
    order { id status }
    userErrors { field message code }    # failures live HERE, in types
  }
}

# and the input contract that makes it safe to retry
input CreateOrderInput {
  lines: [OrderLineInput!]!
  shippingAddress: AddressInput
  idempotencyKey: String!                # required, in the input
}
# a retry with the same idempotencyKey gets the stored response back -
# no second charge, no duplicate order. a retry with NO key is a new
# operation, which is exactly the ambiguity the key exists to close.

userErrors is the 422 channel, in types

HTTP status in GraphQL is almost always 200: the status describes whether the request was well-formed GraphQL, not whether the operation succeeded. The place where a REST 422 body lives is userErrors — a per-mutation payload field carrying field, message and a machine-readable code. Validation failures, permission failures and conflicts go there, where a client can switch on them in typed code; unexpected exceptions remain top-level errors, which is GraphQL's way of saying "I broke", not "you asked for something invalid".

The payoff of the closed enum is compile-time: a client's exhaustive switch over error codes is checked by the compiler, so introducing a new code becomes a deliberate update across the client surface instead of a silent fallthrough. That is the strongest argument for keeping the error channel in the schema at all.

async function createOrderResolver(_root, { input }, ctx) {
  const cached = await ctx.idemStore.get(input.idempotencyKey);
  if (cached) return cached.payload;          // a retry: replay, do not run

  try {
    const order = await ctx.service.create(input);
    const payload = { order, userErrors: [] };
    await ctx.idemStore.put(input.idempotencyKey, payload);   // store first
    return payload;
  } catch (e) {
    if (e instanceof ValidationError) {
      return {
        order: null,
        // the 422 body, in the payload, in the schema's own types
        userErrors: e.fields.map((f) => ({
          field: [f.path], message: f.message, code: f.code,
        })),
      };
    }
    throw e;        // real failures stay top-level errors with a 200
  }
}

enum OrderErrorCode { VALIDATION_FAILED NOT_FOUND PERMISSION_DENIED CONFLICT RATE_LIMITED }
type OrderError {
  code: OrderErrorCode!    # a closed enum, not free strings
  message: String!
  field: [String!]!
}
# a new error code is now a COMPILE-TIME change for every client with
# an exhaustive switch - which is precisely where a belief in "the
# response is a type the client fully understands" gets tested.

Name the transition, and decide partial success

Mutation names should name what happens — createOrder, cancelOrder, refundOrder, publishDraft — not what the data stored is. saveOrder or processOrderData conveys nothing about whether this is a create, an update or a state transition, and the schema is exactly where that information belongs: it is the client's type-level documentation. A refund that creates a Refund entity should say so, because it changes what the client expects to exist afterwards.

The second decision is partial success, and it needs a stated answer. When one line of a batch fails, does anything go through? All-or-nothing is simpler, matches the REST mental model, and surprises nobody; partial success needs a results: [CreateOrderResult!]! where each element carries an order or a userError. Most APIs should be all-or-nothing on a single mutation and use a separate bulk mutation when partial success is genuinely wanted — the sample below develops the schema side of both, and the partial form is where the naming discipline pays, because each result element is itself a fact with a name.

// A MUTATION MAY EXECUTE ONLY ONCE, per request, at the top level.
// This is a protocol guarantee and it is the reason mutations exist:
// it removes the ambiguity of a POST that could be a retry.

mutation CreateOrder {
  createOrder(input: { lines: [...], idempotencyKey: "..." }) {
    order { id status }
    userErrors { field message code }
  }
}
//   returns 200 with errors in the payload, not 4xx. HTTP status is
//   always 200 for a well-formed GraphQL request, even when the
//   operation failed.
//   The userErrors array is where a 422 body goes. Use it.

// MUTATION-LEVEL ERRORS, not field-level
//   the graph is for data, not for control flow. A field resolver
//   should not branch on whether some earlier field failed. This is
//   the "graphQL should return data not errors" rule and it is right:
//   it keeps the response shape predictable.
//   Validation errors, permission errors and conflict errors belong in
//   userErrors. Unexpected exceptions become top-level errors.

// NAMING, and it conveys the semantics
   createOrder       creates
   updateOrder       updates
   cancelOrder       a state transition, not CRUD
   refundOrder       creates a Refund entity (ch1)
   publishDraft      an action with no lasting entity
//   Not: saveOrder, postOrder, processOrderData.

// IDEMPOTENCY, and it is the same contract as REST
input CreateOrderInput {
  idempotencyKey: String!
}
//   Same behaviour as ch5: the client generates it, the server stores
//   the response, a repeat returns the original. Putting it in the
//   input rather than a header is idiomatic GraphQL and works better,
//   because the field cannot be accidentally omitted.

// ERRORS, and the type-safety win you should take
enum OrderErrorCode {
  VALIDATION_FAILED
  NOT_FOUND
  PERMISSION_DENIED
  CONFLICT
  RATE_LIMITED
}
type OrderError { code: OrderErrorCode!  message: String!  field: [String!]! }
//   A closed enum instead of free strings. The client's switch is
//   exhaustive-checked by the compiler, so a new error code is a
//   compile-time change rather than a silent fallthrough.
//   This is the best reason to have GraphQL at all: the response is a
//   type the client fully understands.

// PARTIAL SUCCESS, and it needs a decision
   When one line in a batch fails, do the others go through?
   - all-or-nothing: simpler, matches REST, surprises nobody
   - partial: a `results: [CreateOrderResult!]!` where each has an
//     order OR a userError. More useful for bulk operations, harder to
//   reason about.
//   Decide explicitly and document it. Most APIs should be
//   all-or-nothing on a single mutation and use a separate bulk
//   mutation when partial success is genuinely wanted.

Connection-based pagination

The connection shape solved "what comes next" once

The Relay Connection specification settled the question that every hand-rolled pagination scheme argues about: how does the client know what comes next, and how does it ask for it, in a way that survives concurrent writes? The connection answers in types. pageInfo carries hasNextPage and hasPreviousPage so a client knows which direction is available, and the edges pair each node with its own cursor, so the client never composes a position itself — it passes back an opaque token. There are no offsets anywhere, which is the property that makes it correct: a row inserted or deleted between pages changes neither what you see next nor what you have seen (chapter 3).

The schema below is the shape to copy. The two fields worth keeping exactly are pageInfo (the navigation contract) and edges (the node-plus-cursor pairing); nodes is a convenience many clients never need, and totalCount is a cost, not a courtesy.

# the Relay Connection shape - the standard that removed the argument
type OrderConnection {
  edges: [OrderEdge!]!     # cursor paired with node: the pagination unit
  nodes: [Order!]!         # convenience shorthand; omit if unused
  pageInfo: PageInfo!      # the navigation contract: what comes next?
  totalCount: Int          # expensive - make it nullable or opt-in
}
type OrderEdge { cursor: String!  node: Order! }

type PageInfo {
  hasNextPage: Boolean!     # the client renders "next" from this
  hasPreviousPage: Boolean! # forward AND backward, both directions
  startCursor: String
  endCursor: String
}

# a query, and what it proves:
query {
  orders(first: 20, after: "Y3Vyc29yOjIwfQ==") {
    edges { cursor node { id status } }
    pageInfo { hasNextPage endCursor }
  }
}
# no offset parameter exists. the client can no longer compose a bad
# position, because it has no position - only what the API gave it.

The cursor is opaque, and carries the sort key

The cursor's contract is that the client treats it as an opaque token, and the server encodes into it exactly what the next page needs: the sort key of the last row returned. With ORDER BY created_at, id, that is the pair, because created_at alone is not a total order — two orders created in the same millisecond, and an offset-based page would silently skip or duplicate one of them. The id tie-breaker makes the ordering total, and the cursor therefore points at a unique position that cannot shift.

Two implementation notes. The server must also validate the cursor — a malformed or tampered cursor is a 400, not a scan. And a cursor expires: if your data is keyed tightly, a cursor from last month may point nowhere, and the documented behaviour for that is "return the first page", never an error that looks like a broken API.

// encode the (created_at, id) pair into an opaque token
function encode(createdAt: Date, id: string): string {
  return Buffer.from(`${createdAt.toISOString()}|${id}`).toString('base64url');
}
function decode(cursor: string): { createdAt: string; id: string } {
  try {
    const [createdAt, id] = Buffer.from(cursor, 'base64url').toString().split('|', 2);
    if (!createdAt || !id) throw new Error('bad cursor');
    return { createdAt, id };
  } catch {
    // a tampered cursor is a CLIENT error, and it must be a 400.
    // `SELECT ... WHERE created_at < $1` with garbage input is a scan,
    // not a validation.
    throw new GraphQLError('invalid cursor', { extensions: { code: 'BAD_USER_INPUT' } });
  }
}
// the test that proves stability: paginate to page 3, INSERT a row in
// the middle of the sort, fetch page 4 from the page-3 cursor, and
// assert no node appears twice and none is skipped. offsets cannot
// pass that test; cursors pass it by construction.

The hard cap and the totalCount cost

Every list field needs a hard cap — Math.min(first, 100), enforced regardless of what the client asked — and the query should fetch size + 1 so hasNextPage is exact without a second query. The fraction that gets this wrong is the fraction whose "next" button appears when the next page is empty. And totalCount is a cost with an N+1 flavour: one COUNT(*) per connection, per request, per client that asks. The sample below shows the full resolver — cap, size + 1, exact hasNextPage, validated arguments — and then the count problem, because the two belong in the same decision.

The honest answers for the count are few: compute it in the same query with a window function; resolve it only when explicitly requested; or approximate it from a stats table, labelled as such. A UI that renders "1–20 of 4,281" does not need the exact number often enough to justify a full scan on every page load.

// The Relay Connections Specification, and it is the de-facto standard
// because it solved the "what comes next" problem once.

type OrderConnection {
  edges: [OrderEdge!]!
  nodes: [Order!]!          // convenience; servers usually expose both
  pageInfo: PageInfo!
  totalCount: Int           // expensive. omit or make it opt-in
}

type OrderEdge { cursor: String!  node: Order! }

type PageInfo {
  hasNextPage: Boolean!
  hasPreviousPage: Boolean!
  startCursor: String
  endCursor: String
}

// A QUERY, AND WHAT IT PROVES
query {
  orders(first: 20, after: "Y3Vyc29yOjIwfQ==") {
    edges { cursor node { id status } }
    pageInfo { hasNextPage endCursor }
  }
}
//   forward and backward. hasPreviousPage. No offsets, so no skipping
//   under concurrent writes (ch3).

// THE SERVER, concretely
orders: async (_parent, { first = 20, after }, ctx) => {
  const size = Math.min(first, 100);            // HARD CAP. not 1000.
  const rows = await db.query(
    `SELECT * FROM orders
      WHERE ($1::text IS NULL OR (created_at, id) < ($1::timestamptz, $2))
      ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC
      LIMIT $3`,                                   // size + 1
    [after ? decode(after).createdAt : null,
     after ? decode(after).id : null,
     size + 1]);

  const hasNextPage = rows.length > size;
  const page = rows.slice(0, size);
  return {
    edges: page.map(r => ({ cursor: encode(r.created_at, r.id), node: toOrder(r) })),
    nodes: page.map(toOrder),
    pageInfo: {
      hasNextPage,
      hasPreviousPage: after !== null && after !== undefined,
      startCursor: page[0] ? encode(page[0].created_at, page[0].id) : null,
      endCursor: page.length ? encode(page.at(-1).created_at, page.at(-1).id) : null,
    },
  };
},
//   note: fetch size + 1 so hasNextPage is exact, and never return
//   more than the cap regardless of what the client asked for.

// THE ARGUMENT DEFAULTS THAT PREVENT THE MOST BUGS
   first: Int = 20, last: Int = 20      both optional, sensible default
//   after: String, before: String        mutually exclusive with first/last
//   "first + before" is an error, not a silent precedence
//   reject a negative or absurd first with a validation error, do not
//   clamp silently. A client that asked for 10,000 and got 100 has a
//   bug you want to see.

// totalCount IS A COST, and clients ask for it constantly
   N+1: for every connection, a COUNT(*).
   Fix: count in the same query with a window function.
     SELECT *, COUNT(*) OVER () AS total_count FROM orders ...
//   which is still a full scan, so:
//   - make it nullable and document it as expensive
//   - or resolve it only when explicitly requested
//   - or approximate: an estimate from a stats table, labelled
//   A UI that shows "1-20 of 4,281" does not need the exact number
//   often enough to justify a full count on every request.

Subscriptions and realtime

A WebSocket carrying GraphQL documents

A subscription is a persistent WebSocket carrying GraphQL operations, and its natural fit is "tell me when this changes": one connection, multiplexing many subscribed operations, with the server pushing events as they happen. The schema declares the subscription surface like any other field — orderChanged(id: ID!): Order! — and the client subscribes with a query shaped exactly like a read, which is the design win: the subscription's selection set is the shape of every push.

Two server decisions dominate the section. Authorisation is checked at subscribe time, once, and events are then filtered per subscriber — checking per event is a database query per message and a place to forget the check. And the connection itself is state you now own: heartbeat, reconnect, resubscribe on an identity change, and the gap those reconnects open up.

type Subscription {
  orderChanged(id: ID!): Order!
  messageAdded(conversationId: ID!): Message!
}

subscription OnOrder($id: ID!) {
  orderChanged(id: $id) { id status total { value currency } }
}

# what the client does when the socket drops - and it WILL drop:
#   1. reconnect (exponential backoff, jittered, capped)
#   2. RESUBSCRIBE every operation it had open (conn state is gone)
#   3. REFETCH the current state, because events arriving during the
#      gap were missed and you cannot reconstruct them from nothing
# step 3 is why real clients wrap subscriptions in a "sync on connect"
# that polls once after subscribing. the subscription is for changes;
# the refetch is for truth.

The pubsub scaling problem is the real work

The subscription code is the easy 200 lines; the publish path is the platform. With one instance, an in-process event emitter is enough. With ten instances, a user connected to instance 3 never hears an event published on instance 7 unless there is a shared bus — and the bus choice is a durability choice. Redis pub/sub is simple and at-most-once: an instance restart loses in-flight messages and the reconnect-refetch must cover the loss. NATS or Kafka is durable and ordered within a partition, and is the right answer at real scale. Postgres LISTEN/NOTIFY covers modest scale while keeping the infrastructure count at one.

Whichever bus you choose, the consumer side must stay the same shape: the reconnect-refetch loop from the previous heading, because every bus can lose the race between an event and a reconnect, and the client that does not refetch has a permanent gap.

import { Redis } from 'ioredis';

// one writer, one subscriber, and a fan-out loop per connection set
const pub = new Redis();      // business side publishes here
const sub = new Redis();      // dedicated connection for subscribe

await sub.subscribe('order:changed');
sub.on('message', (channel, message) => {
  const { id, payload } = JSON.parse(message);
  for (const ws of connectionsByOrder.get(id) ?? []) {
    ws.send(JSON.stringify({ type: 'data', payload }));
  }
});

// on the business side: pub.publish('order:changed',
//   JSON.stringify({ id: orderId, payload: event }))
//   publish NEVER runs in the mutation transaction path as a blocking
//   call - queue it or fire-and-forget, or a slow subscriber drags your
//   write latency with it.

// the scaling ladder, honestly:
//   in-process emitter  -> one instance, fine
//   Redis pub/sub       -> simple, at-most-once, lossy on restart
//   NATS / Kafka        -> durable, ordered, the right answer at scale
//   LISTEN/NOTIFY       -> modest scale, one database, no new infra

What to do instead, most of the time

Subscriptions earn their complexity for a specific class: collaboration, presence, typing indicators, live trading — anything where a user would notice a multi-second delay as wrong. A dashboard that refreshes on a schedule does not have that requirement, and for it the honest comparison is a conditional poll: an updatedAfter cursor plus an ETag, which survives every proxy, costs nothing to operate, and never loses an event, because the client controls when truth is re-established.

That comparison is the note the sample below closes with, and it is the right instinct to verify: if "within a second" is not a hard requirement, the cheaper mechanism is the better one, and a subscription platform built on a hunch is a permanent operational cost with a "maybe some day" architect. The subscription schema above and the polling alternative are the two ends of the spectrum; the spectrum in between is the decision procedure.

// SUBSCRIPTIONS: a persistent WebSocket carrying GraphQL documents.
// It is the natural fit for "tell me when this changes".

type Subscription {
  orderChanged(id: ID!): Order!
  messageAdded(conversationId: ID!): Message!
}

subscription OnOrder($id: ID!) {
  orderChanged(id: $id) { id status total { value currency } }
}

// THE SERVER, and every part has a production consequence
  server = new GraphQLServer({
    schema,
    // 1. one connection per user, multiplexed by operation
    subscriptions: {
      // 2. AUTHORISATION AT SUBSCRIBE TIME, not per event.
      //    Check once, when the subscription starts, then filter
      //    events. Checking per event is a database query per
      //    message and a place to forget the check.
      onSubscribe: async (_root, args, ctx) => {
        await assertCanReadOrder(ctx.user, args.id);
        return ctx.pubsub.asyncIterator(`order:${args.id}`);
      },
    },
  });

// THE PUBSUB SCALING PROBLEM, and it is the real work
//   with one instance, an in-process EventEmitter is enough.
//   with 10 instances, a user connected to instance 3 never receives
//   an event published on instance 7. You need a shared bus:
//     Redis pub/sub      simple, at-most-once, messages lost on
//                        instance restart
//     NATS / Kafka       durable, ordered within a partition, and the
//                        right answer at scale
//     Postgres LISTEN/   works for modest scale, and keeps the
//        NOTIFY          infrastructure count down
//   And you must handle reconnect: the client reconnects, resubscribes,
//   and you must handle the gap. That is why every realtime client
//   also polls or refetches on reconnect.

// WHAT TO DO INSTEAD, most of the time
//   SUBSCRIPTIONS solve a problem that polling plus a good ETag solves
//   more cheaply:
     GET /orders?updatedAfter=2026-03-14T09:31:00Z
   //   A cheap conditional poll of the changed set beats a permanent
//   WebSocket for a dashboard, and it survives every proxy.
//   Subscriptions earn their complexity for: collaboration, presence,
//   typing indicators, live trading, and anything where a user would
//   notice a multi-second delay as wrong.

Persisted queries and allowlists

This solves the two largest GraphQL problems — arbitrary client queries and the cost attack — and it is underused.

Three problems, one mechanism

A persisted query replaces the query text on the wire with a hash the server already holds — and that single substitution solves three problems at once. It closes the cost attack, because the client can no longer send an arbitrary document: an unknown id in production is a 400, and every query that executes is one you registered, with its cost bounded in advance. It fixes the caching story, because the request now keys on a stable id instead of a variable-length text blob. And it removes the query text from every request — the "GraphQL over HTTP" overhead that shows up as hundreds of kilobytes of repeated documents on a chatty client.

Registration happens at build time in CI: your codegen step walks the client's real queries, hashes them, and emits a registry the server loads at startup. "The server has the text" stops being a deployment ritual and becomes a checked-in artefact, reviewed like the schema itself.

// BEFORE: query text on the wire, every request, unregulated
POST /graphql
{
  "query": "query OrderScreen($id: ID!) { order(id: $id) { id status } }",
  "variables": { "id": "ord_1" }
}

// AFTER: a hash, and nothing else
POST /graphql
{
  "id": "sha256:8f2a91c4e7d0b1...",
  "variables": { "id": "ord_1" }
}
// the three wins in one move:
//   1. arbitrary queries are impossible: 400 on an unknown id
//   2. the cache keys on the id, not on text that includes whitespace
//      and formatting differences
//   3. the document stops travelling, which is the biggest single
//      payload reduction most GraphQL apps ever ship

Registration at build time, enforced in CI

The registry is a build artefact and the CI rule around it is what makes the security claim true. Generate persisted-documents.json from the client's real queries — never from a hand-maintained list, which rots — and commit it. The server loads it at startup and serves only those ids. In production, an unknown id is a 400; the fallback that accepts query text must exist only in development, and it must be gated on an environment check rather than a flag someone can flip.

The strictest form is an allowlist as a review gate: CI diffs the client's queries against the previous registry and fails the build on a difference until a human has reviewed it. That is the deployment a regulated organisation wants, and it is also the answer for a schema you consider sensitive — your clients literally cannot ask for anything you did not decide to allow.

# graphql-codegen.yml: generate the registry from the real queries
schema: src/schema.graphql
documents: src/**/*.graphql
generates:
  persisted-documents.json:
    plugins:
      - graphql-codegen-persisted-documents

# CI, two lines: the registry is a reviewable artefact
#   diff persisted-documents.json (previous release)
#   fail unless every change has an allowlist approval ticket

# the server half, with the fallback locked to development
apollo.use(ApolloServerPluginPersistedQueries({
  cache: new KeyvAdapter(new Keyv('apollo'), { namespace: 'gql' }),
  allowPersistedQueryNotFound: process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production',
}));
# in production the unknown id never reaches a resolver: 400, bounded,
# and your COST_MAP from the depth section now has a finite input
# space, because the only documents that can execute are registered.

What persisted queries do not fix

The middle ground — automatic persisted queries (APQ) — is a bandwidth and caching win but not a security control: the client sends the full text on first sight, so an attacker can still send arbitrary text, and you still need the depth, alias and cost limits on top. The distinction matters because APQ is what most frameworks ship first, and its presence can feel like the problem is handled. Persisted queries with the hard 400 are the security form; APQ is the economy form, and they should not be confused.

It also does not fix three things the sample below is honest about: HTTP caching is still per-id rather than per-resource, so two persisted queries for the same data still hit twice; rate limiting still applies, per id, with a cost budget; and the schema is still publicly introspectable unless introspection is off. Persisted queries shrink the attack surface to "the queries you wrote" — the schema problem is a separate chapter.

// THE PROBLEM IT SOLVES
//   1. A client can send any query, which is the cost attack (ch9-depth)
//   2. Every distinct query string is a new HTTP cache key, so your
//      CDN hit rate is zero
//   3. The query text travels on every request, wasting bandwidth
//      (the "GraphQL over HTTP" 100-request-overhead problem)

// PERSISTED QUERIES: the client sends a hash, the server has the text
   POST /graphql
   { "id": "sha256:8f2a91c4e7...", "variables": { "id": "ord_1" } }

//   registration, at build time, in your CI
   $ graphql-codegen --persisted
   //   or: your client build sends each query once to a register
   //   endpoint, gets an id, and uses the id from then on

// THE SERVER
   apollo.use(ApolloServerPluginPersistedQueries({
     cache: new KeyvAdapter(new Keyv('apollo'), { namespace: 'gql' }),
     // refuse an unknown id instead of accepting the query text
     //   fallback for development only
     allowPersistedQueryNotFound: process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production',
   }));
//   in production, an unknown id is a 400. The client cannot send a
//   query the server has never seen. That eliminates the cost attack
//   entirely, because the cost of each query is bounded by what you
//   registered.

// AUTOMATIC PERSISTED QUERIES: the middle ground
   The client sends the full text on first sight, the server stores it
   and returns an id; afterwards the client sends the id. Unknown ids
//   fall back to the full text, which means a stale client still works
//   but an attacker can still send arbitrary text — so you still need
//   the cost limits. It is a bandwidth and caching win, not a
//   security control.

// APQ (Automatic Persisted Queries), the older version
   The client hashes the query and sends only the hash. If the server
   has not seen it, the client retries with the full text. One round
//   trip on first use, zero overhead afterwards, and it removes query
//   text from the request body. Still not a security control.

// ALLOWLISTING, the strictest form
   Only queries registered in a reviewed file may execute. CI compares
   the client's queries against the allowlist and fails on a
   difference. This is what a highly regulated deployment wants, and
   it is also the best answer for a schema you consider sensitive:
//   your clients cannot ask for anything you did not decide to allow.

// WHAT PERSISTED QUERIES DO NOT FIX
//   - HTTP caching is still per-id, not per-resource. Two different
//     persisted queries for the same data still hit twice.
//   - rate limiting still applies, per id, with a cost budget.
//   - the schema is still public unless introspection is off.

Federation, and when to stop

Each subgraph owns a domain, and one directive

The problem federation exists for is organisational: org A owns Users, org B owns Orders, org C owns Products, and a client needs a page built from all three. Federating through each organisation's API directly couples every client to every team's latency and availability. Federation's answer is a subgraph per domain, declaring its own types plus the @key that lets other subgraphs reference them, composed behind a single gateway that presents one schema.

The directive is the whole trick, and it has a price. The @key exposes each entity to cross-subgraph joins, and every such join is resolved by the gateway issuing a query into another service — parent rows in subgraph A, then one query per parent into subgraph B. That is the N+1 pattern of the resolver section by construction, at the network boundary, and subgraph B's load balancer sits permanently in your critical path. The anatomy below is the same story told as a diagram; read it with the cost list in mind. 

// THE PROBLEM: org A owns Users, org B owns Orders, org C owns
// Products, and a client needs a page with data from all three.
// Federating through each organisation's API is slow and couples
// every team to every other team's availability.

// THE SOLUTION: a GATEWAY that composes subgraphs
   client -> gateway -> users subgraph (org A)
                     -> orders subgraph (org B)
                     -> products subgraph (org C)
//   each subgraph owns its domain completely. The gateway resolves the
//   cross-subgraph parts of a query by planning across the schemas.

// THE COST, and it is significant
//   - a shared graph becomes a shared DEPLOYMENT. Five subgraphs in
//     one release train means five releases to coordinate, which is
//     the organisational problem federation was supposed to solve.
//   - the gateway is a new availability dependency for everything.
//   - cross-subgraph joins are N+1 by construction: the gateway
//     resolves the parent in subgraph A, then issues one query per
//     parent into subgraph B. A load balancer in subgraph B is not
//     optional.
//   - error semantics must be agreed across teams, which is a real
//     negotiation and it is where federation projects usually stall.
//   - tracing across the whole graph is required to debug anything.

// WHEN IT IS RIGHT
//   - genuinely separate teams with genuinely separate domains and
//     separate deployment cadences
//   - a shared platform team willing to run the gateway
//   - you have already solved cost limits, persisted queries and
//     observability in a single graph
//   Federation is a consequence of organisational boundaries. If you
//   do not have those boundaries, you are adding a distributed system
//   for no reason.

// WHEN TO STOP, which is usually earlier than people think
   - one team, one schema: no federation. A modular monolith with one
//     GraphQL schema is easier and faster than three subgraphs.
//   - subgraphs that split along technical lines rather than
//     organisational ones ("users service" and "billing service") are
//     the worst case: the client has to do the joins either way.
//   - you have tried and could not get cost analysis working across
//     the boundary. Without it, one subgraph's unbounded list field
//     makes the whole gateway unsafe.

// THE ALTERNATIVE THAT OFTEN WINS: REST + a BFF
   A backend-for-frontend that composes several REST services into the
   shape one screen needs. No schema federation, no query planning, no
//   shared graph. You lose the client's ability to compose freely, and
//   you gain: cacheable HTTP, one place to enforce authorisation, and
//   a normal debuggable request.
//   For a first-party UI with a handful of screens, this is usually
//   the better engineering choice.

The gateway is a new availability dependency

The gateway gives you one schema, one endpoint and cross-domain queries, and it costs you a distributed system: a client-visible slow query is now a pile of sub-requests across three services, and debugging it requires distributed tracing before it requires anything else. The organisational promise — five teams deploying on their own cadences — is quietly undone, because every subgraph change feeds the same composed schema, and a composed schema released together is a shared release train, which is the exact coupling federation was supposed to remove.

The composition below shows the machinery and the failure surface together. Nothing about it is wrong; the question is whether the organisations truly cannot share one schema, because that is the only circumstance under which the machinery pays for itself.

# the gateway composition: three subgraphs, one client-facing schema
services:
  - users:    http://users.internal/graphql
  - orders:   http://orders.internal/graphql
  - products: http://products.internal/graphql

# what the gateway must also get BEFORE the first cross-domain query:
#   - distributed tracing wired through every subgraph (a 2s query is
#     a fan of sub-requests; without tracing it is a black box)
#   - error-semantics agreement across teams (which status, which
#     extensions code, partial-vs-total failure) - the negotiation
#     where federation projects usually stall
#   - a fallback when a subgraph is down: the whole graph has no
#     graceful degradation by default
# none of these are optional, and all of them are ownership the
# gateway team inherits from every subgraph team.

When it is right, and when to stop

Federation is right when the boundaries are real: genuinely separate teams with genuinely separate domains and separate deployment cadences, plus a platform team willing to run the gateway. It is wrong when the boundaries are cosmetic — one team, one schema, no federation — because then you are adding a distributed system for no reason, and a modular monolith with one GraphQL schema is easier and faster than three subgraphs joined at the network. The decision list in the sample below is the honest ordering: solve cost limits, persisted queries and observability in a single graph first, because the single graph is the base case federation inherits from, and a federation without those three is a lottery.

And the point where most teams should stop is earlier than they think. The one-schema-for-one-org case exists, the one-schema-per-org case is the overwhelmingly common reality, and the composition machinery in between is justified by an organisational chart, not by a schema diagram.

// 1. THE HTTP OVERHEAD, and it is measurable
//   A page needing 6 REST calls: 6 round trips.
//   One GraphQL call: 1 round trip, but a much larger request body
//   and a single large response.
//   On a 30ms connection that is ~150ms saved. On a 150ms mobile
//   connection, ~900ms. On HTTP/2 with multiplexing over one connection,
//   it is far less: maybe 60ms.
//   So: GraphQL's headline win is biggest on high-latency networks and
//   small on a modern HTTP/2 connection to a nearby server. Measure,
//   do not assume.

// 2. THE SERVER COST IS HIGHER, not lower
//   GraphQL does more work per request: planning, validation against
//   the type system, permission checks per resolver, N+1 avoidance.
//   A GraphQL endpoint serving the same data as a well-designed REST
//   endpoint with ETags and a data loader may use more CPU, not less.
//   The saving is in the CLIENT's round trips, not in the server's
//   work. Plan for it.

// 3. CACHING IS EFFECTIVELY GONE at the HTTP layer
   CDN, browser cache, ETag, 304: all of it depends on the URL
   identifying the resource. Every GraphQL POST is the same URL.
//   You get a cache by keying on the persisted-query id and the
//   variables, in Redis, inside your application. That is work, and
//   the cache invalidation is now your problem rather than HTTP's.

// 4. MONITORING IS HARDER
//   HTTP status is always 200. Dashboards built on status codes show
//   nothing. You need resolver-level timing and per-field error rates,
//   which means instrumenting the server, not the transport.
//   And "is the API up" becomes "is p99 resolver time under budget and
//   the error rate near zero", which is a harder question.

// 5. THE SCHEMA IS THE API AND IT IS HARD TO CHANGE
   REST: an additive field is additive. GraphQL: the whole schema is
   inspectable by any client, so a client WILL be using a field you
   consider internal. There is no "internal" in a public schema.
//   This is the cost that bites years later, and it is why schema
//   review and a deprecation process (ch7) matter more here.

// 6. ONBOARDING A DEVELOPER IS HARDER
   To use your GraphQL API they must learn your domain's shape. A
   REST consumer can guess: GET /orders/{id}. A GraphQL consumer must
   learn that Order has an OrderLineConnection with edges and nodes and
//   pageInfo, or use codegen. Codegen solves it, and codegen is
//   mandatory infrastructure that has to keep working with every
//   language your consumers use.

// THE HONEST SUMMARY
//   GraphQL costs you: HTTP caching, status-code monitoring, schema
//   evolvability, and a security surface that needs cost analysis.
//   It buys you: exactly the data a client asked for, one round trip,
//   typed responses, and a self-documenting schema.
//   For a first-party front end on a complex domain, the trade is good.
//   For a public or partner API, it usually is not, and REST with a
//   careful contract (chapters 1-8) is the safer default.

Exercises 9

  1. Write a GraphQL schema for an order domain from scratch. Then review every field for nullability and justify each choice in terms of the invariants. Change three decisions and write down what would break.
  2. Write a nested-friends query to depth 8 against a test schema and instrument it. Then add a depth limit and a DataLoader, and measure the resolver count before and after.
  3. Implement a cost model for your own schema, calculate the cost of the ten queries your front end actually sends, and set the limit just above the worst of them. Verify a pathological query is rejected.
  4. Add persisted queries in production mode, confirm an unknown id is a 400, and measure the request body size reduction on your heaviest query.
  5. Build the same screen twice: once with six REST calls, once with one GraphQL query. Measure both on a throttled connection and on a local one, and write down which won where.

10 · gRPC and Protobuf

When gRPC is the right answerThe protobuf schemaField numbers are the contractService definitions and streamingDeadlines, cancellation and errorsStatus codes, not exceptionsInterceptors: the middleware storygRPC over HTTP: the gatewayReflection and codegenThe traps

gRPC is a binary RPC contract with a code generator. That gives you typed clients in every language and fast serialisation, and it costs you HTTP semantics, human debuggability, and the ability to call your API from a browser without a proxy.

When gRPC is the right answer

Internal service-to-service is the correct default

When you own both ends and both ends have a codegen toolchain, gRPC gives you a typed client and server from one schema, a contract checked at compile time in every language, and a payload roughly 5–10x smaller than the equivalent JSON. The justification is almost never "it is faster"; it is "we have twenty internal services and the REST DTOs drift". The server below is a complete, runnable unary service. The only escaping in it is the address-of operator, written & so the sample stays valid HTML.

// server/main.go
package main

import (
	"context"
	"log"
	"net"

	"google.golang.org/grpc"

	ordersv1 "example.com/gen/orders/v1"
)

type server struct{ ordersv1.UnimplementedOrderServiceServer }

func (s *server) GetOrder(ctx context.Context, req *ordersv1.GetOrderRequest) (*ordersv1.Order, error) {
	return &ordersv1.Order{
		Id:     req.GetId(),
		Status: ordersv1.OrderStatus_ORDER_STATUS_PAID,
	}, nil
}

func main() {
	lis, err := net.Listen("tcp", ":50051")
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatal(err)
	}
	grpcServer := grpc.NewServer()
	ordersv1.RegisterOrderServiceServer(grpcServer, &server{})
	log.Fatal(grpcServer.Serve(lis))
}

Public, browser and partner consumers rule it out

Browser JavaScript cannot read HTTP/2 trailers, and gRPC carries its status there. That one constraint is why "gRPC in the browser" always means a proxy — gRPC-Web, Connect or an Envoy filter — and why a partner integrating with curl will have a bad day. What a raw client receives is a length-prefixed frame with no status line to read.

# A browser and curl cannot speak gRPC. The response is a binary frame.
curl -sS -X POST https://api.example.com/orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder \
  -H 'content-type: application/grpc' \
  -H 'te: trailers' \
  --data-binary $'\x00\x00\x00\x00\x05{"id":"1"}' | xxd | head -2
# 00000000: 0000 0000 05 ...   raw protobuf, no status line to read

# The supported route is a proxy in front of the same handler.
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"id":"1"}' \
  localhost:50051 orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder
# browser -> Envoy (grpc_web filter) -> OrderService

Measure the endpoint before you commit to a rewrite

Converting REST to gRPC is a rewrite of every caller, so establish the real numbers first. If the bottleneck is an unindexed query, gRPC will not move it. Compare the same call shape on both transports with a load tool, and read bytes per request as well as latency.

# ghz is the gRPC load tester; wrk/vegeta cover the REST side.
ghz --insecure \
    --proto ./orders/v1/orders.proto \
    --call orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder \
    -d '{"id":"ord_1"}' \
    -n 20000 -c 50 \
    localhost:50051

# Compare: requests/sec, p50, p90, p99, and bytes per request.
# A REST endpoint at 5k req/s with pooling is usually not the problem.
"gRPC is better" is not an adoption reason. "We stream and run 20k internal calls a second and own both ends" is. Adopting gRPC to fix a slow database makes a slow system with a code generator.
// THE CASE FOR
//   - INTERNAL service-to-service traffic where you own both ends.
//     This is the overwhelmingly common correct use.
//   - You need strong typing across a language boundary and you have
//     a codegen toolchain for both languages.
//   - Latency and payload size matter. Protobuf is roughly 5-10x
//     smaller than JSON for the same data, and it skips the parse.
//   - You need streaming: bidirectional, server-streaming or
//     client-streaming. REST has no real answer for this.
//   - Many small messages per second. 20,000 unary calls a second
//     across services is where gRPC's efficiency shows.

// THE CASE AGAINST
//   - PUBLIC or PARTNER APIs. gRPC-Web exists and is a workaround, not
//     a good experience.
//   - BROWSER CLIENTS without a proxy. HTTP/2 with trailers is not
//     available to browser JavaScript. This is a hard constraint.
//   - THIRD-PARTY INTEGRATIONS where curl and Postman matter. Nobody
//     has ever debugged a gRPC call with curl successfully.
//   - Anything where a middlebox will break it. Corporate proxies that
//     do not understand HTTP/2 trailers will fail some gRPC calls and
//     pass others, which is a wonderful class of bug.
//   - WHEN YOU NEED HTTP CACHING or a CDN. There is none.

// THE DECISION, in one line
   gRPC internal, REST at the edge. Almost every system that uses
// gRPC correctly has exactly this shape: HTTP/JSON or gRPC-Web in
// front for browsers and partners, gRPC between internal services,
// often with the same handler serving both.

// WHAT YOU ACTUALLY LOSE, and be honest about it
//   - a URL. No GET with a path. Every call is a POST to one URL, so
//     every middleware that keys on the path is useless
//   - status codes. 200 or a gRPC status. No 404, no 429, no 503
//   - headers for caching, ETag, conditional requests. gRPC has
