//     metadata but it is not HTTP semantics
//   - curl. `grpcurl` exists and is fine, but it is not universal
//   - inspectability. A corporate proxy that logs paths cannot log
//     gRPC. You lose request-level observability unless you add it
//   - human-readable errors. Errors are a status enum, not a body a
//     person can read

The protobuf schema

Messages carry the wire types

A message is a named set of numbered fields. Each field has a type and a number, and the number — not the name — is what travels on the wire. Scalars have no null, repeated is a growable list, and map keys must be integral or string. Note that the angle brackets of a map type are escaped here, exactly as they must be in the file.

syntax = "proto3";
package orders.v1;

import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";

message Order {
  string id = 1;                     // opaque, never an integer
  OrderStatus status = 2;
  Money total = 3;
  repeated OrderLine lines = 4;      // a growable list
  map<string, string> metadata = 5;  // string keys only
  Timestamp created_at = 6;          // use the well-known types
}

message OrderLine {
  string sku = 1;
  int32 quantity = 2;
  Money unit_price = 3;
}

Enums, presence, and the zero value

The first enum value must be 0, because 0 is what an omitted field yields. Name it UNSPECIFIED so an old client that does not know a new state reports "I do not know" rather than a plausible wrong value. For a scalar where zero is a real value, add optional to get explicit presence tracking.

enum OrderStatus {
  ORDER_STATUS_UNSPECIFIED = 0;   // must be zero, always
  ORDER_STATUS_PENDING = 1;
  ORDER_STATUS_PAID = 2;
  ORDER_STATUS_SHIPPED = 3;
}

message Discount {
  optional int32 percent = 1;     // 0 is now distinguishable from unset
  // without `optional`, an absent percent and percent = 0 are identical
}

File layout and naming

One package per bounded context, versioned in the package name, one file per service. A single giant .proto is a compile-time and code-review problem: every change rebuilds every consumer and every reviewer sees a thousand-line diff. The naming rules are not enforced by the compiler but every ecosystem follows them, which is what makes a generated client legible.

# one package per bounded context, versioned in the name
proto/
  orders/v1/orders.proto      # package orders.v1
  orders/v1/refunds.proto
  inventory/v1/stock.proto    # package inventory.v1

# messages      PascalCase        OrderLine
# fields        snake_case        customer_id
# enum values   SCREAMING_SNAKE   ORDER_STATUS_PAID
# packages      lowercase dotted  orders.v1
# RPC methods   PascalCase        GetOrder

syntax = "proto3";
package orders.v1;

import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";
import "google/protobuf/empty.proto";

// MESSAGES: the wire types
message Money {
  int64 value = 1;          // MINOR UNITS. always. (ch1)
  string currency = 2;      // ISO 4217, uppercase
}

message Order {
  string id = 1;            // opaque string, same convention as REST
  OrderStatus status = 2;
  Money total = 3;
  string customer_id = 4;
  Timestamp created_at = 5;
  repeated OrderLine lines = 6;
  map<string, string> metadata = 7;
}

message OrderLine {
  string sku = 1;
  int32 quantity = 2;
  Money unit_price = 3;
}

enum OrderStatus {
  ORDER_STATUS_UNSPECIFIED = 0;   // required as zero
  ORDER_STATUS_PENDING = 1;
  ORDER_STATUS_PAID = 2;
  ORDER_STATUS_SHIPPED = 3;
  ORDER_STATUS_CANCELLED = 4;
}

message GetOrderRequest { string id = 1; }
message ListOrdersRequest {
  int32 page_size = 1;
  string page_token = 2;
  OrderFilter filter = 3;
}
message ListOrdersResponse {
  repeated Order orders = 1;
  string next_page_token = 2;
}
message CreateOrderRequest {
  repeated OrderLine lines = 1;
  string idempotency_key = 2;      // same contract as REST (ch5)
}

// THE PROTO3 RULES THAT MATTER

// 1. NO REQUIRED. proto2 had required; proto3 removed it. Every field
//    is optional in wire terms and the zero value is indistinguishable
//    from unset. Consequences:
//    - you cannot tell "not provided" from "set to the default"
//    - you cannot distinguish a field explicitly set to 0/false/"" from
//      an absent field. Use `optional` for that:
        optional int32 discount = 5;   // explicit presence tracking
//    - this is why `google.protobuf.Int32Value` and friends exist:
//      wrappers carry presence for a non-optional field
//    - proto3 `optional` has been supported since protoc 3.15 and every
//      language now supports it. Use it rather than wrappers.

// 2. THE FIRST ENUM VALUE MUST BE ZERO AND SHOULD BE UNSPECIFIED
//    0 is the proto3 default, so omitting a field yields 0. If 0 were
//    a real state, every old client that did not know about the new
//    state would silently report it.
//    ORDER_STATUS_UNSPECIFIED = 0 forces you to handle "I do not know".

// 3. SCALARS HAVE NO NULL. Ever.
//    There is no null string. An unset string is "". If you need
//    "absent" versus "empty", use optional or a wrapper.

// 4. USE THE WELL-KNOWN TYPES, not your own
    Timestamp        not a string. (ch1)
    Duration         "1.5s", "500ms". parseable, and comparable
    Money            from google.type, or your own. Never a bare int64
    Date             calendar date only, no timezone
    FieldMask        for PATCH: which fields to update
    Empty            for methods returning nothing
    Struct/Value     dynamic JSON, use sparingly
    Any              a protobuf message you do not know. Dangerous.
    wrappers         Int32Value etc., for presence without optional

// 5. NAMING CONVENTIONS, which are not enforced and everyone follows
    messages      PascalCase        OrderLine
    fields        snake_case        customer_id
    enums         SCREAMING_SNAKE   OrderStatus -> ORDER_STATUS_PAID
    packages      lowercase dotted  orders.v1
    RPC methods   PascalCase        GetOrder
    service       PascalCase        OrderService
//   the enum prefix rule matters: enum values live in the enclosing
//   scope in C++, so an unprefixed PENDING would collide with another
//   enum's PENDING in the same package. The prefix is what prevents it.

// 6. FILE NAMING and package-per-domain
   orders/v1/orders.proto
   orders/v1/refunds.proto
//   one package per bounded context, versioned at the package level
//   (ch11). Files per service, not one giant file: a big proto file is
//   a compile-time and review problem.

Field numbers are the contract

This is the single most important rule in protobuf and the one that causes irreversible data loss when broken.

Reserve a deleted number, and its name

When a field is removed, its number must never be reused: an old client will still be sending it, and the new server will read those bytes as whatever now occupies that number. reserved makes the compiler refuse a future reuse, and reserving the old names too stops a typo reintroducing a field under a name a consumer still recognises.

message Order {
  reserved 4, 7 to 9;                    // numbers never to be reused
  reserved "old_status", "legacy_id";    // names too

  string id = 1;
  OrderStatus status = 2;
}
// protoc now rejects any new field that takes number 4
// or the name old_status. Try it and watch the build fail.

The canonical JSON mapping

protoc can emit and accept a canonical JSON form, and the gateway in a later section relies on it. Two facts surprise every client author: output uses lowerCamelCase even though the field is snake_case, and every int64 is rendered as a string, because a JSON number is a double and would lose precision above 253.

// protoc emits lowerCamelCase and renders int64 as a STRING.
const canonical = JSON.stringify({ customer_id: "42", quantity: 2 });
// {"customerId":"42","quantity":2}     <- note the rename

// Never round-trip a 64-bit id through a JS number.
Number("9007199254740993");   // 9007199254740992   precision gone

// ON THE WIRE, A FIELD IS ITS NUMBER. THE NAME IS ONLY FOR CODE GENERATION.

message OrderV1 {
  string id = 1;
  string status = 2;      // a string
  int32  quantity = 3;
}
message OrderV2 {
  string id = 1;
  OrderStatus status = 2; // NOW AN ENUM
  int32  quantity = 3;
}
//   Compatible. Old clients read 2 as a string and get the enum's
//   name. New clients read 2 as an enum and get a value they know.
//   This is how you change a field's type, and it works.

// RENAMING IS FREE FOR THE WIRE
message OrderV3 {
  string id = 1;
  OrderState state = 2;   // renamed from status. field number 2.
  int32  quantity = 3;
}
//   still wire-compatible. The generated JSON changes, which breaks
//   REST-gateway consumers, but gRPC-to-gRPC is fine.

// CHANGING A FIELD NUMBER IS DATA LOSS, SILENTLY
message OrderV4 {
  string id = 1;
  OrderState state = 3;   // WAS 2. renumbered by accident.
  int32  quantity = 4;    // WAS 3.
}
//   Old client sends status=1. New server reads field 3, which is
//   quantity. Now quantity is 1 for every order.
//   No error. No warning. Silent, permanent corruption.

// THE RULES
//   1. NEVER REUSE A FIELD NUMBER. Ever. For the life of the message.
//   2. NEVER RENUMBER. Delete a field by marking it reserved.
//   3. Reserve deleted numbers and names explicitly, which makes the
//      compiler reject a future reuse:
//        message Order {
//          reserved 4, 7 to 12;
//          reserved "old_status", "legacy_id";
//          string id = 1;
//          OrderState state = 2;
//        }
//   4. RESERVE THE NAMES TOO, so a typo cannot reintroduce a field
//      with an old name.
//   5. when you delete a field, the number is burned forever. Plan
//      numbering with gaps if you expect churn.

// THE SAFE CHANGES LIST
   ADD a new field with a new number: safe
   RENAME a field, same number: safe for gRPC, breaks JSON
   CHANGE a type, keeping wire compatibility: safe
      string -> bytes, string -> enum-as-string, int32 <-> int64
   WIDEN a numeric type (int32 -> int64): safe
   NARROW (int64 -> int32): NOT safe, truncation
   MAKE A FIELD REPEATED: NOT safe, wire-incompatible
   SPLIT a message into fields: NOT safe
   REMOVE A FIELD: wire-safe (the number is just unknown) but the
      sender's data is gone from the reader's view. Fine with a plan.

// THE JSON MAPPING, which surprises people
//   protoc can emit and accept JSON (canonical, lowerCamelCase):
     GET /v1/orders/1  -> {"id":"1","state":"PAID","quantity":2}
//   the JSON gateway (ch10-http) uses this.
//   THE TRAP: the canonical JSON name is lowerCamelCase (customerId)
//   while the proto field is snake_case (customer_id). Both are
//   accepted on input; output is lowerCamelCase. If your REST clients
//   have been using snake_case, they will see a rename you did not make.
//   Also: int64 is a STRING in JSON, because JSON numbers are doubles
//   and would lose precision. This surprises every JavaScript client
//   the first time and then forever.

Service definitions and streaming

Unary is the default shape

A unary RPC is one request and one response, and it accounts for the overwhelming majority of traffic. Design it exactly as you would a REST endpoint: a request message, a response message, and a name that says what it does. Because protobuf has no URL, the method name is the operation, so name it like one.

service OrderService {
  rpc GetOrder(GetOrderRequest) returns (Order);
  rpc ListOrders(ListOrdersRequest) returns (ListOrdersResponse);
  rpc CreateOrder(CreateOrderRequest) returns (Order);
  rpc CancelOrder(CancelOrderRequest) returns (Order);
}

Choosing the streaming mode

Server-streaming is one request and a sequence of responses — a watch, a tail, a progress feed. The client still needs a policy for when to give up, because gRPC does not time out on its own. Client-streaming is a sequence of requests and one response, and it is usually the wrong shape: batching is better as one request carrying a repeated field, since a stream has per-message overhead and a partial-failure question nobody answers. Bidirectional is the hardest to load-balance, proxy, retry and reason about; use it only for genuinely interactive traffic.

// server: a watch is server-streaming
func (s *server) WatchOrder(req *ordersv1.GetOrderRequest, stream ordersv1.OrderService_WatchOrderServer) error {
	for {
		order, err := s.store.Get(stream.Context(), req.GetId())
		if err != nil {
			return status.Errorf(codes.NotFound, "order %s", req.GetId())
		}
		if err := stream.Send(order); err != nil {
			return err
		}
		if order.GetStatus() == ordersv1.OrderStatus_ORDER_STATUS_SHIPPED {
			return nil   // the stream ends when the work is done
		}
		select {
		case <-stream.Context().Done():
			return stream.Context().Err()   // client went away or deadline
		}
	}
}

Versioning lives in the package name

There is no URL path to bump, so the version is the package: orders.v1 and orders.v2 coexist. Run both, ideally in one process with one implementation and a translation layer where the shapes differ. A generated client imports the package it wants, so an old consumer keeps working while a new one moves on.

// orders/v1/orders.proto
package orders.v1;   // stays frozen once shipped

// orders/v2/orders.proto
package orders.v2;   // the new shape; v1 keeps working
message Order {
  string id = 1;
  Money total = 2;
  ShippingAddress shipping = 3;   // the field that forced v2
}

// A SERVICE DEFINITION
service OrderService {
  rpc GetOrder(GetOrderRequest) returns (Order);
  rpc ListOrders(ListOrdersRequest) returns (ListOrdersResponse);
  rpc CreateOrder(CreateOrderRequest) returns (Order);
  rpc CancelOrder(GetOrderRequest) returns (Order);

  // STREAMING, and when each kind is right
  rpc WatchOrder(GetOrderRequest) returns (stream Order);      // server
  rpc UploadOrders(stream Order) returns (ListOrdersResponse); // client
  rpc Chat(stream ChatMessage) returns (stream ChatMessage);    // bidi
}

// UNARY, the default
//   one request, one response. 90% of your traffic. It maps exactly to
//   a REST endpoint, and you should design it the same way.

// SERVER-STREAMING
//   one request, a sequence of responses.
//   Use for: a watch, a log tail, a progress stream, a very large
//   result set you want to stream rather than buffer.
//   THE CLIENT STILL NEEDS A POLICY: at what point does it give up?
//   gRPC does not time out by default (ch10-deadlines). A server
//   streaming from a dead upstream will hang forever unless the client
//   sets a deadline.

// CLIENT-STREAMING
//   a sequence of requests, one response.
//   Use for: a bulk upload, a client-side batch.
//   THIS IS USUALLY A WRONG SHAPE. Batching is better as one request
//   carrying a repeated field, because a streaming RPC has per-message
//   overhead and a partial-failure problem:
//     what do you do with 5,000 messages where 3 failed?
//   If you use client streaming, define how partial failure is
//   reported. This is the question people forget.

// BIDIRECTIONAL
//   both directions, interleaved.
//   Use for: voice/video, interactive terminals, collaborative
//   editing, a chat protocol.
//   AVOID for anything request/response shaped. It is the hardest kind
//   to load balance, to proxy, to retry and to reason about, and it
//   has no useful HTTP analogue.

// THE RETRY QUESTION, which streaming makes harder
//   A unary call is naturally retryable, with the idempotency-key
//   discipline from ch5.
//   A server stream that was half-delivered cannot be resumed without
//   a cursor. If you need resumable streams, put a resume token in the
//   messages and accept that it is your problem, not gRPC's.
//   Do not retry a non-idempotent RPC transparently. gRPC's automatic
//   retry configuration is per-service and per-method; leave it off
//   unless the method is idempotent.

// VERSIONING IN THE PACKAGE NAME, which is the gRPC convention
package orders.v1;
package orders.v2;
//   a package is a versioned namespace. v1 and v2 coexist. There is no
//   URL path to bump, because there are no URLs. Consumers import the
//   generated package they want.
//   Run BOTH services, or run one service exposing both packages. The
//   second is usually better: one process, two generated interfaces,
//   one implementation, and a translation layer where the shapes
//   differ.

Deadlines, cancellation and errors

The single most valuable feature of gRPC, and the one most implementations get wrong. A deadline is a request-scoped timeout that propagates across the whole call chain.

Set a deadline on every call

A client call with no deadline hangs forever. That is the most common gRPC production incident: one hung upstream, then every in-flight call piles up until the connection pool is exhausted and everything fails. Put a deadline on the context of every call, and treat a missing deadline as a bug.

ctx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(context.Background(), 3*time.Second)
defer cancel()

order, err := client.GetOrder(ctx, &ordersv1.GetOrderRequest{Id: "ord_1"})
if status.Code(err) == codes.DeadlineExceeded {
	// the budget is spent; do not retry a non-idempotent write
}

Propagate the remaining budget

A deadline travels in the protocol, so every downstream call inherits it. The handler must derive the child deadline from what is left, not grant a fresh timeout: three layers each granting three seconds is nine seconds of work for a request that dies at three. Subtract the local cost you are about to spend.

func (s *server) GetOrder(ctx context.Context, req *ordersv1.GetOrderRequest) (*ordersv1.Order, error) {
	deadline, ok := ctx.Deadline()
	if !ok {
		return nil, status.Error(codes.InvalidArgument, "caller sent no deadline")
	}
	// leave 50ms for serialisation and the network hop back to the caller
	child, cancel := context.WithDeadline(ctx, deadline.Add(-50*time.Millisecond))
	defer cancel()

	return s.orders.ForCustomer(child, req.GetId())
}

Cancellation is cooperative

A cancelled call does not kill the handler; it signals it. Blocking work must select on ctx.Done() and stop, and the whole chain must stop together so no layer keeps computing a result nobody will read.

select {
case <-ctx.Done():              // deadline passed or caller cancelled
	return nil, status.FromContextError(ctx.Err()).Err()
case res := <-s.fanOut(ctx):    // the in-flight work respects ctx too
	return res, nil
}
Measure p99, not p50. A budget set to the median means half your requests exceed it, which is not a budget — it is a coin flip with a number attached.
// A DEADLINE IS A PROPAGATED TIMEOUT. THIS IS THE WHOLE POINT.

const deadline = new Date(Date.now() + 3000);
const order = await client.getOrder(
  { id: 'ord_1' },
  { deadline }                       // client sets it
);
// internally:
//   Client -> OrderService (deadline 3000)
//              -> InventoryService (deadline 2800, less local cost)
//                 -> Database      (deadline 2700)
//   if the database takes 3s, every layer sees CANCELLED or
//   DEADLINE_EXCEEDED, and nothing does useless work afterwards

// WHY IT MATTERS MORE THAN ctx TIMEOUTS
//   A context timeout cancels the call you started. A DEADLINE is
//   carried in the protocol: every downstream call sees it, and every
//   downstream deadline is the upstream one minus the local cost.
//   So a whole call chain stops together, and no layer keeps working
//   on a result nobody will read.

// THE RULES
//   1. EVERY CLIENT CALL HAS A DEADLINE. Without one, gRPC calls hang
//      forever. This is not optional and it is the most common gRPC
//      production bug: a hung upstream produces a hung service and a
//      connection-pool exhaustion that takes everything down with it.
//   2. THE CLIENT DEADLINE IS THE BUDGET FOR THE WHOLE CHAIN. Do not
//      give every layer 3 seconds; that is 9 seconds of work for a
//      request that dies at 3.
//   3. PROPAGATE. Set the downstream deadline to
//      deadline - timeAlreadySpent - estimatedLocalCost. Most
//      generated clients do this for you if you forward the deadline.
//   4. CANCELLATION IS COOPERATIVE. A cancelled call does not kill
//      your handler; it tells it to stop. If you are in a blocking
//      call, check for it.
//   5. CHECK THE DEADLINE BEFORE EXPENSIVE WORK, not just around I/O.
//      A handler that starts a 400MB scan 10ms before the deadline
//      wastes the whole budget.

// WHAT TO DO ON DEADLINE_EXCEEDED
//   - retry with a SHORT deadline and jitter, up to a budget. A
//     cascading timeout storm is a classic failure mode: everything
//     times out at once and retries in lockstep.
//   - if the deadline was yours and the upstream is slow, fail fast
//     and return 503 with Retry-After. That is far better than
//     holding a connection for 30 seconds.
//   - never retry a non-idempotent RPC on a deadline.

// THE TIMEOUT BUDGET, for a request that crosses four services
   total request budget        3000ms
     - edge/TLS/auth           100ms
     - own business logic       300ms
     - downstream fan-out      2400ms   (in parallel, not sequential)
     - serialisation           100ms
     - margin                  100ms
//   And the downstream fan-out is parallel, so 2400ms is per branch,
//   not the sum. If it is sequential, your 3000ms budget is fiction.
//   Measure p99, not p50. A budget set to the median means half your
//   requests exceed it.

Status codes, not exceptions

Map application errors to status codes once

gRPC has seventeen status codes and that is the whole error model. Decide the mapping from your domain errors to codes in one function, so every handler reports the same failure the same way. The mapping mirrors HTTP: INVALID_ARGUMENT is 400/422, ABORTED is a concurrency 409, UNAVAILABLE is the retryable one.

func codeFor(err error) codes.Code {
	switch {
	case errors.Is(err, ErrValidation):
		return codes.InvalidArgument     // 400 / 422
	case errors.Is(err, ErrNotFound):
		return codes.NotFound            // 404
	case errors.Is(err, ErrConflict):
		return codes.Aborted             // 409, retry once
	case errors.Is(err, ErrForbidden):
		return codes.PermissionDenied    // 403
	default:
		return codes.Internal            // 500, details logged, not returned
	}
}

Structured details beat a string

A bare throw new Error(...) becomes UNKNOWN with the text buried in the message field, and the caller cannot react. The standard answer for field-level validation is a google.rpc.Status carrying a BadRequest detail with one entry per offending field.

import "google.golang.org/genproto/googleapis/rpc/errdetails"

st := status.New(codes.InvalidArgument, "validation failed")
withDetails, _ := st.WithDetails(&errdetails.BadRequest{
	FieldViolations: []*errdetails.BadRequest_FieldViolation{
		{Field: "lines[0].quantity", Description: "must be at least 1"},
	},
})
return withDetails.Err()

A server must never return UNKNOWN

An unmapped exception becomes UNKNOWN, and in many runtimes the stack trace lands in the message field — an information leak and useless to the caller. Wrap every handler so unmapped errors are logged where they are safe and returned as a plain INTERNAL.

func recoverInterceptor(ctx context.Context, req any, _ *grpc.UnaryServerInfo, handler grpc.UnaryHandler) (any, error) {
	resp, err := handler(ctx, req)
	if err == nil {
		return resp, nil
	}
	if _, ok := status.FromError(err); ok {
		return resp, err                 // already a proper status
	}
	log.Error("unmapped handler error", "err", err)      // detail stays here
	return nil, status.Error(codes.Internal, "internal error")
}

// gRPC HAS TEN STATUS CODES. That is the whole error model.
  OK                     0   success
  CANCELLED              1   the caller cancelled or the deadline passed
  UNKNOWN                2   an exception with no mapping. AVOID.
  INVALID_ARGUMENT       3   the client is wrong. do not retry.
  DEADLINE_EXCEEDED      4   the deadline passed. retryable, maybe
  NOT_FOUND              5
  ALREADY_EXISTS         6   a unique constraint. a 409 in REST
  PERMISSION_DENIED      7   403
  RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED     8   quota or rate limit. retry, with backoff
  FAILED_PRECONDITION    9   the system state forbids it. a 409
  ABORTED               10   concurrency conflict. retry from the top
  OUT_OF_RANGE          11   an argument is out of range
  UNIMPLEMENTED         12
  INTERNAL              13   yours
  UNAVAILABLE           14   try again. the retryable one
  DATA_LOSS             15   unrecoverable
  UNAUTHENTICATED       16   401

// THE MAPPING FROM REST, which is worth writing down once
   400 -> INVALID_ARGUMENT
   401 -> UNAUTHENTICATED
   403 -> PERMISSION_DENIED
   404 -> NOT_FOUND
   409 -> ABORTED (concurrent) or FAILED_PRECONDITION (state)
   412 -> ABORTED
   422 -> INVALID_ARGUMENT
   429 -> RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED
   500 -> INTERNAL
   502 -> UNAVAILABLE
   503 -> UNAVAILABLE
   504 -> DEADLINE_EXCEEDED

// DETAILS, and this is where the value is
  throw grpc.status.PERMISSION_DENIED;
  throw new Error('user cannot cancel a shipped order');
// the second line is a dead giveaway. INTERNAL, and the first line is
// lost. The structured version is what lets a client react.

  const e = new Error('user cannot cancel a shipped order');
  e.code = grpc.status.FAILED_PRECONDITION;
  e.details = { currentStatus: 'SHIPPED', attempted: 'CANCELLED' };
  throw e;

// WHAT THE CLIENT CAN DO WITH EACH
   INVALID_ARGUMENT     surface it. retrying is pointless.
   NOT_FOUND            surface it, or treat as success for a delete
   ALREADY_EXISTS       surface it. the user must change the input.
   FAILED_PRECONDITION  surface it. do not retry.
   ABORTED              re-read, re-apply, retry ONCE.
   RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED   retry with backoff. it is a quota.
   UNAVAILABLE          retry with backoff.
   DEADLINE_EXCEEDED    retry with backoff, or fail fast and surface.
   INTERNAL             surface it as a failure and log it loudly.
   UNKNOWN              surface it as a failure and log the details.
//   This is the same table as ch2's retryability table. Same contract,
//   different encoding, which is the point: decide the semantics once
//   and let the transport carry them.

// THE THING YOU GIVE UP, and it is real
//   Rich error bodies. gRPC details are binary protobuf, so a
//   validation error with a field-level list does not fit. Two
//   workarounds:
//     - a google.rpc.Status with a google.rpc.BadRequest details
//       entry, which carries a field_violations list. This is the
//       correct answer and the tooling exists in every language.
//     - a normal proto message in a single field called `error`,
//       which is simple and is what many teams do
//   If you need field-level validation errors, use the first. It is
//   fiddly and it is the standard.

// THE BIG ONE: A SERVER MUST NEVER RETURN AN UNKNOWN STATUS
//   An unmapped exception becomes UNKNOWN with a stack trace in the
//   message field in many runtimes, which is an information leak and
//   is also just useless to the caller. Wrap every handler:
//     try { return await handler(req, ctx); }
//     catch (e) {
//       ctx.log.error({ err: e, req }, 'handler failed');
//       throw new InternalError('internal error');   // no details
//     }
//   and log the details yourself, where they are safe.

Interceptors: the middleware story

HTTP has middleware. gRPC has interceptors, and they are the right place for almost everything you would put in Express middleware.

Build the server chain deliberately

Interceptors run in the order you list them, and order is semantic: request-id must come first so everything below can log it, and panic recovery must be outermost-last so it catches failures from the rest of the chain. Authentication here is coarse — transport-level — while authorisation stays in the handler, because only the handler knows what it is touching.

grpc.NewServer(
	grpc.ChainUnaryInterceptor(
		requestIDInterceptor,   // first: everyone below can log it
		loggingInterceptor,
		authInterceptor,        // verify the principal early
		recoverInterceptor,     // last, so it catches the whole chain
	),
)

Client interceptors carry context outward

The client side injects the auth token, refreshes it, propagates the OpenTelemetry trace context, and applies the per-call timeout. Keep these few and simple: a retry interceptor belongs only on idempotent methods, and it is configured per service, never globally.

conn, err := grpc.NewClient("dns:///orders.internal:443",
	grpc.WithTransportCredentials(creds),
	grpc.WithChainUnaryInterceptor(authInterceptor, traceInterceptor),
)

Carry the principal in a per-request context

Reaching the authenticated identity inside a handler is the awkward part. The reliable pattern in Go is a context value set by the interceptor and read by the handler; JavaScript achieves the same with AsyncLocalStorage. Whatever the mechanism, never smuggle the principal through the message body.

type ctxKey struct{}

func principalFrom(ctx context.Context) *Principal {
	p, _ := ctx.Value(ctxKey{}).(*Principal)
	return p
}

func authInterceptor(ctx context.Context, req any, info *grpc.UnaryServerInfo, h grpc.UnaryHandler) (any, error) {
	p, err := verify(ctx)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, status.Error(codes.Unauthenticated, "invalid token")
	}
	return h(context.WithValue(ctx, ctxKey{}, p), req)
}

// AN INTERCEPTOR wraps every call. This is where auth, logging,
// metrics, retries and rate limits go.

function authInterceptor(svc, call, next) {
  const meta = call.metadata;              // the headers
  const token = meta.get('authorization')[0];

  // ASYNC ITERABLE, and getting this wrong is the classic bug
  return new grpc.InterceptingCall(next(call), {
    start: (md) => {
      md.set('x-request-id', call.requestId);
      next.start(md);
    },
    sendMessage: (message, md, ...rest) => next.sendMessage(message, md, ...rest),
    halfClose: () => next.halfClose(),
  });
}
//   NOTE WHAT IS MISSING. The naive version returns next(call)
//   directly, which skips the metadata injection. Return an
//   InterceptingCall when you modify headers.

// ASYNC, for auth that needs a database
function authInterceptor(svc, call, next) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    verify(call.metadata).then((principal) => {
      call.principal = principal;          // hmm. see below
      resolve(next(call));
    }).catch((e) => {
      reject({ code: grpc.status.UNAUTHENTICATED, details: 'invalid token' });
    });
  });
}
//   Passing the principal through call metadata is awkward. The usual
//   pattern is a closure per server instance, or a per-request context:
//     const ctx = new AsyncLocalStorage();
//     ctx.run({ principal }, () => handle(req))

// SERVER-SIDE INTERCEPTORS are where the real work is
  authentication       read metadata, verify, fail with UNAUTHENTICATED
  authorisation        the ownership check. per-method, because only
                       the handler knows what it is touching
  request id           read or generate, inject into metadata
  logging              method name, duration, status, principal
  metrics              call count and duration histogram per method
  panic recovery       catch a panic, return INTERNAL, do not crash
  deadline enforcement  fail a handler that ignores its deadline
  rate limiting        RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED

// CLIENT-SIDE INTERCEPTORS
  inject auth metadata   the access token, refreshed if needed
  load balancing hint    pick-first, round-robin, or weighted
  retry                  per the retry policy, idempotent methods only
  tracing                OpenTelemetry propagation

// THE METHOD METADATA, the gRPC replacement for a REST path
  // declare requirements in the proto, so the contract is in the
  // schema rather than in documentation
  rpc GetOrder(GetOrderRequest) returns (Order) {
    option (google.api.http) = { get: "/v1/orders/{id}" };
    option (google.api.method_signature) = "id";
  }

// LOGGING WITHOUT EVERYTHING BEING A STRING
   ctx.log.info({ orderId, principal: ctx.principal.id },
                'cancelling order');
//   The default logger stringifies everything. A structured logger
//   passed as the `logger` option to the server makes this work, and
//   it is what makes gRPC debuggable at all. Set it in the first hour,
//   not after an incident.

gRPC over HTTP: the gateway

The escape hatch that makes gRPC usable from a browser, and the reason you can have gRPC internally and REST externally from one implementation.

Annotate the proto so the gateway can build itself

grpc-gateway reads google.api.http annotations and transpiles the proto into a REST/JSON mux, so the HTTP contract is derived from the schema and cannot drift. A name in braces binds to a field in the request message; body: "*" maps the whole request body.

import "google/api/annotations.proto";

service OrderService {
  rpc GetOrder(GetOrderRequest) returns (Order) {
    option (google.api.http) = { get: "/v1/orders/{id}" };
  }
  rpc CreateOrder(CreateOrderRequest) returns (Order) {
    option (google.api.http) = { post: "/v1/orders" body: "*" };
  }
  rpc CancelOrder(CancelOrderRequest) returns (Order) {
    option (google.api.http) = { post: "/v1/orders/{id}/cancel" body: "*" };
  }
}

Map the statuses to HTTP in the gateway

The gateway returns gRPC statuses unless you map them. Without a custom error handler every failure reaches the browser as a bare status encoded in a protobuf-shaped error, so write the mapping down once and test it.

# grpc-gateway's defaults; override per status in your error handler
#   INVALID_ARGUMENT      400
#   UNAUTHENTICATED       401
#   PERMISSION_DENIED     403
#   NOT_FOUND             404
#   ALREADY_EXISTS        409
#   FAILED_PRECONDITION   409
#   ABORTED               409
#   RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED    429
#   DEADLINE_EXCEEDED     504
#   UNAVAILABLE           503
#   INTERNAL              500

Connect: gRPC semantics over plain HTTP

Connect (connectrpc.com) serves gRPC, gRPC-Web and its own JSON protocol from one handler. Unary calls are ordinary POSTs to /package.Service/Method, read-only calls may use GET — so the CDN can cache them — and errors are a JSON body with a real HTTP status. If you want gRPC's developer experience without its deployment constraints, this is the first answer to reach for.

# One handler, three clients. The JSON form is plain curl-able.
curl -sS -X POST https://api.example.com/orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder \
  -H 'content-type: application/json' \
  -d '{"id":"ord_1"}'
# {"id":"ord_1","status":"ORDER_STATUS_PAID"}

# Read-only RPCs also work as cacheable GETs.
curl -sS --get https://api.example.com/orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder \
  --data-urlencode 'id=ord_1'

// grpc-gateway TRANSPILES a proto into a REST/JSON API by reading
// google.api.http annotations from the schema.

import "google/api/annotations.proto";

service OrderService {
  rpc GetOrder(GetOrderRequest) returns (Order) {
    option (google.api.http) = {
      get: "/v1/orders/{id}"            // {id} maps from the body field
    };
  }
  rpc ListOrders(ListOrdersRequest) returns (ListOrdersResponse) {
    option (google.api.http) = {
      get: "/v1/orders"                  // query params from the body
    };
  }
  rpc CreateOrder(CreateOrderRequest) returns (Order) {
    option (google.api.http) = {
      post: "/v1/orders"
      body: "*"                          // whole body, or "lines" for
                                         // a partial body mapping
    };
  }
  rpc CancelOrder(CancelOrderRequest) returns (Order) {
    option (google.api.http) = { post: "/v1/orders/{id}/cancel" body: "*" };
  }
}

// generate
$ buf generate
//   protoc-gen-grpc-gateway produces a mux that maps HTTP to gRPC
//   in-process, so a browser request and a gRPC request hit the SAME
//   handler. One implementation, two protocols.

// WHAT THE GATEWAY GETS RIGHT
//   - one business-logic implementation, no divergence
//   - the REST contract is derived from the proto, so it cannot drift
//   - your browser clients get HTTP semantics: status codes, CORS,
//     caching, plain error bodies

// WHAT IT DOES NOT FIX
//   - ERRORS ARE STILL GRPC STATUSES. Map them to HTTP in the gateway,
//     or every error reaches the browser as a bare status code:
//       code      HTTP
//       INVALID_ARGUMENT    400
//       NOT_FOUND           404
//       ALREADY_EXISTS      409
//       FAILED_PRECONDITION 409
//       ABORTED             409
//       PERMISSION_DENIED   403
//       UNAUTHENTICATED     401
//       RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED  429
//       DEADLINE_EXCEEDED   504
//       UNAVAILABLE         503
//       INTERNAL            500
//   - NO CACHING. Every gateway call is a POST to one path, so the
//     CDN and the browser cache are as useless as they are for gRPC.
//     If you need caching at the edge, terminate it in the gateway
//     with explicit Cache-Control and an ETag from the handler.
//   - gRPC-Web is not this. gRPC-Web is a browser-compatible protocol
//     with base64 framing and a trailer-based status. It works
//     without a gateway, through Envoy, but you inherit gRPC-Web's
//     own limitations: base64 overhead, no streaming in some browsers,
//     and a proxy story you have to get right.

// THE SHAPE THAT WORKS
   browser / partner
        |
     [ HTTP/JSON ]  grpc-gateway mux, in the same process
        |
   OrderService implementation  <- handlers, interceptors, business logic
        |
     [ gRPC ]  to other internal services
//   One set of handlers. Two protocols. The gateway is thin.

Reflection and codegen

Debug with grpcurl and server reflection

With reflection enabled, grpcurl discovers the schema from the live server — no local .proto file — which turns it into the gRPC equivalent of curl. Reflection is reconnaissance for an attacker, so keep it enabled only in development and staging.

# reflection on (dev/staging): no -proto flag needed anywhere
grpcurl -plaintext localhost:50051 list
grpcurl -plaintext localhost:50051 describe orders.v1.OrderService
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"id":"ord_1"}' \
  localhost:50051 orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder

# production: disable reflection, and never expose the server's schema

Generate code with buf, not bare protoc

buf pins every plugin version in one config file and generates every language from it, reproducibly on any machine — no local protoc version drift. The registry and its lint pass are the parts that make protobuf maintainable; the generation itself is the boring bit you do not want to hand-roll.

# buf.gen.yaml
version: v2
plugins:
  - remote: buf.build/protocolbuffers/go
    out: gen/go
  - remote: buf.build/grpc/go
    out: gen/go
  - remote: buf.build/protocolbuffers/typescript
    out: gen/ts
# run: buf lint && buf generate

Run the breaking-change check in CI

buf breaking compares the schema against the last published version and fails the build on an incompatible change — the automated form of the field-number rule. It is worth the whole toolchain on its own: it catches a renumbering before it ships, which is the one mistake that corrupts data silently.

# .github/workflows/proto.yml
- name: Lint the schema
  run: buf lint
- name: Reject breaking changes
  run: buf breaking --against 'https://github.com/acme/protos#branch=main'
Generated code is not your code. Never edit it, never review it, lint it out (eslint --ignore-pattern '**/*.pb.go'), and treat a generator upgrade as a real change — method names and options shift between versions.
// REFLECTION: a server that serves its own schema
import { ReflectionService } from '@grpc/reflection';

server.addService(ReflectionService, {
  reflection: ReflectionService.createReflection({
    // only enable in dev and staging
    services: [orderServiceDefinition],
  }),
});
//   then grpcurl and grpc_cli can discover and call your service with
//   no .proto file: grpcurl -plaintext localhost:50051 list
//   THIS IS HOW YOU DEBUG gRPC. grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"id":"1"}' \
//     localhost:50051 orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder
//   or grpcurl -plaintext -import-path . -proto orders.proto ... localhost:50051 describe
//   With server reflection on, you do not need the -proto.
//   In production, keep reflection OFF. It is reconnaissance.

// grpcurl, and the equivalent tools
  grpcurl                    the CLI. reflection makes it self-sufficient
  grpcurl -d @               read a JSON request from stdin
  grpcurl -format=text       human-readable output, not JSON
  grpcurl -max-msg-sz 100M   raise the 4MB default
  GRPC_GO_LOG_VERBOSITY_LEVEL=99 grpcurl ...   verbose wire logging
//   Also: grpcurl -reflect -v, and BloomRPC as a GUI.

// CODEGEN, and the plugin matrix
$ buf generate                 // the modern tool. one buf.gen.yaml
                             // for every language, one version of
                             // every plugin
version: v2
plugins:
  - remote: buf.build/protocolbuffers/go
    out: gen/go
  - remote: buf.build/grpc/go
    out: gen/go
  - remote: buf.build/protocolbuffers/typescript
    out: gen/ts
  - remote: buf.build/grpc/typescript
    out: gen/ts
//   WHY buf AND NOT protoc DIRECTLY
//   - one config for all languages and plugins
//   - reproducible: plugin versions pinned in the config
//   - BSR (the Buf Schema Registry) can hold your schemas and do
//     breaking-change detection
//   - lint and format built in: buf lint, buf format
//   - works identically on every platform, so no local protoc
//     version drift

// THE BREAKING-CHANGE CHECK, and it is worth the whole toolchain
$ buf breaking --against '.git#branch=main'
//   fields 2 and 3 deleted    ERROR
//   field 2 renamed           OK for wire, WARNING for JSON
//   field 2 type changed      ERROR
//   enum value 3 removed      ERROR
//   field 4 added             OK
//   service method removed    ERROR
//   new required field        n/a, proto3 has none
//   wire-compatible change    OK
//   Run this in CI. It is the automated version of ch10-contract and it
//   will catch the renumbering mistake before it ships.

// CODEGEN IN PRACTICE
//   GENERATED CODE IS NOT YOUR CODE
//   - never edit it, never read it during review, add it to
//     .gitignore or commit it but mark it as generated
//   - lint it out: eslint --ignore-pattern '**/*.pb.go'
//   - its own package, so nothing else imports its internals
//   - your proto style guide belongs in buf lint's config, not in a
//     document people skip

The traps

A default deadline on every stub

Most gRPC incidents share one root: a client with no deadline and an upstream that hangs. Fix it once, centrally, in the interceptor every connection uses, rather than trusting each call site to remember.

conn, _ := grpc.NewClient(target,
	grpc.WithChainUnaryInterceptor(
		func(ctx context.Context, method string, req, reply any,
			cc *grpc.ClientConn, invoker grpc.UnaryInvoker, opts ...grpc.CallOption) error {
			if _, ok := ctx.Deadline(); !ok {
				var cancel context.CancelFunc
				ctx, cancel = context.WithTimeout(ctx, 3*time.Second)
				defer cancel()
			}
			return invoker(ctx, method, req, reply, cc, opts...)
		},
	),
)

The 4MB message limit is silent

gRPC's default receive limit is 4MB; a larger message dies with RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED and an "exceeded message size" message that reads like a quota error. A 40MB "get everything" call is a design smell anyway — page it, stream it, or return a URL — but when both ends agree, raise the limit on both sides or the failure just moves.

srv := grpc.NewServer(
	grpc.MaxRecvMsgSize(16<<20),   // 16 MiB receive
	grpc.MaxSendMsgSize(16<<20),   // 16 MiB send
)
cli, _ := grpc.NewClient(target,
	grpc.WithDefaultCallOptions(
		grpc.MaxCallRecvMsgSize(16<<20),
		grpc.MaxCallSendMsgSize(16<<20),
	),
)

Retries are off by default — and that is correct

gRPC deliberately does not retry: a transparent retry of a non-idempotent call is a duplicate charge. When you enable retry, do it per service and per method, only for idempotent operations, with a budget and backoff. This config binds GetOrder to three attempts on the two retryable statuses and nothing else.

{
  "methodConfig": [{
    "name": [{ "service": "orders.v1.OrderService", "method": "GetOrder" }],
    "timeout": "3s",
    "retryPolicy": {
      "maxAttempts": 3,
      "initialBackoff": "0.1s",
      "maxBackoff": "1s",
      "backoffMultiplier": 2,
      "retryableStatusCodes": ["UNAVAILABLE", "DEADLINE_EXCEEDED"]
    }
  }]
}

// 1. NO DEADLINE, and the cascade
   A client with no deadline + an upstream that hangs = the service
   accumulates in-flight calls until the connection pool is exhausted.
//   Then EVERYTHING fails, including calls that would have succeeded.
//   This is the most common gRPC production incident and it has one
//   fix: a default deadline on every client stub.

// 2. REVERSING PROXIES DROP HTTP/2 TRAILERS
   gRPC carries its status in HTTP trailers. A proxy or an ALB that
   does not forward trailers breaks gRPC: you get a 200 with an empty
// body and no status, which surfaces as UNKNOWN or INTERNAL.
//   Symptom: works locally, fails behind the load balancer, and only
//   for some calls. There is a compatibility mode for this:
//     env: GRPC_XDS_EXPERIMENTAL_RBAC=1
//     or force HTTP/2 trailers with h2c and set
//     GRPC_HTTP2_ENABLE_TRAILERS=1 (older runtimes)
//   Modern clients retry on this. Do not rely on it.

// 3. THE 4MB MESSAGE LIMIT, silently
   gRPC's default max receive size is 4MB. Send 5MB and the receiver
   kills the call with RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED and a message about
   "message size". That message is a common source of confusion
   because it reads like a quota error.
//   Large payloads are a design smell. A 40MB "get order with
//   everything" call will be slow, will block a worker, and will not
//   parallelise. Page it, stream it, or return a URL to fetch.
//   If you must: raise max_recv_msg_length on both ends, and
//   understand that you now need a backpressure strategy.

// 4. STREAMING PROXIES AND LOAD BALANCERS
   A long-lived bidirectional stream holds a connection. A load
//   balancer with a connection limit, or a proxy with an idle timeout
//   of 60s, will break it. Long-lived streams need:
//   - LB idle timeouts configured above your longest expected silence
//   - connection limits sized for concurrent streams
//   - reconnection and resubscription logic in the client
//   - and, ideally, a heartbeat: a periodic ping keeps the connection
//     alive through most proxies and detects a dead peer

// 5. EVERYTHING IS A STRING ON THE WIRE FOR METADATA
   Metadata keys are lowercase ASCII; values are strings. To pass a
   number, JSON-encode it or use a proto message field. A timestamp in
   metadata is a footgun.

// 6. GENERATED STUBS ARE NOT INTERFACES
   They are concrete classes with a connect method. That makes them
   hard to mock cleanly in tests. The usual answer: define your own
   thin interface over the generated client and inject that:
//     type OrderClient interface {
//       GetOrder(context.Context, *GetOrderRequest, ...grpc.CallOption) (*Order, error)
//     }
//   One interface per service, written by hand, three lines of work,
//   and it makes every consumer testable.

// 7. UPGRADING THE PROTO LAYER
   gRPC protocol versions are negotiated. A very old client and a new
   server can fail to negotiate and fall back to plaintext, which
   looks like an encryption bug.
//   Pin a minimum: require HTTP/2 and TLS, and reject plaintext
//   explicitly rather than allowing a fallback.

// 8. THE RETRY DEFAULT IS OFF, AND PEOPLE ASSUME ON
   gRPC does not retry by default, deliberately: a transparent retry of
   a non-idempotent call is a duplicate charge. So enabling retry is
// correct, per service, with a method whitelist, only for idempotent
   operations, plus a budget and backoff.
//   serviceConfig: {
//     methodConfig: [{ name: [{ service: 'orders.v1.OrderService',
//                               method: 'GetOrder' }],
//       timeout: '3s',
//       retryPolicy: { maxAttempts: 3, initialBackoff: '0.1s',
//                      maxBackoff: '1s', backoffMultiplier: 2,
//                      retryableStatusCodes: [UNAVAILABLE,
//                                            DEADLINE_EXCEEDED] } }],
//   }

Exercises 10

  1. Write a proto for an order domain, then run buf lint and fix every complaint. Then deliberately renumber a field and confirm buf breaking catches it.
  2. Implement a server interceptor chain: request id, structured logging, metrics, authentication, and panic recovery. Verify each fires on a successful call and on a panicking handler.
  3. Add a grpc-gateway REST mux in front of a gRPC service, map every status code to its HTTP equivalent, and verify with curl that the JSON shape and the error body are what you intended.
  4. Measure your call graph's latency and set a deadline budget per hop. Then deliberately make the deepest service sleep for 5 seconds and confirm nothing downstream keeps working after the deadline.
  5. Set up reflection in staging and use grpcurl with no local .proto file to list services, describe a message, and call a method. Then disable reflection and confirm the behaviour changes.

11 · Choosing a Protocol, and Combining Them

The comparison, honestlyA decision procedureThe edge protocol and the internal protocolThe BFF patternAsync and realtime protocolsJSON: the details that biteOther wire formatsErrors across protocolsMigrating a protocol

The choice is not one protocol for everything. Almost every mature system runs a different protocol at the edge and another between services, and the reason is about who is calling, not about which is technically better.

The comparison, honestly

The three axes that decide it

Everything else in this chapter follows from three questions. Who is calling decides whether binary framing and trailers are even allowed (browsers disqualify gRPC outright without a proxy). Whether the caller knows what it wants divides codegen'd, fixed-shape interfaces from query-shaping ones. And what breaks when it is slow decides whether you need streaming or push at all.

// Decide on three axes, in this order:
//   1 WHO calls?       browser/partner  -> REST/JSON or GraphQL
//                      your own service -> gRPC or strict REST
//   2 FLEXIBILITY?     fixed shape      -> REST/gRPC, codegen'd
//                      caller shapes it -> GraphQL
//   3 LATENCY?         push required    -> gRPC streams, SSE, WebSocket
//                      otherwise        -> request/response is fine

The honest default, by consumer

Write these down and you will stop re-arguing the choice in every design review. The defaults are not about which protocol is technically superior; they are about who has to live with the trade. A public API that changes its wire format is a public event; an internal one is a deploy.

// THE HONEST DEFAULT
//   public, partner, browser, unknown      REST/JSON
//   internal, you own both ends            gRPC
//   first-party UI on a changing domain    GraphQL, or a BFF
//   events to systems outside your control webhooks
//   above a load balancer, below a browser Connect or a gateway

//                    REST/JSON   GraphQL    gRPC      AsyncAPI/
//                                                 webhook
// browser support    yes         yes*       no**      n/a
// human readable     yes         no         no        yes
// cacheable          yes         no         no        n/a
// codegen required   no          yes        yes       no
// payload size       large       medium     small     small
// server CPU         moderate    higher     lower     low
// learning curve     low         high       medium    low
// N+1 risk           low         HIGH       low       low
// query flexibility   low         HIGH       fixed     none
// streaming           no          sub        yes       no
// auth model         rich        rich       metadata  signature
// spec discipline    loose       strict     strict    loose
//
//   * gRPC-Web exists and is workable, with compromises
//  ** needs a proxy, or gRPC-Web, or Connect
//
//
// THE THREE AXES THAT ACTUALLY DECIDE IT
//
// 1. WHO IS CALLING?
//    a browser, a script on a machine, or another service you deploy.
//    Browsers constrain everything: no trailers, no HTTP/2 control,
//    no binary framing. That single fact rules out gRPC for browsers
//    without a proxy, and rules out most of GraphQL's HTTP advantages.
//
// 2. DO THEY KNOW WHAT THEY WANT?
//    A service that knows its callers has one endpoint per operation
//    and a codegen'd contract: gRPC or a strict REST schema.
//    A client that needs to shape its own view: GraphQL.
//    Neither knows: REST with fields, or GraphQL.
//
// 3. WHAT BREAKS IF IT IS SLOW?
//    A chat message that arrives late is useless. A report that
//    arrives late is fine. The first needs streaming or push; the
//    second does not, and streaming infrastructure is a cost.

// THE HONEST DEFAULT
//   Public, partner, browser, or unknown consumers:  REST/JSON
//   Internal services, you own both ends:              gRPC
//   First-party UI on a complex, changing domain:      GraphQL, or
//                                                        a BFF
//   Events to systems outside your control:           webhooks
//   Everything above a load balancer and below a browser:
//                                                        Connect or a
//                                                        gateway
//   And REST is a perfectly good default for internal services too.
//   "gRPC is better" is not a reason to adopt it; "we have streaming
//   and 20k calls a second and we own both ends" is.

A decision procedure

Ask the questions in order

A decision procedure removes the taste test. The first question that gets a yes decides it, and the ordering matters: browser support dominates everything, so it is first. Streaming is comparatively cheap to add later, so it is third. If nothing binds, the default is REST/JSON behind a real OpenAPI document.

// Q1 browser or external consumer?  -> REST/JSON, GraphQL, or Connect
// Q2 caller shapes its own query, and you trust it? -> GraphQL, with limits
// Q3 streaming required?            -> gRPC (internal) or SSE/WebSocket
// Q4 do you own both ends?          -> if no, REST
// Q5 the domain is a graph the client traverses? -> GraphQL or a BFF
// Q6 is volume or latency the binding constraint? -> gRPC
// otherwise                         -> REST/JSON + OpenAPI. Not a failure.

The migration-cost hierarchy breaks ties

When two protocols are tied, the cheapest thing to change later wins. Renumbering a proto field and switching protocols entirely are in a different universe from adding an endpoint, and the hierarchy below is a good approximation of the blast radius.

// adding a REST endpoint                 minutes
// adding a GraphQL field                 minutes
// adding a gRPC method                   minutes, once codegen exists
// changing a REST field's type           breaks consumers (ch7)
// changing GraphQL nullability           breaks consumers, silently
// renumbering a proto field              catastrophic, silent corruption
// changing protocol entirely             a rewrite of every consumer

// ASK THESE IN ORDER. The first yes decides it.

// Q1. WILL A BROWSER OR AN EXTERNAL CONSUMER CALL IT?
   yes -> REST/JSON, or GraphQL, or Connect-protocol (which is gRPC
          over a normal HTTP/1.1 + HTTP/2 + gRPC-Web stack and gives you
          typed clients without the browser problems).
          gRPC proper is out unless you add a gateway.
// Q2. DOES THE CALLER NEED TO CHOOSE ITS OWN FIELDS, AND DO YOU TRUST IT?
   yes -> GraphQL. With ch9-depth's limits, without exception.
// Q3. DO YOU NEED STREAMING?
   yes -> gRPC (server or bidirectional). Or WebSocket/SSE, which are
          not APIs but are the right tool for a single stream to a
          browser.
// Q4. DO YOU OWN BOTH ENDS?
   no  -> REST. Codegen and strong typing do not help if you cannot
          push a schema change to the consumer.
// Q5. IS THE DOMAIN A GRAPH THE CLIENT TRAVERSES?
   yes, and the consumer is first-party -> GraphQL, or a BFF.
// Q6. IS VOLUME OR LATENCY THE BINDING CONSTRAINT?
   yes, and both ends are yours -> gRPC.
// otherwise -> REST/JSON with a real OpenAPI document. It is the most
//   understood protocol in software, it is debuggable with tools
//   everyone has, and it will not be the wrong choice.

// THE TWO ANSWERS PEOPLE GET WRONG

// "WE NEED gRPC BECAUSE REST DOES NOT SCALE"
//   Test this. A REST endpoint doing 5,000 req/s with connection
//   pooling, compression and a sensible framework is fine. gRPC's
//   advantage shows at 20,000+ calls a second across many services, or
//   with payloads where JSON parsing dominates.
//   Before adopting gRPC, find your actual bottleneck. It is often
//   the database, and gRPC will not fix that.
//
// "WE NEED GraphQL BECAUSE OUR MOBILE APP MAKES TOO MANY REQUESTS"
//   Test this too. A BFF with 3 aggregate endpoints, or adding field
//   selection to 3 REST endpoints (ch1, ch3), often solves it with a
//   fraction of the cost and none of the security surface.
//   GraphQL's real justification is UI complexity and multiple
//   first-party clients, not round-trip count alone.

// THE MIGRATION-COST HIERARCHY, and it should decide ties
//   adding a REST endpoint      minutes
//   adding a GraphQL field      minutes
//   adding a gRPC method        minutes, once both ends have codegen
//   changing a REST field's type breaking for consumers (ch7)
//   changing a GraphQL field's
//     nullability               breaking for consumers, silently at runtime
//   renumbering a proto field   catastrophic, silent corruption
//   changing protocol entirely  a rewrite of every consumer

The edge protocol and the internal protocol

Separate the public contract from the private one

The edge is REST even when the interior is gRPC, for five reasons: CDNs only understand HTTP GET; browsers cannot speak gRPC so a gateway exists anyway and might as well be a real HTTP API; rate limiting and shaping are REST-shaped jobs; debugging at the edge needs curl; and the edge contract is the one you must keep stable while the interior should be free to restructure in a single deploy.

//   browser / partner                internal services
//      |  HTTPS, JSON                    |  mTLS, protobuf
//      v                                 v
//   [ EDGE / BFF ]  --gRPC-->  [ SERVICES ]  --SQL-->  [ database ]
//   public contract                private, free to refactor
//   versioned, OpenAPI             rename a message, split a service

Connect unifies the two without a gateway

Connect keeps gRPC's semantics over standard HTTP: unary calls are POSTs to /package.Service/Method, read-only RPCs may be called with GET so they cache, and errors are a JSON body with an HTTP status. The same server binary serves Connect, gRPC and gRPC-Web, so one implementation covers browsers, partners and internal services.

# one handler, three wire protocols. The JSON form is plain curl.
curl -sS -X POST https://api.example.com/orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder \
  -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"id":"ord_1"}'
# GET is allowed when the RPC is side-effect-free, so the CDN can cache:
curl -sS --get https://api.example.com/orders.v1.OrderService/GetOrder \
  --data-urlencode 'id=ord_1'

Whatever the protocol, the edge must do these

The edge's job list is protocol-independent, and each item is a chapter elsewhere in this guide: TLS termination, authorisation, rate limiting with the right headers, correlation ids, response shaping, body validation, audit, and converting unhandled errors to a safe body. If a middleware stack is for anything, it is for this list.

// - terminate TLS, enforce HSTS
// - authenticate; authorise per resource
// - rate limit (ch6), Retry-After when shed (ch2)
// - request + trace ids propagated to every downstream call
// - shape responses: aggregate, filter, mask, cache GETs
// - validate the body before business logic
// - record an audit event per mutation
// - convert unhandled errors to a safe body (ch2)

// THE SHAPE THAT SCALES AND STAYS UNDERSTANDABLE

   browser / mobile / partner / webhook receiver
              |
              |  HTTPS, JSON, cookies or bearer tokens
              |  CDN in front: cacheable GETs, TLS termination, WAF
              v
     +------------------------+
     |  EDGE / BFF            |   public contract lives here.
     |  REST/JSON or GraphQL  |   versioned. documented in OpenAPI.
     |  auth, rate limiting,  |   this is what you can change, and
     |  request shaping       |   changing it is a real event
     +------------------------+
              |
              |  mTLS, protobuf, short-lived tokens
              |  deadlines, retries, circuit breakers
              v
     +------------------------+
     |  INTERNAL SERVICES     |   private. you own both ends.
     |  gRPC                  |   free to restructure: rename a
     |  or REST internally    |   message, split a service, change
     +------------------------+   the schema, in one deploy
              |
              v
          database, queue, third parties

// WHY THE EDGE IS REST EVEN IF THE INTERIOR IS gRPC
//   1. CDNs and caching only understand HTTP GET. Everything else is
//      uncacheable.
//   2. Browsers cannot speak gRPC. A gateway exists anyway, so let it
//      be a real HTTP API rather than a transliteration.
//   3. The edge is where you rate limit, authenticate, and shape
//      responses for many clients with different needs. That is a
//      REST-shaped job.
//   4. Debugging at the edge needs to work with curl.
//   5. The edge contract is the one you must keep stable. The interior
//      is one you should be able to change freely. Separating them is
//      what makes that possible.

// THE CONNECT PROTOCOL, which is the modern unifier
//   Connect (connectrpc.com) is gRPC's semantics over standard HTTP:
//     - unary calls are ordinary POSTs to /package.Service/Method
//     - GET is used for calls with no side effects, so it caches
//     - the same handler serves Connect, gRPC and gRPC-Web clients
//     - errors are a JSON body with an HTTP status code
//   So one Go or TypeScript service exposes all three, the browser
//   gets typed clients and proper status codes, and the CDN can cache
//   the GETs. If you want gRPC's developer experience without its
//   deployment constraints, this is the answer to reach for first.

// WHAT THE EDGE MUST DO, regardless of protocol
   - terminate TLS, and enforce HSTS
   - authenticate, and authorise per resource
   - rate limit (ch6), with the headers from ch2
   - request and response IDs, propagated to every downstream call
   - correlation, so one user request is traceable across 5 services
   - shape responses: aggregate, filter fields, mask, cache
   - validate the body before it reaches your business logic
   - record the audit event: who did what, to which resource
   - convert unhandled errors to a safe response (ch2)

The BFF pattern

A backend-for-frontend is the most underrated answer in this chapter, and it solves most of the problems people adopt GraphQL for.

Aggregate with per-field degradation

A BFF composes several services into one payload built for one screen. The failure mode matters: fan out in parallel with per-call deadlines, let each call fail independently, and degrade per field rather than failing the whole page. An all-or-nothing BFF makes the user experience worse than six parallel calls would.

app.get('/mobile-bff/home', async (req, res) => {
  const [feed, promos, unread] = await Promise.allSettled([
    feedSvc.home(req.user.id, { deadline: 400 }),
    promo.eligible(req.user.id, { deadline: 200 }),
    inbox.unread(req.user.id, { deadline: 300 }),
  ]);

  res.json({
    feed: feed.status === 'fulfilled' ? feed.value : [],
    promos: promos.status === 'fulfilled' ? promos.value : [],
    unread: unread.status === 'fulfilled' ? unread.value : 0,
    degraded: [feed, promos, unread].some((r) => r.status === 'rejected'),
  });
});

The one rule: no business logic

A BFF composes, shapes, caches and translates. The moment a business rule appears in it, the next BFF reimplements it differently and the two screens drift. The test is blunt: if the BFF has a database connection, it is a service, and the logic belongs in one.

// WRONG: a business rule in the BFF.
if (order.total > 1000) req.body.requiresManagerApproval = true;

// RIGHT: the rule lives in the service; the BFF asks and passes through.
const approval = await orders.needsApproval(orderId, { deadline: 300 });
res.json({ orderId, requiresManagerApproval: approval.required });

When GraphQL is still the better answer

A BFF is right for one or two first-party clients where you want reviewable, cacheable REST. GraphQL wins when many client teams each need a different shape and you do not want a BFF per team, when the domain is still being explored and a negotiation per screen is too slow, and when the client team is in another organisation where a BFF would be a boundary they have to go through.

// BFF when:        one UI, REST is fine, auth review per route matters
// GraphQL when:    many client teams shape their own queries
//                  the domain's shape is not known yet
//                  clients are external to your organisation

// WHAT IT IS
//   One backend per client type, owned by that client's team, which
//   aggregates and shapes for that client specifically.
//     /web-bff/products/:id      aggregates catalogue, pricing, reviews
//     /mobile-bff/home          one payload for the home screen
//     /admin-bff/reports/...    admin-specific aggregation
//   Each BFF is thin. Business logic stays in the services.

// THE TWO VARIANTS
//   A. AGGREGATION BFF: composes several services into one payload.
//      Replaces most of the round-trip problem.
//   B. BACKEND-ONLY UI-SPECIFIC: contains UI-specific logic, like a
//      template or a format decision, that would be awkward in a
//      general service.

// WHY IT IS OFTEN BETTER THAN GRAPHQL FOR THE SAME PROBLEM
   - the contract is REST: cacheable GETs, HTTP status codes, curl
   - authorisation is explicit and reviewable per endpoint
   - N+1 across services is one fan-out you control, not a query plan
   - no cost analysis to implement, no depth limits to get wrong
   - it is testable with HTTP-level tools and no GraphQL client
   - the failure modes are ordinary: one downstream is down, the
//     field is null, the response is partial with a documented shape
//   The cost: it is not self-service. A client cannot write its own
//   query; it needs a BFF change. For a first-party UI that is a
//   feature, not a limitation.

// WHEN GRAPHQL IS STILL BETTER
//   - many client teams, each needing different shapes, and you do
//     not want to run a BFF per team
//   - the UI is exploring a domain and the shape is not known yet. A
//     BFF is a negotiation per screen; GraphQL is a schema you design
//     once.
//   - the client team is in another organisation. Then a BFF is a
//     team boundary they have to go through.

// A BFF IMPLEMENTATION THAT DOES NOT BECOME A MONOLITH
   // the rule: a BFF contains NO business rules. It composes, shapes,
   // caches and translates. If a rule appears in a BFF, it belongs in a
   // service, because the next BFF will reimplement it differently.
   app.get('/web-bff/products/:id', async (req, res) => {
     const [product, price, reviews] = await Promise.all([
       catalogue.get(req.params.id, { timeout: 800 }),
       pricing.quote({ id: req.params.id, qty: 1 }, { deadline: 500 }),
       reviews.summary(req.params.id, { timeout: 800 }),
     ]);

     // degrade per field rather than failing the whole page
     res.json({
       id: product.id,
       name: product.name,
       price: price.ok ? price.value : null,
       rating: reviews.ok ? reviews.average : null,
       degraded: !price.ok || !reviews.ok,   // and say so explicitly
     });
   });
//   note the deadlines (ch10) and the per-field degradation. A BFF
//   whose failure mode is "all-or-nothing" makes the user experience
//   worse than six parallel calls would.

// THE TEST FOR A BFF GOING WRONG
   Does it have a database connection? Then it is a service and it has
   business logic. Move the logic to a service and give the BFF a
   client.

Async and realtime protocols

Server-sent events: the browser plumbing is done for you

SSE is a plain HTTP GET that streams text/event-stream — through ordinary proxies, no library on the client, automatic reconnection, and a Last-Event-ID header on reconnect so the server can resume where the client left off. Its limits are honesty: one-directional (which is right for notifications), JSON only, and a browser's six-connections-per-origin cap under HTTP/1.1, so one stream per notification starves the rest of the page.

app.get('/api/orders/:id/events', async (req, res) => {
  res.writeHead(200, {
    'Content-Type': 'text/event-stream',
    'Cache-Control': 'no-cache',
    Connection: 'keep-alive',
  });
  const lastId = req.get('Last-Event-ID');   // browser resumes here
  for await (const evt of orderEvents(req.params.id, lastId)) {
    res.write(`id: ${evt.id}\ndata: ${JSON.stringify(evt.data)}\n\n`);
  }
  req.on('close', () => { /* unsubscribe and free the listener */ });
});

WebSocket: flexible, and all the plumbing is yours

WebSockets give both directions over one persistent connection, and take away reconnection, heartbeats, backpressure, ordering and replay — you implement them. A reconnecting client has missed whatever happened while it was down, so the pragmatic pattern is a WebSocket for the live channel and a REST endpoint for catch-up: never rely on the socket for state.

socket.on('connect', () => {
  socket.emit('subscribe', { orderId });
  lastSeen = null;
});
socket.on('order.updated', (evt) => {
  lastSeen = evt.id;
  apply(evt);
});
socket.on('reconnect', async () => {
  // everything missed while down is fetched over REST
  const missed = await api.get(`/api/orders/${orderId}?since=${lastSeen}`);
  missed.events.forEach(apply);
});

Event streaming: the broker decides

For internal event delivery the choice is replay. Kafka's log and NATS JetStream replay history, which is what you need to build a new read model from the past; NATS core, MQTT and Redis pub-sub do not replay and are right when all consumers are live. Whatever the broker, define the event schema once — that is what AsyncAPI exists to do.

// browser, one-way notifications             SSE
// browser, two-way collaboration              WebSocket + REST catch-up
// internal, both ends yours, need replay      Kafka / NATS JetStream
// internal, no replay needed                  NATS / MQTT / Redis
// external consumer                           webhooks (ch8)

// FOUR DIFFERENT THINGS, OFTEN CONFLATED

// WEBHOOKS: you push an HTTP POST to a consumer's URL. ch8.
//   consumer is external. reliability is at-least-once and you manage
//   retries, signatures and delivery logs.

// ASYNCAPI / EVENT STREAMING: a schema for an event-driven interface.
//   MQTT, AMQP, Kafka, NATS. Consumers subscribe to channels and the
//   broker handles delivery, replay and fan-out.
//   right when both ends are yours and you need at-least-once with
//   backpressure. Kafka's log is the replay mechanism, which is why it
//   is the right tool for building a new read model from history.

// SERVER-SENT EVENTS: a browser opens a plain HTTP GET and the server
//   streams text/event-source responses.
   GET /api/orders/ord_1/events HTTP/1.1
   Accept: text/event-stream
   event: order.paid
   data: {"status":"paid"}

   id: evt_8f2a91
   data: {"status":"shipped"}

//   + works through ordinary HTTP proxies, no library, reconnects
//     automatically, and sends the Last-Event-ID header on reconnect
//     so you can resume
//   + one-directional, which is exactly what most notification needs
//   - no binary framing, so JSON only
//   - a browser limits concurrent connections to 6 per origin for
//     HTTP/1.1, so a few long-lived SSE streams can starve everything
//     else on the same origin. This is the one real limitation and it
//     bites apps that try to hold one stream per notification.

// WEBSOCKETS: bidirectional, persistent, and the most flexible.
//   + low latency, both directions, works for text and binary
//   - you must implement reconnection, heartbeats, backpressure and
//     ordering yourself
//   - no automatic replay: a reconnecting client has missed whatever
//     happened while it was down, so you still need a catch-up query
//   - an HTTP/1.1 problem, solved partly by HTTP/2 in future browsers
//   the pragmatic pattern: WebSocket for the live channel, a REST
//   endpoint for catch-up. Never rely on the socket for state.

// THE CORRECT COMBINATION
   socket open  -> subscribe to live events
   reconnect    -> GET /api/orders/ord_1?since=LAST_SEEN_ID  (REST)
   render       -> live events update the view, the catch-up
                   reconciles anything missed
//   SSE is the same idea with the plumbing already handled by the
//   browser, so use SSE unless you genuinely need the client to speak.

// THE DECISION
   browser, notifications, one direction      SSE
   browser, chat/collaboration, two ways       WebSocket + REST catch-up
   internal, both ends yours, need replay      Kafka or NATS JetStream
   internal, both ends yours, no replay needed MQTT or NATS or Redis
   external consumer                           webhooks (ch8)

JSON: the details that bite

JSON is the substrate of most APIs, and it has three genuine problems that produce real bugs.

64-bit ids and money must not ride as numbers

JSON has one number type and it is a double. A 64-bit id over 253 silently loses precision in every mainstream parser, and it shows up as an intermittent "record not found". Money is worse: never a float in any language, in any API. Minor units in an integer, or a decimal string.

// A 64-bit id must be a STRING on the wire, in every language.
JSON.parse('{"id":9007199254740993}').id;   // 9007199254740992  lost
JSON.parse('{"id":"9007199254740993"}').id; // "9007199254740993" safe

// Money: minor units as a small integer, currency beside it.
{ "total": { "amount": 1050, "currency": "GBP" } }   // GBP 10.50

Null, missing and empty are three different things

One encoding carries three meanings, and clients in different languages collapse them differently. The rules: never send null where you mean "no value" — omit the field; reserve null for "explicitly null"; and for PATCH make omission mean "do not change" while null means "set to null", the exact semantics of RFC 7386 merge-patch.

{ "a": null }   // present, value is null
{}              // absent
{ "a": "" }     // present, empty string
{ "a": [] }     // present, empty array

// PATCH: omission = leave alone, null = clear the field.
PATCH /v1/orders/1
{ "shipping_address": null }        // clears it
{ "status": "PENDING" }             // leaves shipping_address alone

The encoding details that surface as incident tickets

JSON is UTF-8 by specification; a mislabelled Content-Type is the usual cause of "the accented characters are broken". Duplicate keys are last-wins in every parser, so validate on ingest. No comments, no trailing commas, and no depth limit in the spec — cap nesting depth on untrusted input or a deeply nested document is a DoS vector.

# JSON is UTF-8 by spec; charset=utf-8 is redundant but harmless.
curl -sS https://api.example.com/v1/orders/1 \
  -H 'Accept: application/json' | jq '.id'

# Cap nested depth on input you do not trust:
# a "depth bomb" ({'a':{'a':{'a':...}}}) can reach gigabytes.

// 1. NUMBERS. The problem that reaches production most often.
   JSON has one number type. RFC 8259 says a number may exceed the
   range of a 64-bit double, and every mainstream parser will lose
   precision on it.

   the bug:
     // JavaScript
     JSON.parse('{"id":9007199254740993}')  // 9007199254740992
     // 2^53 + 1 is 9007199254740993. It parsed as ...992.
     // Your database row id has silently changed.
   Symptom in production: a record cannot be found, intermittently,
   only for large ids. It looks like a caching bug for a week.

   the rules
   - a 64-bit id MUST be a STRING in JSON, in every language
   - money MUST be minor units in an integer, or a decimal STRING
   - timestamps as ISO 8601 strings, or epoch SECONDS as a number.
     Epoch MILLISECONDS in a field called `timestamp` is a coin flip
     for whoever reads it. Name it `createdAtMs` or use ISO.
   - never use a float for money, in any language

   the double-rounding bug, which is worse and less known
   // Java, JSON -> double -> long
   long id = (long) json.get("id").asDouble();   // 9007199254740993
                                                        // -> ...992
   // SnakeCase, Jackson with a LongDeserializer configured for
   // strings, or store the id as a string in the first place.

// 2. NULL, MISSING, AND EMPTY. Three different things, one encoding.
   {"a": null}     present, and its value is null
   {}              absent
   {"a": ""}       present, empty string
   {"a": []}       present, empty array
//   A client cannot tell "the field was omitted" from "the field is
//   null" unless you document it, and different languages disagree:
//   Go's encoding/json collapses them for a struct field. Python's
//   dict keeps them. JavaScript's Object.keys distinguishes them.
//
//   THE RULES
//   - never send a null where you mean "no value"; omit the field
//   - use null for "this is explicitly null" and omit for "I am not
//     telling you", and say which in the documentation
//   - for PATCH, omission means "do not change" and null means "set
//     to null". They MUST be distinguishable. RFC 7386 merge-patch
//     has exactly this semantics; a plain JSON merge that conflates
//     them makes it impossible to clear a field.

// 3. STRINGS, and the encoding round trip
   {
     "unicode": "caf\u00e9 \u2014 \ud83d\ude00",
     "raw":      "café — 😀"
   }
//   both are valid and both parse to the same string. Which you emit
//   is a choice:
//   - ESCAPED: safest for terminals, logs, and anything that is not
//     UTF-8. No risk of a byte-order mark or a terminal mangling it.
//   - RAW: smaller, and readable in a diff.
//   Be consistent within a project. Mixed output is the real problem,
//   because a proxy or log viewer that guesses wrong corrupts it.
//
//   THE ACTUAL BUG: latin-1 vs utf-8
   Content-Type: application/json; charset=utf-8
//   JSON is UTF-8 by specification (RFC 8259), so charset=utf-8 is
//   redundant and some parsers treat a different charset as an error.
//   Include it and ensure your server does not lie. A mislabelled
//   Content-Type is the cause of most "the accented characters are
//   broken" reports.

// THE OTHER THINGS WORTH KNOWING
   - duplicate keys: last one wins, in every parser. Do not emit them,
     and validate on ingest because a client may parse differently.
   - comments: not allowed. This is why JSON Schema files and some
     config files ship as .jsonc.
   - depth: no limit in the spec, and a parser with no limit is a DoS
     vector. Cap nesting depth on untrusted input.
   - trailing commas: invalid. A trailing comma is a common cause of
     "works locally, fails in production" for a config file, because
