     the dev tool is lenient and Node is not.

Other wire formats

YAML and TOML are for configuration, not the wire

YAML's readability buys ambiguity: indentation is significant, and in YAML 1.1 the words yes, no, on, off and ~ are booleans or null — the canonical failure is the Norway problem, a country code NO parsed as false. TOML removes the ambiguity with real types and no indentation sensitivity. Neither belongs on the API wire; both belong in your CI and config files.

# YAML 1.1: unquoted scalars are booleans. This is the Norway problem.
country: NO      # parsed as false by a YAML 1.1 parser
country: "NO"    # a string, correctly

# TOML: unambiguous types, explicit datetimes, no indentation rules.
[server]
host = "api.example.com"
port = 8443

MsgPack and CBOR: only after you have measured

Both are compact binary encodings — CBOR self-describing, MsgPack schema-based — and both beat JSON on size and parse time. They earn their keep only when you control both ends and have measured JSON as the bottleneck. Otherwise they are a dependency plus a debugging tax for a hypothetical gain.

# Measure first. Same data, three encodings:
curl -sS https://api.example.com/v1/orders/1 -o order.json
wc -c order.json                               #  812 bytes JSON
msgpack-cli2 encode order.json | wc -c         #  470 bytes MsgPack
cbor2 encode order.json | wc -c                #  489 bytes CBOR
# If JSON is not your measured bottleneck, keep the readable wire.

Form data is not obsolete

application/x-www-form-urlencoded and multipart/form-data are what a plain HTML form can send without JavaScript, which is what progressive enhancement and accessibility depend on. If your API has even one HTML form, it needs both — or the no-JS form silently fails. Everything else should be application/json.

# application/x-www-form-urlencoded   a plain HTML form, no JS
# multipart/form-data                 a file upload from a form
# application/json                    everything else
# form-urlencoded values are percent-encoded:
name=Amin&query=api+design     # + means space in form encoding

// AVRO
   A schema-based binary format with schema resolution. A reader with
   an older schema can read data written with a newer one, using field
   names and defaults. That is genuinely useful for a data pipeline
   where producers and consumers deploy independently.
//   Not for an API. The schema negotiation requires shared state.

// MSGPACK / CBOR
   Compact binary, self-describing (CBOR) or schema-based (MsgPack).
   Faster than JSON, more compact. Used inside some RPC frameworks and
   by MQTT.
//   Choose if you control both ends and have measured JSON as a
     bottleneck. Otherwise it is a dependency for a hypothetical gain.

// YAML
   A superset of JSON that is far more readable and, in practice, far
   easier to get wrong: indentation is significant, `yes`/`no`/`on`/
// `off`/`null`/`~` are all booleans or null in YAML 1.1, `1.0` can be
   a float where you meant a string, and duplicate keys are allowed
   with last-wins.
//   Use YAML for configuration and CI files, where humans read them.
//   Do NOT use it as an API wire format. The Norway problem (country
//   code NO becoming false) is the canonical example and it is not a
//   joke.
//   If you must accept YAML: use a YAML 1.2 parser with JSON schema
//   (yaml-language-server's, or go-yaml v3) and validate.

// TOML
//   Unambiguous types, explicit datetimes, no indentation
//   sensitivity. A better fit than YAML for configuration, and it is
//   what Cargo.toml, pyproject.toml and Docker Compose use. Still not
//   an API format.

// FORM DATA, and the honest note
//   application/x-www-form-urlencoded and multipart/form-data are not
//   obsolete. They are what a plain HTML form can send without
//   JavaScript, and that matters for accessibility and for progressive
//   enhancement.
//   If your API has one HTML form, it needs both, or your form does
//   not work without JavaScript. Do not pretend otherwise and then
//   ship a form that silently fails.
//   Everything else: application/json.

// PROTOCOL BUFFERS, THrift, AVRO-CLASSIC
//   Thrift predates protobuf and has cross-language code generation
//   including Java, C++, Python, Go, JS and Rust. It is still used in
//   large stacks. Its IDL is less pleasant than protobuf's and its
//   ecosystem is smaller.
//   Protobuf wins on tooling, on the well-known types, and on the
//   ecosystem's adoption. Choose it if you have a choice.

export interface Order {
  id: string;              // NOT a number: 2^53 (ch11-json)
  status: 'PENDING' | 'PAID' | 'SHIPPED' | 'CANCELLED';
  total: { value: number; currency: string };   // minor units
  createdAt: string;       // ISO 8601 with Z
  customer: { id: string; name: string };
  lines: { sku: string; quantity: number; unitPrice: number }[];
}
//   the TypeScript shape, and note what it forced: id as a string,
//   the currency carried alongside the number, and the enum as a
//   literal union so a bad value is a compile error rather than a
//   runtime surprise. The type is where most API mistakes are caught.

Errors across protocols

The most important rule in this section: a failure must be representable in every protocol your system speaks, and it must mean the same thing.

Define the failure catalogue once

One source of truth listing each failure kind, its machine code, HTTP status, gRPC status, GraphQL code, retryability and the fields its message may interpolate. A BFF translating a gRPC failure to REST then does not invent a mapping — it looks up the catalogue. Nothing else survives a 3am incident.

{
  "invalid_transition":    { "http": 409, "grpc": "FAILED_PRECONDITION", "retry": false },
  "conflict":              { "http": 409, "grpc": "ABORTED",             "retry": "once" },
  "rate_limited":          { "http": 429, "grpc": "RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED",  "retry": true },
  "upstream_unavailable":  { "http": 503, "grpc": "UNAVAILABLE",         "retry": true },
  "timeout":               { "http": 504, "grpc": "DEADLINE_EXCEEDED",   "retry": true },
  "internal":              { "http": 500, "grpc": "INTERNAL",            "retry": false }
}

Translate in one function, tested per row

A gateway that speaks HTTP and gRPC needs a deterministic translation. Keep it in a single function with a test per catalogue row, and resist the temptation to add a bespoke case for "this one endpoint" — that is how a gRPC status leaks to the browser.

func httpStatus(code codes.Code) int {
	switch code {
	case codes.InvalidArgument:
		return http.StatusUnprocessableEntity   // 422
	case codes.NotFound:
		return http.StatusNotFound              // 404
	case codes.Aborted, codes.FailedPrecondition:
		return http.StatusConflict              // 409
	case codes.ResourceExhausted:
		return http.StatusTooManyRequests       // 429
	case codes.DeadlineExceeded:
		return http.StatusGatewayTimeout        // 504
	case codes.Unavailable:
		return http.StatusServiceUnavailable    // 503
	default:
		return http.StatusInternalServerError   // 500
	}
}

What must never cross a boundary

Stack traces, exception class names, SQL errors, file paths and an upstream's own error body leak internals and cannot be grouped. Log the catalogue code, not the interpolated message, or "how many invalid_transition errors today" is impossible to query.

// never send:  exception class name, stack trace, SQL error, file path,
//              an upstream's own error body, per-request message text
// always send: stable machine code, human message, retryability
// log:         the machine code, so failures group into one query

// THE CANONICAL FAILURE, AND ITS FOUR ENCODINGS

   "this order cannot be cancelled because it has shipped"

   REST/JSON              HTTP 409
     { "error": {
         "code": "invalid_transition",
         "message": "Cannot cancel an order that has already shipped",
         "details": { "currentStatus": "SHIPPED" } } }

   GraphQL                HTTP 200, payload error
     { "data": { "cancelOrder": {
         "order": null,
         "userErrors": [{ "code": "FAILED_PRECONDITION",
                          "message": "Cannot cancel an order that has
                                      already shipped",
                          "field": ["status"] }] } } }

   gRPC                  status FAILED_PRECONDITION
     { code: 9, message: "Cannot cancel an order that has already
       shipped",
       details: { currentStatus: "SHIPPED" } }

   gRPC-Web / Connect    HTTP 409
     { "code": "failed_precondition",
       "message": "Cannot cancel an order that has already shipped" }

// THE RULE: define the failure catalogue ONCE
   A single source of truth, in one package or one schema, listing:
     - a stable machine code: invalid_transition
     - a human message template
     - the HTTP status
     - the gRPC status
     - the GraphQL error code
     - the retryability
     - which fields the message may interpolate
//   Then a BFF translating a gRPC failure to REST does not invent a
//   mapping: it looks up the catalogue. This is the difference between
//   an edge that fails clearly and an edge that fails at 3am with an
//   untranslated INTERNAL.

   type FailureKind =
     | 'validation_failed'      // 422  INVALID_ARGUMENT   no retry
     | 'unauthenticated'        // 401  UNAUTHENTICATED    no retry
     | 'forbidden'              // 403  PERMISSION_DENIED  no retry
     | 'not_found'              // 404  NOT_FOUND          no retry
     | 'conflict'               // 409  ABORTED            once
     | 'invalid_transition'     // 409  FAILED_PRECONDITION no retry
     | 'rate_limited'           // 429  RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED retry, backoff
     | 'upstream_unavailable'   // 503  UNAVAILABLE        retry, backoff
     | 'timeout'                // 504  DEADLINE_EXCEEDED  retry, backoff
     | 'internal';              // 500  INTERNAL           no retry

// WHAT MUST NOT CROSS A BOUNDARY
//   - an exception class name from an implementation language
//   - a stack trace
//   - a SQL error, including the constraint name
//   - a file path
//   - an upstream's own error body, which may leak its internals
//   - anything that varies per request for the same cause, which
//     breaks log-based grouping and alerting

// THE ERROR LOGGER MUST BE ABLE TO GROUP
//   log every failure with the catalogue code, so "how many
//   invalid_transition errors today" is a query. If the log carries
//   the message text, you cannot group, because the message
//   interpolates ids. This is a small design decision that determines
//   whether your error dashboard works.

Migrating a protocol

Run gRPC beside REST on the same handlers

The migration is not a cutover; it is a second adapter on the same service class. The REST layer maps to DTOs, the gRPC layer maps to protos, and the mapping layer — the only duplication — is exactly where you want it. Both transports then share one business-logic implementation and cannot drift.

type OrderService struct{ store OrderStore }

// REST adapter: maps to DTOs
func (h *HTTPHandler) GetOrder(w http.ResponseWriter, r *http.Request) {
	o, err := h.svc.Get(r.Context(), r.PathValue("id"))
	writeJSON(w, toDTO(o), err)
}

// gRPC adapter: maps to protos
func (s *GRPCServer) GetOrder(ctx context.Context, req *ordersv1.GetOrderRequest) (*ordersv1.Order, error) {
	o, err := s.svc.Get(ctx, req.GetId())
	return toProto(o), err
}

Migrate internal callers one at a time

Each internal service switches from the REST SDK to the gRPC stub as its own revertible deploy. Do not remove the REST endpoint until its usage is zero — which means logging REST calls by caller, something worth having anyway. If gRPC is not better on the measured numbers for a given call, keep REST for that call: a gateway can cost more than the saving on a cheap one.

// 1 add gRPC beside REST, same handlers
// 2 switch internal callers one at a time, each revertible
// 3 measure p50/p99, error rate and CPU on both paths
// 4 keep a gateway so browsers and partners still work
// 5 deprecate the REST route for internal use; remove when usage is zero

The protocol change that is free

Moving HTTP/1.1 to HTTP/2 to HTTP/3 is invisible to the contract if you keep the same URLs, methods, headers and status codes and do not rely on 1.1-isms like chunked trailers or Upgrade. One gotcha: HTTP/3 is QUIC over UDP, so a firewall or proxy that blocks UDP silently keeps you on HTTP/2 — verify which you actually got before measuring a difference.

# Check which version you are actually on, not the one you configured.
curl -sSI --http3 https://api.example.com/v1/orders/1 | head -1
curl -sSI --http2 https://api.example.com/v1/orders/1 | head -1
# A UDP-blocking firewall silently downgrades http3 to http2.
Never change the protocol and the semantics in the same release. "It is gRPC now AND totals are in major units AND status is an int" is three breaks at once, and nobody can tell which one broke them.
// REST -> gRPC, the common case, done safely

// STEP 1: ADD gRPC BESIDE REST. Same handlers.
//   @GetMapping("/v1/orders/{id}")
//   public OrderDto get(@PathVariable String id) { ... }
//   @Override
//   public Order getOrder(GetOrderRequest req, Metadata md) { ... }
//   both call the same service class. The REST layer maps to DTOs,
//   the gRPC layer maps to protos. That mapping layer is the only
//   duplication, and it is where you want it.

// STEP 2: MIGRATE INTERNAL CALLERS ONLY
//   an internal service switches its client from the REST SDK to the
//   gRPC stub. One caller at a time. Each switch is a single service's
//   deploy, revertible on its own.
//   do NOT remove the REST endpoint until the usage is zero. That
//   means logging REST calls by caller, which is worth having anyway.

// STEP 3: MEASURE
   p50/p99 latency, error rate, and CPU on both paths. If gRPC is not
//   better for this call, keep REST. Not every endpoint improves, and
//   some get slightly worse because the gateway costs more than the
//   saving for a cheap call.

// STEP 4: ADD A GRPC-GATEWAY SO BROWSERS STILL WORK
   if the REST surface is public, it must stay. The gateway keeps it
   working against the same handlers.

// STEP 5: DEPRECATE THE REST ENDPOINT FOR INTERNAL USE
   mark it internal, keep it working, and remove it when nothing uses
   it. The public REST surface usually stays forever.

// THE THINGS THAT GO WRONG
//   - removing the old path before the new one is proven. Every
//     consumer you did not know about fails at the same time.
//   - changing semantics while changing protocol. "It is gRPC now AND
//     total is in major units AND status is an int" is three breaks at
//     once, and nobody can tell which one broke them.
//   - not logging per-caller usage, so you cannot tell when it is safe
//   - assuming the codegen output is API-compatible. A generator
//     upgrade changes method names and options. Pin it.
//   - no deadline, so the first hang cascades (ch10-deadlines).

// THE PROTOCOL CHANGE THAT IS FREE
   Changing your HTTP version. HTTP/1.1 -> HTTP/2 -> HTTP/3 is
   invisible to the contract, as long as:
     - you keep the same URLs, methods, headers and status codes
     - you do not rely on HTTP/1.1-specific behaviours (chunked
       trailers, Connection: keep-alive, header case-insensitivity
       quirks, Upgrade)
//   HTTP/3 is QUIC over UDP, so a proxy or a firewall that blocks UDP
   will silently keep you on HTTP/2. Check whether you actually got it
   before you measure a difference.

Exercises 11

  1. Answer the decision procedure for your own system and write down which question decided it. Then argue the opposite case and identify what evidence would settle the disagreement.
  2. Build a BFF endpoint that fans out to three services with per-field degradation, deadlines on every call, and a degraded flag. Fail one downstream and verify the page still renders.
  3. Write the failure catalogue for your system: code, HTTP status, gRPC status, GraphQL code, retryability. Then implement a translator from gRPC to REST in a gateway and verify every row.
  4. Demonstrate the JSON 64-bit precision problem in two languages, then fix your own API's id types and check every client you control.
  5. Add server-sent events for a notification stream and verify automatic reconnection with Last-Event-ID resumes without gaps. Then open six streams on one origin and observe the connection limit.

12 · Performance and Latency Budgets

Write the budget down firstWhere API latency actually goesOptimise p99, not the meanPayload size and compressionConnections and poolingDatabase-shaped latencyCaching layers, honestlyRate limits and queuesMeasuring an endpoint properlyThe tail, and why it dominates

An API performance budget is a design decision made before you write code. This chapter is about deciding what latency is acceptable, finding where the time actually goes, and avoiding the optimisations that cost more than they save.

Write the budget down first

A budget is a number per operation class

A budget is a promise: this class of operation will return within this p99. Write the numbers down before the endpoint exists, per class, at p99, measured at the server excluding network. The table makes "how fast should this be" a decision instead of an argument after the code is written.

// read one record         50ms   p99, at the server, excl. network
// list a page            150ms   p99
// write one record       100ms   p99
// a search               500ms   p99
// anything asynchronous  202 + a poll interval
// anything a user waits  10 seconds: 202 immediately

The budget includes what you do not control

"My handler is 50ms" does not mean the user sees 50ms. Design top-down: client render, TLS, DNS, network RTT, edge, handler, database. Budgeting the whole journey shows what is actually left for the part you own, and makes a 205ms total instead of a surprise.

// client render            20ms
// TLS (keep-alive)         30ms   100ms+ without keep-alive
// DNS (cached)              5ms
// network RTT              30ms
// edge / gateway           10ms
// handler                  50ms   <- the part you own
// database                 40ms
// network to origin        20ms
// ------------------------------
// total                  ~205ms

Put the budget in a test or an alert

A budget nobody checks is an aspiration. The cheapest protection is a load-test threshold asserting p99 per route — not per service, because a single global percentile hides the report endpoint dragging the number up while the fast ones are fine. The worse regression this prevents: a third downstream call added to a 50ms endpoint without a redesign.

// k6 thresholds turn the budget into a failing build.
export const options = {
  thresholds: {
    'http_req_duration{route:get_order}':   ['p(99)<150'],
    'http_req_duration{route:list_orders}': ['p(99)<300'],
  },
};

// A BUDGET IS A NUMBER PER OPERATION CLASS, AND IT IS A PROMISE
//   read one record        50ms   p99, at the server, excluding network
//   list a page            150ms  p99
//   write one record       100ms  p99
//   a search               500ms  p99
//   anything asynchronous  202 + a poll interval
//   anything a user waits  10 seconds: 202 immediately

// WHY p99 AND NOT THE MEAN
//   p50 is the experience of a lucky request. p99 is the experience of
//   the 1-in-100 request, which in a system with 10k requests a
//   second is 100 requests a second that users notice.
//   p99.9 is where the outages are: connection pool exhaustion,
//   GC pauses, lock contention, a cold cache. Those affect a large
//   enough group of people to generate tickets.

// THE BUDGET HAS TO INCLUDE THE PARTS YOU DO NOT CONTROL
   client render          20ms
   TLS handshake          30ms   (amortised by keep-alive; 100ms+ when
                                  not, which is the real cost)
   DNS                    5ms    (cached)
   network RTT            30ms   (0.3% of requests miss the cache and
                                  go to an origin 200ms away)
   edge / gateway         10ms
   your handler           50ms   <- the number you control
   database               40ms
   + network to origin    20ms
   ----------------------
   total                  ~205ms
//   So "my handler is 50ms" does not mean the user sees 50ms. Design
//   the budget top-down and you know what is available for the part
//   you own.

// THE RULE THAT PREVENTS THE MOST REGRESSION
   Every new feature must fit inside the existing budget. Adding an
   third downstream call to a 50ms endpoint does not get 100ms. It
// gets a redesign: parallel, cache, or make it asynchronous.
//   A budget nobody checks is an aspiration. Put it in a test or an
//   alert. A load test asserting p99 < 150ms for the ten most-used
   endpoints is the cheapest performance protection there is.

// WHERE THE NUMBERS COME FROM
   - your own monitoring, p99 per route, not per service
   - a percentile is only meaningful with enough samples. 100 requests
     in a CI run gives you a meaningless p99. Use a real load test for
     that, and monitoring for production.
//   - p99 per route AND per endpoint class. A global p99 across every
     route hides a report endpoint dragging the number up while the
     fast ones are fine.

Where API latency actually goes

The typical split for a read-one-return-JSON call

Know the ratios before you optimise. The database is the biggest single item at 20–40%, but "the database" is not the problem — the query pattern is. Logging and tracing can quietly consume 5–15%, and framework overhead often exceeds the code you actually wrote. These are the three places a profiler points at first, and they are usually not where the team guessed.

// TLS handshake            1-3%   amortised by keep-alive
// network RTT             10-25%  the floor you cannot beat
// auth                     2-5%   JWT verify is microseconds;
//                                 a session lookup is a database call
// business logic          10-20%
// database                20-40%  the biggest single item
// serialisation           5-10%   JSON encoding is not free
// logging/metrics/tracing 5-15%   larger than people expect
// framework overhead       5-20%  framework-dependent

Check the query plan before anything else

A correctly indexed query is ~1ms; an unindexed one over ten million rows is seconds, and no cache, replica or bigger instance fixes it. EXPLAIN names the failure in one run. If the plan shows a Seq Scan on a large table, the index — not the CPU, not the pool, not the cache — is the fix.

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT id, total FROM orders
WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND created_at > $2
ORDER BY created_at DESC
LIMIT 50;
-- Seq Scan on orders (cost=0.00..270000.00 rows=2000000)
--   -> missing composite index on (tenant_id, created_at)
-- CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY ON orders (tenant_id, created_at DESC);

Name the cost with a profiler, not a guess

Guessing at performance reliably produces work that does nothing. A continuous profiler names the function in minutes; distributed tracing splits handler, query, downstream and serialisation into separate spans; a flame graph shows all three findings above at once. If you have none of these, add tracing first.

py-spy record -o profile.svg --pid "$PID"     # Python, sampled CPU
go tool pprof -http=:8080 \
  http://svc:6060/debug/pprof/profile?seconds=30
dotnet-trace collect --process-id "$PID" --duration 00:00:20
# then read spans: handler, each query, each downstream call, serialisation

// FOR A TYPICAL "READ ONE THING, RETURN JSON" REQUEST
   TLS handshake           1-3%   amortised with keep-alive
   network RTT             10-25% and the floor you cannot beat
   authentication          2-5%   a JWT signature check is microseconds;
                                  a database-backed session check is
                                  not, and that is a real design choice
   authorise (ownership)   1-3%
   business logic          10-20%
   database                20-40% and the biggest single item
   serialisation           5-10%  JSON encoding is not free
   logging/metrics/tracing 5-15%  and much larger than people expect
   framework overhead      5-20%  and depends entirely on the framework

// THE THREE FINDINGS THAT CHANGER MOST TEAMS' PRIORITIES

// 1. THE DATABASE IS NOT USUALLY THE PROBLEM. The QUERY PATTERN is.
   A correctly indexed query is 1ms. An unindexed one on 10 million
   rows is 8 seconds and will not be fixed by a faster CPU, a bigger
   instance, a read replica, or a cache in front of it.
//   Check EXPLAIN before anything else. Every time.

// 2. LOGGING AND TRACING CAN BE 10%+ and IS USUALLY UNOPTIMISED
   - synchronous, per-request logging with a flush
   - logging an entire request or response body
   - serialising a large object into a log line "for context"
//   - a tracing span per function, where a per-operation span is
//     enough
//   - a metrics library incrementing 200 times per request
//   - log serialisation happening even when the level is filtered
//     (a logger that formats before checking is not lazy)
//   Measure it. A profiler names it in five minutes; a guess takes a
//   week and is usually wrong.

// 3. THE FRAMEWORK'S OVERHEAD IS LARGER THAN THE CODE YOU WROTE
   A minimal framework doing routing, auth, validation, serialisation
   and DI can cost more than the handler. That is not a reason to
   rewrite; it is a reason to know the ratio, and to know which layer
//   you are paying for. Moving validation from the framework to a
   compiled schema validator, or dropping a reflection-based serialiser
   for a codegen one, is usually a 2-5x win on the serialisation
   half.

// WHERE TO LOOK INSTEAD OF GUESSING
   1. A continuous profiler (py-spy, pprof, async-profiler, dotnet-trace).
      CPU time per function, sampled. This finds the answer in
      minutes and it is right.
   2. Distributed tracing with spans around: handler, each database
      query, each downstream call, and serialisation separately.
      You need the database time AS a span, not just as a log line.
   3. A flame graph. Three of the findings above are visible in one.
   4. Query-level database timings from the engine itself, not from
      your ORM's log.
//   If you have none of these, add tracing first. Guessing at
//   performance is the least efficient activity in software
//   engineering, and it reliably produces work that does nothing.

Optimise p99, not the mean

What produces a bad tail, in order of frequency

The mean is a lie: a request that takes 500ms one percent of the time leaves a mean of 55ms that looks fine. The tail is made of runtime pauses, cold caches, pool exhaustion, cross-zone calls, slow dependencies, lock contention and sync I/O. List the causes in this order and you will stop "optimising" the handler that was never the problem.

// 1 GC / runtime pauses      10-100ms, periodic, hits in-flight requests
// 2 cold caches              a periodic cliff at every TTL
// 3 connection-pool exhaustion p50 fine, p99 seconds
// 4 cross-AZ / cross-region  a 2ms call becomes 40ms on a bad route
// 5 a downstream's own tail  your p99 is the sum of their p99s
// 6 lock contention          one mutex on a hot path
// 7 slow logs / sync I/O

Size the pool with Little's Law, then bound the queue

L = lambda x W: a throughput of 500 req/s at 20ms service time needs ten connections, not forty. A pool bigger than the database can serve just moves the queue into the database. The order of fixes matters: make the query faster first, bound the queue and fail fast with 503 second, cache third, and only then add connections.

// Little's Law: L = lambda x W
//   throughput     500 req/s
//   service time    20 ms
//   connections = 500 x 0.020 = 10   + headroom, not 4x headroom
// 1 make the query faster, so the pool size falls out
// 2 bound the queue, return 503 with Retry-After
// 3 cache, so fewer requests need the database at all
// 4 only then, add connections

Reject instead of queueing forever

Queuing is invisible in the p50 and lives in the p99: requests wait, memory grows, timeouts fire. When the pool is full, fail fast with 503 and a retry hint. A bounded semaphore is the whole mechanism — acquire with a deadline, and reject on the default branch.

sem := make(chan struct{}, 10)      // the bound

func acquire(ctx context.Context) error {
	select {
	case sem <- struct{}{}:
		return nil                    // got a slot
	case <-ctx.Done():
		return status.Error(codes.DeadlineExceeded, "busy")
	default:
		return status.Error(codes.ResourceExhausted, "pool full, retry")
	}
}

// THE MEAN IS A LIE, and the gap between mean and p99 is where your
// users' complaints live.

   a request that takes 500ms 1% of the time:
     mean   55ms      looks fine
     p50    12ms      looks fine
     p99    500ms     the actual problem
     p99.9  4000ms    the actual outage

// WHAT PRODUCES A BAD TAIL, IN ORDER OF FREQUENCY
   1. GARBAGE COLLECTION / RUNTIME PAUSES. 10-100ms, periodic, and
      they hit every request that happens to be in flight.
//      mitigations: reduce allocation, tune the heap, use a concurrent
//      collector, or a runtime without a stop-the-world phase.
//   2. COLD CACHES. A cache with a short TTL has a periodic cliff.
//   3. CONNECTION POOL EXHAUSTION. Queuing: p50 fine, p99 seconds.
//      The queue is invisible unless you measure queue depth and
//      wait time, not just response time.
//   4. CROSS-AZONE AND CROSS-REGION CALLS. A 2ms call becomes 40ms
//      with an unlucky route. Freezing a region to one AZ removes
//      this entirely and is the cheapest p99 improvement available to
//      most teams.
//   5. A DOWNSTREAM'S DOWNSTREAM. Your p99 is the sum of everyone
//      else's p99s, and a dependency with a bad tail dominates you.
//   6. LOCK CONTENTION. A single mutex on a hot path.
//   7. SLOW LOGS AND SYNC I/O.

// WHAT ACTUALLY MOVES p99
   - remove the GC pause: biggest single win in most managed runtimes
   - make the cold path impossible: pre-warm, or avoid the cold path
   - bound the queue: fail fast with 503 rather than queueing for 30s
   - eliminate cross-AZ hops in the request path
   - set timeouts on every downstream call so one bad dependency
     cannot become a slow one
   - shed load early: reject fast rather than queue

// WHAT DOES NOT MOVE p99
   - micro-optimising the handler. If the handler is 2ms, making it
//     1.2ms changes nothing about a 500ms tail.
//   - a bigger instance. It lowers CPU-bound latency and does nothing
//     about GC pauses, locks, network, or a slow dependency. It also
//     raises cost per request and makes the tail worse under load
//     because fewer instances means less queueing.
//   - "reducing allocations" in a language where allocations are
//     cheap. Measure whether allocation is actually the problem
//     before you write unsafe code.

// THE CONNECTION POOL, because it causes the worst tails
   pool size 20, 200 concurrent requests
     -> 180 requests waiting for a connection
     -> p99 is 180 / (throughput per connection) seconds
//   This is a queue and it is not free: those connections are held,
//   memory grows, and timeouts fire.
//   Sizing: Little's Law. L = lambda x W.
//     throughput   500 requests/second
//     service time 20ms
//     connections  = 500 x 0.020 = 10
//   Add headroom, but understand that a pool larger than the database
//   can serve just moves the queue into the database.
//   The correct answers, in order:
//     1. make queries faster, so the pool size falls out
//     2. bound the queue and return 503 with Retry-After
//     3. cache, so fewer requests need the database at all
//     4. only then, add connections

Payload size and compression

Compression with a correct negotiation

A JSON response compresses 5–10x, which is the highest-ratio optimisation available — but the negotiation must be right. Vary: Accept-Encoding is mandatory: without it a shared cache serves the brotli response to a client that asked for gzip and that client fails to parse it. Below ~1KB the header and CPU cost exceed the saving.

GET /v1/orders HTTP/1.1
Accept-Encoding: gzip, br, zstd

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Encoding: br
Vary: Accept-Encoding
Content-Length: 2410        // the COMPRESSED length

Static beats dynamic when the shape is finite

Dynamic compression costs real CPU per request — brotli at level 5 is measurable. For an API with a fixed schema and a finite number of shapes, compress at build time: zero per-request CPU and the artefacts are CDN-cacheable forever. And for big payloads gzip is not enough: its 32KB window means a large JSON array compresses poorly, where brotli's much larger window does not.

# Build-time compression: zero per-request CPU.
brotli -q 11 -o orders.json.br orders.json

# gzip's window is 32 KB; a large array defeats it.
gzip -9  -c orders.json | wc -c    # 1,400,000
brotli -q 5 -c orders.json | wc -c #   380,000

Compression is a DoS surface

A tiny request that decompresses to a hundred megabytes is a decompression bomb, and it has been used against real APIs. Cap the decompressed size in the framework, cap Content-Length on uploads, and reject absurd compression windows. The prefix of every mitigation is the same: the server controls what it is willing to expand.

# nginx: bound the request body; do not compress below ~1 KB.
client_max_body_size 2m;

gzip on;
gzip_min_length 1024;
gzip_types application/json;
# and a decompression cap on the app side for gzip/brotli/zstd bodies.

// COMPRESSION IS THE HIGHEST RATIO OPTIMISATION AVAILABLE
   a JSON API response compresses 5-10x. It costs CPU on both ends and
   saves bandwidth and time-to-first-byte, which matters most on
   mobile.

// WHEN TO COMPRESS
   - responses over ~1KB. Below that the header and CPU cost exceed
     the saving.
//   - any response a mobile client fetches over a real network.
//   - NOT already-compressed content: images, video, zip, gzip,
//     brotli-compressed assets. Compressing those is waste.
//   - NOT a response the client will decompress immediately and throw
//     away (a health check).

// THE NEGOTIATION, and getting it wrong is common
GET /v1/orders
Accept-Encoding: gzip, deflate, br, zstd

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Encoding: br
Vary: Accept-Encoding
Content-Length: 2410      // the COMPRESSED length
//   Vary is mandatory. Without it a shared cache will serve the
//   brotli response to a client that did not ask for it, and that
//   client will fail to parse it.

// BROTLI AND ZSTD, and the static/dynamic decision
   - compress the response on the fly (dynamic): per-request CPU, but
     always optimal for that body. Cost is real: brotli level 5 costs
     measurable CPU. Cache the compressed form or use a lower level.
//   - compress at build time (static): zero per-request CPU, and the
//     artefacts are CDN-cacheable forever. This is the right choice for
//     an API with a fixed schema and a finite number of shapes, and it
//     is how a CDN-compressed asset pipeline works.
//   - zstd is faster than brotli at the same ratio and is now widely
//     supported. If your clients can take it, prefer it; gzip is the
//     universal fallback.

// GZIP IS NOT ENOUGH FOR BIG PAYLOADS
   gzip's window is 32KB, so a large JSON array compresses poorly
   because the data is far apart. Brotli's window is much larger.
//   A 5MB order history: gzip 1.4MB, brotli+zstd under 400KB.

// THE SIZE PROBLEMS, AND THEIR FIXES
   sparse fieldsets   ?fields=id,total,status   (ch3). Works, but
                     explodes your cache-key cardinality. Two fixed
                     endpoints are usually better.
   short keys         {"n":"Ada","e":"a@b.c"}   ugly, breaks your
                     readability and your SDK. Do not.
   omit nulls         {"a":1,"b":null} -> {"a":1}  saves a little on
                     most payloads and a lot on sparse ones. It breaks
                     the "absent vs null" distinction from ch11-json,
                     so pick one deliberately.
   gzip the whole
   response           the default answer, and often the whole answer.

// COMPRESSION AND DOS, which is the security note
   A tiny request that decompresses to 100MB is a decompression bomb.
//   Mitigations:
//     - cap the decompressed size on the server (most frameworks have
//       a limit)
//     - cap Content-Length on upload endpoints
//     - reject an absurd Content-Encoding: zstd with an enormous
//       window
//   This is a real vulnerability class and it has been used in real
//   attacks against APIs.

// THE MEASUREMENT THAT MATTERS
   compressed and uncompressed bytes, at p50 and p99, split by
   endpoint. A "response time" metric without a size metric cannot tell
   you whether an improvement was real work or a smaller payload.

Connections and pooling

Client pools are different under HTTP/2

A TLS handshake is 1–2 round trips and up to 100ms — often more than the request itself — which is why keep-alive is where most of the win is. Under HTTP/2 the shape flips: multiplexing means you want a small number of connections with high per-connection stream limits, not a large pool like HTTP/1.1. And honour the server's SETTINGS_MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS: a client that asks for more sees REFUSED_STREAM and must retry.

tr := &http.Transport{
	ForceAttemptHTTP2:   true,
	MaxIdleConns:        64,
	MaxConnsPerHost:     8,        // few connections, many streams
	MaxIdleConnsPerHost: 8,
	IdleConnTimeout:     90 * time.Second,
}

Head-of-line blocking, by HTTP version

HTTP/1.1 carries requests on a connection in order — a slow response blocks everything behind it, and a pool of ten only mitigates it. HTTP/2 multiplexes streams so one slow response no longer stalls its neighbours, but a single lost packet still stalls the whole connection. HTTP/3 runs independent QUIC streams over UDP with per-stream loss recovery, which only shows up on lossy networks: mobile, satellite, long-haul wireless. Measure on your users' networks, not your office.

// HTTP/1.1  one request blocks the rest of the connection
// HTTP/2    streams multiplex; a lost packet stalls the connection
// HTTP/3    per-stream loss recovery over UDP
//           (matters on lossy networks: mobile, satellite)

TIME_WAIT and Slowloris

A server that closes connections aggressively exhausts ephemeral ports — the default 5–75s keep-alive is reasonable, a 1s close is not. On the client, many short-lived connections do the same; the fix is a long-lived client with a small pool, not a bigger ephemeral range. And an attacker who opens a connection and never sends a request is Slowloris: mitigate with header timeouts and per-IP connection limits, which is a reason to keep a reverse proxy in front.

# See the damage before you tune it:
ss -s
sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_fin_timeout
sysctl net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range

# nginx: cap headers and per-address connections.
client_header_timeout 10s;
limit_conn addr 20;

// KEEP-ALIVE IS WHERE MOST OF THE WIN IS
   A TLS handshake is 1-2 round trips and up to 100ms. Per request
   that is often more than the request itself.
//   HTTP/1.1 keep-alive, HTTP/2 multiplexing, HTTP/3 QUIC: all
   eliminate or amortise it. All are configured, and all are commonly
   left at a default that is not right for your load.

// CLIENT SIDE
//   HTTP/2 multiplexes many requests over one connection, so a client
//   with maxConnections: 10 is wrong for HTTP/2. Over HTTP/2 you want
//   a small number of connections with high per-connection stream
//   limits.
//   HTTP/2's own constraint: SETTINGS_MAX_CONCURRENT_STREAMS,
//   typically 100 server-side. A client asking for 1000 streams sees
//   REFUSED_STREAM and must retry. Honour the server's limit.
//   CONNECTION WARMING: pre-open connections at startup. A serverless
//   or autoscaled service that opens connections on the first request
//   pays a handshake for the first N requests. Some runtimes warm
//   automatically; check yours.

// SERVER SIDE
   TIME_WAIT accumulation: a server that closes connections
   aggressively exhausts ephemeral ports. Default keep-alive of 5-75
   seconds is reasonable; aggressive 1-second closes cause problems.
   TIME_WAIT on the CLIENT side with many short-lived connections
   exhausts the client's ephemeral ports. The fix is a long-lived
   client with a small pool, not a bigger ephemeral range.
//   Slowloris: an attacker opens a connection and never sends a
//   request. Mitigations: request-header timeouts, connection limits
//   per IP, and a reverse proxy in front. Every reverse proxy does
//   this and it is a reason to have one.

// HEAD OF LINE BLOCKING, and why HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 are different
   HTTP/1.1: a TCP connection carries requests in order. A slow
     response blocks everything behind it on that connection. A pool
     of 10 connections mitigates it; it does not solve it.
//   HTTP/2: streams multiplex over one connection, so no HOL blocking
//     at the stream level. A single slow packet still stalls the
//     connection, but not other requests on it.
//   HTTP/3: QUIC runs streams over UDP with independent loss
//     recovery, so one lost packet does not stall other streams. This
//     is the real reason for HTTP/3 and it only shows up on lossy
//     networks: mobile, satellite, long-haul wireless.
//   Measured on a good wired connection HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 are close.
//   Measured on a phone, HTTP/3 is clearly better. Measure on your
//   actual users' networks, not your office.

// THE CONNECTION FORMULA for a synchronous server
   threads = cores x (1 + wait/compute)
//   8 cores, 10ms work, 40ms waiting on the database: 8 x (1 + 4) = 40
//   But this is for CPU-bound work. If you are waiting on I/O almost
//   all of the time, the useful number is concurrency, not cores, and
//   the limit is your downstream pool, not your CPU.
//   The practical rule: your concurrency should be about
//   throughput x latency. 500 req/s at 50ms latency needs 25 in
//   flight. Size the pool to that, and bound the queue above it.

Database-shaped latency

N+1 is a round-trip problem

Loading twenty orders and then querying lines for each is twenty-one round trips — 21ms at 1ms each, 315ms at the 15ms a warm cache on a busy database can cost. The database does set operations better than you do: one batched IN or a JOIN replaces the loop. If the ORM generates the loop for you, that is the query shape to read.

-- N+1: 20 orders, then 20 line queries. Fix with one batched read.
SELECT * FROM order_lines
WHERE order_id = ANY($1::uuid[]);
-- or a JOIN; never a SELECT per parent row.

The offset deep scan, and keyset pagination

OFFSET 100000 makes the database generate and discard 100,000 rows to answer a page of fifty. Keyset pagination — filter on the last row's sort key instead — is index-friendly and O(log n) regardless of page depth. This is why cursors exist, and it is the page under load that exposes the difference.

-- OFFSET scans deeper every page. Keyset does not:
SELECT id, created_at FROM orders
WHERE tenant_id = $1
  AND (created_at, id) < ($2, $3)      -- the cursor from the last page
ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC
LIMIT 50;

Two settings that save production databases

An idle-in-transaction connection holds locks and a snapshot: set idle_in_transaction_session_timeout everywhere. A long-running statement without a timeout holds locks until it finishes however long that is: set statement_timeout. And never hold a database transaction open across an external API call — a two-second payment call holds the locks and the connection for two seconds.

-- Set these on every server, in every environment.
SET statement_timeout = '5s';
SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '30s';

-- WRONG: the transaction stays open during the external call.
--   BEGIN; INSERT ...; call payment API (2s); UPDATE ...; COMMIT;
-- RIGHT: do the external call first, then the short transaction.

// THE QUERY PATTERNS THAT ACCOUNT FOR MOST SLOW ENDPOINTS

// 1. N+1
   the handler loads 20 orders, then queries lines for each.
//   21 round trips at 1ms each is 21ms, and at 15ms each (a warm cache
//   on a busy database) it is 315ms.
//   FIX: a JOIN, a batched IN, or a DataLoader (ch9-resolvers).
//   20 x WHERE id IN (...) is 1 round trip and the database is better
//   at it than you are.

// 2. THE OFFSET DEEP SCAN
   OFFSET 100000 makes the database generate and discard 100,000 rows.
//   This is why cursors exist (ch3).
//   FIX: keyset pagination. Index-friendly, O(log n).

// 3. A MISSING OR WRONG INDEX
   the single most common cause. Check EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS):
     a Seq Scan on a large table   -> add an index
     rows estimated 10, actual 2M -> stale statistics, ANALYZE
     "Rows Removed by Filter: 2M"  -> the index is not selective
                                         enough for this predicate
     a Bitmap Heap Scan with a high
     recheck count                   -> the index is not covering;
                                         add INCLUDE columns

// 4. A COUNT(*) ON A LARGE SET
   counts the whole thing. Use an estimate (planner statistics) for a
   UI hint, or a maintained counter, or do not return it (ch3).

// 5. THE MISSING COMPOSITE INDEX ORDER
   WHERE tenant_id = ? AND created_at > ? ORDER BY created_at
   needs an index on (tenant_id, created_at). The column order in a
   composite index is the column order in the WHERE clause, not the
   SELECT list, and getting it wrong means a scan.
//   "Equality columns first, then the range, then the sort" is the
//   rule of thumb.

// 6. A TRANSACTION HELD OPEN
   an idle-in-transaction connection holds locks and a snapshot. It is
//   a database-wide problem caused by one endpoint.
//   SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '30s';
//   This one setting has saved more production databases than any
//   other operational change. Set it everywhere.

// 7. A LONG-RUNNING STATEMENT WITHOUT A TIMEOUT
   statement_timeout = '5s'
//   Without it, one pathological query holds locks until it finishes,
//   which can be a very long time.

// 8. THE WRITE AMPLIFICATION OF AN UPDATE
   updating a row rewrites it and every index on it. A "soft delete"
//   flag adds a write to every create. Fine at volume; a problem at
//   a million writes a minute. Batch where you can.

// 9. THE TRANSACTION PATTERN THAT COSTS YOU
   doing an external call inside a database transaction:
//     BEGIN; INSERT ...; call payment API (2 seconds);
//     UPDATE ...; COMMIT;
//   The transaction is open for 2 seconds, holding locks and a
//   connection. Do the call first, then the transaction. Or use the
//   outbox pattern (ch8's transactional outbox).

// THE TWO METRICS TO PUT ON A DASHBOARD
   - queries per second, by endpoint
   - pool wait time, not pool size. Queue time is the p99.

Caching layers, honestly

The five places, considered in order

The HTTP cache is free — the client's own cache and the CDN with zero code. Below it, the process cache is microseconds but per-instance and stale on deploy; the shared cache is sub-millisecond and the workhorse; a materialised view makes an expensive read cheap at the cost of staleness and write amplification; a read replica moves load and adds replication lag and a second failure mode, and is not a cache. Caching exists to absorb read bursts, protect an expensive query and tolerate a downstream outage — not to make a 3ms query faster.

// 1 HTTP cache          client + CDN. zero code. cacheable GETs only
// 2 process cache       in-process LRU. microseconds. per-instance
// 3 shared cache        Redis / Memcached. sub-millisecond. the workhorse
// 4 materialised view   maintained table. cheap reads, write amplif.
// 5 read replica        moves read load, replication lag. not a cache
// a 3ms query cached in Redis for a 0.4ms saving is a net loss

Single-flight is the antidote to the stampede

When a key expires under load, a thousand concurrent requests miss and all hit the database. Request coalescing — one loader per key, everyone else waits — turns that into one query. The cache-read pattern must always go through it, or you have a stampede at every expiry.

var group singleflight.Group

func Read(ctx context.Context, key string) ([]byte, error) {
	if v, ok := local.Get(key); ok {
		return v.([]byte), nil
	}
	v, err, _ := group.Do(key, func() (any, error) {
		data, err := compute(ctx)        // exactly one loader per key
		if err == nil {
			_ = redis.Set(ctx, key, data, 300*time.Second)
		}
		return data, err
	})
	return v.([]byte), err
}

TTL is a product decision, and it needs jitter

TTL equals your acceptable staleness — long enough that the cache is not overhead, short enough that stale data does not become a bug report. A long TTL with explicit invalidation on write beats a short one if you can guarantee the invalidation, which you cannot, so most systems use both. And jitter every TTL: a thousand keys expiring at the same instant is a stampede on a schedule.

// Jitter turns a coordinated expiry into a trickle.
const ttl = 300 + Math.floor(Math.random() * 30);
await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(value), { EX: ttl });

// Invalidation: delete the key AND write the fresh value, or a
// request already in flight writes the stale value back afterwards.

// THE FIVE PLACES, AND THE ORDER TO CONSIDER THEM
   1. HTTP cache        the client's own cache and the CDN (ch-networking
                       material in tech-networking.html). Zero code.
//   2. Process cache     an in-process LRU. Microseconds. Per-instance,
//                       so it does not share and it is stale on deploy.
//   3. Shared cache      Redis or Memcached. Sub-millisecond. The workhorse.
//   4. Materialised view a table maintained by triggers or a job. Makes
//                       an expensive read cheap at the cost of staleness
//                       and write amplification.
//   5. Read replica      moves read load, adds replication lag and a
//                       second failure mode. Not a cache.

// WHAT CACHING IS ACTUALLY FOR
   - absorbing read bursts, which is the strongest argument
   - protecting an expensive query
   - tolerating a downstream outage, with a stale TTL
//   It is NOT for making an already-fast query faster. A 3ms query
//   cached in Redis for a 0.4ms saving is a net loss: you added a
//   network hop, a serialisation cost, an invalidation problem and a
//   staleness window.

// THE INVALIDATION PROBLEMS, RANKED BY HOW OFTEN THEY BITE
   1. THE BUG YOU HAVE NOT FOUND YET. A cache means your test suite
      passes or fails depending on what is cached. Fix: never read
      through a cache in tests, or make it a no-op in the test env.
//   2. DOUBLE INVALIDATION. You delete the key, then the request that
//      was in flight writes the stale value back. Fix: version the
//      key, or write the fresh value at invalidation time, or delete
//      twice with a delay (which is a hack).
//   3. THE CACHE STAMPEDE. A key expires; 1,000 concurrent requests
//      miss; all 1,000 query the database; the database falls over.
//      Fix: request coalescing (one loader, many waiters), or a short
//      probabilistic early expiry, or a lock.
//   4. HOT KEY EVICTION. One key under enormous load gets evicted and
//      re-fetched continuously. Fix: never cache a singleton like a
//      feature-flag set in a small LRU.
//   5. MEMORY PRESSURE. An unbounded cache is an OOM. Always bound it,
//      and prefer eviction policies that understand TTL.
//   6. NO SINGLE SOURCE OF TRUTH. A cached entity that has been
//      updated elsewhere is now permanently wrong until it expires.
//      An event-driven invalidation is the only real fix.

// THE CACHE-READ PATTERN THAT IS ACTUALLY SAFE
  const cached = await redis.get(key);
  if (cached !== null) return JSON.parse(cached);

  // SINGLE FLIGHT: only one loader per key, everyone else waits
  const fresh = await singleflight(key, async () => {
    const data = await compute();
    await redis.set(key, JSON.stringify(data), { EX: ttl });
    return data;
  });
  return fresh;
//   The single-flight wrapper is the important part. Without it you
//   have a stampede every expiry.

// THE TTL, and there is no safe default
   - TTL = your acceptable staleness. That is a product decision.
//   - too long: users see stale data and you get "the app is broken"
//   - too short: you re-compute constantly and the cache is overhead
//   - a long TTL with explicit invalidation on write is better than a
//     short TTL, IF you can guarantee the invalidation. You cannot,
//     which is why most systems use both.
//   - jitter your TTL. Every key with a TTL of exactly 300s expires at
//     the same moment and the stampede is guaranteed:
//       EX 300 + random(0, 30)

Rate limits and queues

Shed with 503 and Retry-After

When the system is overloaded there are three behaviours: shed (reject immediately), queue (accept and wait), or degrade (serve a cheaper answer). Shedding is the most honest — the client retries with backoff and most users never notice — and it is strictly better than queueing a request you are going to reject at the 30-second mark anyway.

// Shed early and tell the client when to come back.
if (pool.waiting > 50) {
  res.status(503)
     .set('Retry-After', '2')
     .json({ error: { code: 'overloaded', retryAfter: 2 } });
  return;
}

A queue is only safe bound, and with a deadline

An unbounded queue converts an outage into an OOM. Bound the depth, put a deadline on every request so one that will arrive too late is not queued at all, and reject — 503 with Retry-After, never a hang — when the queue is full.

q := make(chan job, 100)   // explicit max depth

func submit(j job) error {
	select {
	case q <- j:
		return nil               // queued
	default:
		return errQueueFull      // 503: do not block the caller
	}
}

Priority lanes survive a traffic spike

A single FIFO queue at capacity gives a batch export the same latency as a 40ms read, so payments queue behind reports. Separate lanes — admit payments immediately, queue medium work with a bound, reject low work at capacity — buy back the p99 with no extra capacity. It is a configuration decision, not a capacity increase.

// high    payment, auth        admit immediately
// medium  reads, writes        admit with a bounded queue
// low     reports, exports     reject at capacity
// one FIFO at capacity: exports occupy slots, reads wait behind them
// two lanes: reads never wait for a report. same resources.

// WHEN THE SYSTEM IS OVERLOADED, PICK ONE OF THREE BEHAVIOURS
   1. SHED: reject immediately with 503 and Retry-After. Fastest and
      the most honest. The client retries with backoff and most users
      do not notice.
   2. QUEUE: accept and wait. Better for the user, and it converts an
      overload into a latency collapse and a timeout avalanche. Only
      works with a BOUNDED queue and a deadline.
   3. DEGRADE: serve a cheaper answer. Drop the enrichment, return
      cached data, skip the optional downstream calls.

// QUEUING HAS RULES, AND BREAKING THEM IS AN INCIDENT
   - BOUND THE QUEUE. An unbounded queue converts an outage into an
     OOM. Every serious queue has a max depth.
//   - SET A DEADLINE ON EVERY REQUEST. A request that will arrive too
     late to matter should not be queued at all. Reject it.
//   - REJECT WHEN THE QUEUE IS FULL. 503 with Retry-After, not a hang.
//   - LOAD SHED BY PRIORITY. Not every request is equally important. A
     search can be shed; a payment cannot. Admit-later priorities
     first is the right queue discipline for mixed traffic.
//   - DO NOT QUEUE WHAT YOU CAN REJECT. Queuing a request you are
     going to reject in 30 seconds wastes a worker for 30 seconds.

// PRIORITY QUEUING, which is how you survive a traffic spike
   high priority   payment, authentication     admit immediately
   medium          reads, writes               admit with a queue
   low             reports, exports, search    reject at capacity
//   With a single FIFO queue at capacity, everything gets the same
//   latency, which means your latency-sensitive requests queue
//   behind a batch export. Priority lanes fix that and cost nothing.

// ANIMATED EXAMPLE of the difference
   capacity 100 concurrent, 500 req/s, two workloads:
     450 interactive reads, 50 report generations taking 10s each
   single FIFO: reports occupy 50 slots for 10s. interactive requests
     find 50 slots, wait behind them, p99 goes from 40ms to seconds.
   two lanes: reports have their own 40-slot lane with a queue depth
     of 10. Interactive requests never wait for a report.
//   Same resources, different latency. This is a configuration decision,
//   not a capacity increase.

// BACKPRESSURE, so the overload reaches the caller
   - publish your own saturation: a gauge for queue depth and
     connection pool wait
   - shed on saturation rather than on absolute rate: if pool wait
     exceeds 50ms, reject. That is self-correcting and it protects the
     actual bottleneck, whatever it turns out to be.
//   - rate limit at the edge so the overload arrives shaped rather
     than as a wall (ch6)
//   - and be honest in the response: a 503 with Retry-After is more
     useful to a client than a 200 that never arrives.

Measuring an endpoint properly

Cardinality is the metric explosion that bites

Label with the route template, not the path: /v1/orders/{id}, never /v1/orders/abc123, or every request is a unique series and your backend silently starts dropping data. User ids, emails, raw URLs and exception messages are not labels. A user-id label produces one series per user; an exception-message label is the most common version of this mistake.

// labels:  route (template), method, status, region, tier
// NOT:     user id, order id, email, raw URL, exception message
// each unique label combination is a separate time series;
// a user-id label is one series per user -> limits hit, data lost

Percentiles come from histograms, never averages

Record histograms with sensible bucket boundaries — 5, 10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2500, 5000ms — and compute percentiles from them. A counter's average cannot reproduce a tail; averaged rates are meaningless. Split duration into handler, each database query, each downstream call and serialisation, or a 200ms endpoint stays a mystery.

# Prometheus: buckets plus histogram_quantile.
http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{
  route="/v1/orders/{id}", le="0.150"} 10

histogram_quantile(0.99,
  sum by (le, route) (rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])))

A load test that tells you something

Pick one representative endpoint, not the whole app. Test with production-like data volume — ten rows and ten million rows differ by three orders of magnitude on the same query. Ramp gradually and watch p99, not throughput: the knee where p99 starts rising is your real capacity. Run long enough to hit the slow paths — pool exhaustion, certificate rotation, GC, cache expiry, cron — and test the failure modes: kill a dependency, add latency, saturate the database.

# Ramp gradually, watch p99 (the knee is capacity), run long.
k6 run --vus 10 --duration 2h loadtest.js
# Two hours finds cold-cache cliffs, cron collisions and pool
# exhaustion; ten minutes misses all of them.

// WHAT TO RECORD, PER REQUEST
   route (the TEMPLATE, not the path with ids substituted, or every
          request is a unique metric and your time series explodes)
   method, status
   duration, split into: handler, each database query, each downstream
     call, serialisation
   request id, and the trace id
   response bytes in and out, compressed and uncompressed
   queue wait, if there is a queue
   cache hit or miss, per cache
   pool wait, per pool

// THE SPLIT IS THE POINT
   a 200ms endpoint with no split is a mystery. With splits it is
   "database 150ms, downstream 40ms, serialisation 8ms", and the
   150ms turns out to be one query missing an index.
//   Instrument the database queries as spans. A framework that logs
   "db: 150ms" is not enough; you need to know which query.

// HISTOGRAMS, NOT AVERAGES
   Averages hide tails. Record histograms (or a summary with buckets)
//   and compute percentiles from them. Most metrics backends can do
   this over a histogram; a counter's average cannot.
//   Bucket boundaries matter: 5, 10, 25, 50, 100, 250, 500, 1000, 2500,
//   5000ms. Percentiles computed from averaged rates are meaningless.

// THE SAMPLE RATE
   100% of errors, 100% of p99-relevant slow requests, and a fraction
   of fast ones. Adaptive sampling: keep everything above p95, sample
   the rest. Or head-based sampling, which keeps a fixed fraction and
   better preserves the tail.
//   Never sample the traces for errors. Ever.

// CARDINALITY, the metric explosion that will bite you
   labels: route, status, method, region, tier. NOT: user id, order
   id, email, raw URL, exception message.
//   Each unique label combination is a separate time series. A user
   id label produces one series per user, and you will hit your
   backend's limit and start losing data silently.
//   Exception message as a label is the most common version of this.

// A LOAD TEST THAT TELLS YOU SOMETHING
   1. pick ONE representative endpoint, not the whole app
   2. test with production-like DATA VOLUME. A table with 10 rows and
      a table with 10 million rows differ by three orders of magnitude
      on the same query.
//   3. ramp gradually and WATCH p99, not throughput. The knee is where
//      p99 starts rising, and that is your real capacity.
//   4. run it long enough for the slow paths: connection pools,
//      certificate rotation, cron jobs, GC, cache expiry. Ten minutes
      misses all of them; two hours finds at least one.
//   5. test the failure modes: kill a dependency, add 200ms of
      latency, saturate the database. The behaviour under degradation
      is what you actually need to know.
//   6. run it in an environment resembling production: same instance
      types, same data volume, same network path. A load test on a
      laptop proves nothing about a cluster.

The tail, and why it dominates

One slow request becomes six bad pages

The arithmetic is why per-request metrics understate user experience. One call in a hundred takes three seconds, and a page makes six calls: 1 − 0.996 ≈ 6% of pages have a slow element — six times the request error rate. And a service that calls ten dependencies each at 99.9% is 0.99910 ≈ 99.0% available, which is why every dependency is a reliability decision.

// one call is slow 1% of the time; a page makes 6 calls
//   any call slow    -> 1 - 0.99^6            = 5.9% of pages
//   two calls slow   -> roughly 0.15% of pages (6 seconds)
// ten dependencies at 99.9% each -> 0.999^10 = 99.0% availability

The circuit breaker needs three states and a strategy

Closed counts failures; open fails fast without calling; half-open admits probes on a schedule. The untuned decisions matter more than the states: how many failures open it (count, or a percentage over a window), how long it stays open (exponential backoff beats fixed — it stops hammering a genuinely down dependency), and what counts as a failure. A breaker on errors only misses the slow-but-not-failing dependency, which is the more common case.

var breaker = gobreaker.NewCircuitBreaker(gobreaker.Settings{
	Name:        "pricing",
	MaxRequests: 3,                  // probes while half-open: FEW
	Interval:    10 * time.Second,   // count failures over a window
	Timeout:     30 * time.Second,   // stay open before probing
	ReadyToTrip: func(c gobreaker.Counts) bool {
		// percentage over the window, not just a raw count
		return float64(c.TotalFailures)/float64(c.Requests) > 0.5
	},
})

Mitigations, in order of effectiveness

Short timeouts everywhere come first — a 3s timeout is three seconds of a user waiting, and 500ms with a retry is usually better. Then retries with backoff and jitter on idempotent operations only, circuit breakers, hedged requests for reads with a high-variance tail, parallelising sequential calls, removing the dependency, and bulkheading so one slow dependency cannot exhaust the shared pool.

// sequential:  A 40ms then B 40ms = 80ms
// parallel:    max(A, B) = 40ms           -> usually free
// hedged read: 2nd request at 50ms; take the first answer
//              (+2% load, cuts p99 on a high-variance tail; reads only)
// remove:      does this call need the enrichment at all?
// fast average, useless tail: every user has a bad day
// slow average, good tail:    everyone has a slightly worse day

// THE ARITHMETIC, and it is why the tail is the whole problem
   One request in 100 takes 3 seconds. Your page makes 6 calls.
     per call: 1% at 3s, 99% at 50ms
     page:     1% chance any one call is slow -> 6% of pages have a
               3-second element on them
     and if the 6 calls are sequential, 6% of pages are slow
     and 0.01% of pages have TWO slow calls -> 6 seconds
//   So 1% slow requests produce 6% bad pages, and the user-visible
//   error rate is six times the request error rate. This is why
//   per-request metrics understate user experience, and why you need
//   to measure the page or the operation, not the request.

// THE FAN-OUT AMPLIFICATION
   Service A calls B, C, D. If each has a 1% chance of being slow,
   then A has a ~3% chance of a slow response. Add E, F and you are at
   ~6%. The error rate of a deep call graph is the product of its
   parts' reliability.
//   With ten dependencies each at 99.9%, A's availability is 99%.
//   THIS IS WHY DEPENDENCY COUNT IS A RELIABILITY DECISION. Every
//   dependency is a multiply, and adding one for a convenience lookup
//   costs you a full order of magnitude more than it feels.

// MITIGATIONS, IN ORDER OF EFFECTIVENESS
   1. TIMEOUTS EVERYWHERE, SHORT. A 3-second timeout is 3 seconds of
      a user waiting. 500ms, with a retry, is usually better.
   2. RETRY WITH BACKOFF AND JITTER, on idempotent operations only
      (ch5). A retry that takes 200ms and succeeds beats a user-facing
      failure. A retry that takes 3s makes the tail worse.
//   3. CIRCUIT BREAKERS. After N failures, fail fast for M seconds.
//      This is the single biggest availability improvement available
//      to most systems, and most systems do not have one.
//   4. HEDGED REQUESTS. Send a second request 50ms after the first;
//      take the first to answer. Adds 2% load and cuts p99
//      dramatically on a high-variance tail. Only for reads.
//   5. PARALLELISE. Sequential dependency A then B is 40 + 40 = 80ms.
//      Parallel is 40ms. Usually free, always worth checking.
//   6. REMOVE THE DEPENDENCY. The most reliable optimisation. Does
//      this call need the enrichment at all, or can it be asynchronous
//      and optional?
//   7. BULKHEADING. A separate pool and thread group per dependency,
//      so a slow one cannot exhaust the shared resource. Hystrix's
//      original insight and still correct.

// THE CIRCUIT BREAKER, and it needs three states plus a strategy
   CLOSED      normal. failures counted.
   OPEN        failing fast, immediately, without calling.
   HALF-OPEN   a probe every N seconds. success -> CLOSED.
               failure -> OPEN, with a longer wait.
//   The strategy matters and nobody tunes it:
//     - how many failures to open (count, or a percentage over a
//       window)
//     - how long to stay open (fixed, or exponential backoff, which
//       is better: it stops hammering a dependency that is genuinely
//       down for a while)
//     - what counts as a failure. A 500? Also a timeout? Also a slow
//       response? A breaker on errors only misses a dependency that
//       is slow rather than failing, which is the more common case.
//   And: half-open must allow FEW probes, not many. A burst of 100
//   probes when the breaker closes is a second thundering herd.

// THE COMPREHENSIVE SUMMARY
//   fast average, useless tail:  every user has a bad day
//   slow average, good tail:  everyone has a slightly worse day
//   The second is better. Measure p99, and if you only fix one thing,
//   fix the tail.

Exercises 12

  1. Write a latency budget for five operation classes in your own API, top-down from the user's perceived target. Then measure your actual p99 for each and compute the gap per component.
  2. Add span-level tracing separating handler, database, downstream calls and serialisation. Find one endpoint whose p99 is unexplained and chase it to a cause.
  3. Add a connection pool with a bounded queue that returns 503 with Retry-After when full, and verify p99 stops growing under load instead of timing out.
  4. Implement request coalescing for a cache, then expire a hot key under 500 concurrent requests and verify the database sees one query, not 500.
  5. Add a circuit breaker with exponential backoff to one dependency, then make that dependency return errors. Verify it opens, fails fast, half-opens, and recovers — and that recovery does not stampede.

API Design - REST, GraphQL, gRPC and Webhooks. One-page interactive textbook. Progress is saved in your browser (theme, last chapter). Open in any modern browser. No internet needed.
